BASIC VLSI DESIGN

Course Objectives:

  • Understand the fundamental aspects of circuits in silicon
  • Relate to VLSI design processes and design rules

Course Outcomes:

  • Identify the CMOS layout levels, and the design layers used in the process sequence.
  • Describe the general steps required for processing of CMOS integrated circuits.
  • Design static CMOS combinational and sequential logic at the transistor level.
  • Demonstrate different logic styles such as complementary CMOS logic, pass-transistor Logic, dynamic logic, etc.
  • Interpret the need for testability and testing methods in VLSI.
UNIT I: Moore’s law, speed power performance, nMOS fabrication, CMOS fabrication: n-well, pwell processes, BiCMOS, Comparison of bipolar and CMOS. Basic Electrical Properties of MOS And BiCMOS Circuits: Drain to source current versus voltage characteristics, threshold voltage, trans conductance.
UNIT II: Basic Electrical Properties of MOS And BiCMOS Circuits: nMOS inverter, Determination of pull up to pull down ratio: nMOS inverter driven through one or more pass transistors, alternative forms of pull up, CMOS inverter, BiCMOS inverters, latch up. Basic Circuit Concepts: Sheet resistance, area capacitance calculation, Delay unit, inverter delay, estimation of CMOS inverter delay, super buffers, BiCMOS drivers.
UNIT III: MOS and BiCMOS Circuit Design Processes: MOS layers, stick diagrams, nMOS design style, CMOS design style Design rules and layout & Scaling of MOS Circuits: λ - based design rules, scaling factors for device parameters

UNIT V: Subsystem Design and Layout-2: Clocked sequential circuits, dynamic shift registers, bus lines, General considerations, 4-bit arithmetic processes, 4-bit shifter, RegularityDefinition & Computation Practical aspects and testability: Some thoughts of performance, optimization and CAD tools for design and simulation.

UNIT-I: Introduction to VLSI Technology

1.1 The Integrated Circuit & Why It Won

The invention of the transistor (Shockley, Brattain, Bardeen at Bell Labs) and the subsequent development of the Integrated Circuit by Jack Kilby (1960, Texas Instruments) revolutionized electronics. Over past decades, Silicon CMOS emerged as dominant fabrication process due to packing density, low static power, and batch economic scalability.

The Exam Line

"An IC is an ensemble of active and passive devices formed on and within a single-crystal semiconductor substrate and interconnected by a metallization pattern." — Sze & Lee (p. 505)

Lucid Intuition · Why Discrete Devices Lost

Discrete circuits join packaged devices with physical wires or PCB traces. ICs replace external wiring with monolithic on-chip thin-film conductors. Three decisive advantages won the market:

  • Parasitic Reduction: Multi-level metallization slashes wiring lengths from centimeters to micrometers, minimizing parasitic inductance and capacitance for high-speed operation.
  • Real Estate Utilization: Millions to billions of active devices pack into a compact silicon chip die instead of bulky boards.
  • Batch Economy: All devices on every chip across the entire wafer are manufactured in unison through repeated planar chemical steps. Wire bonding is eliminated between internal gates.
Figure 1.2: Speed-power performance trade-off — Propagation delay per gate vs. power dissipation per gate for CMOS, nMOS, BiCMOS, ECL, and GaAs. CMOS delivers extraordinarily low static power dissipation at competitive switching delays.

Where Discrete Devices Still Survive

Microwave, photonics, and ultra-high-power applications still demand discrete components: IMPATT diodes as microwave generators, injection laser diodes as optical emitters, and thyristors as high-voltage industrial switches.

Fig. 1: Monolithic planar fabrication cycle — Film formation, lithography, etching, and impurity doping.
Fig. 2: Scale comparison — 100–300 mm semiconductor wafer → 1–20 mm chip die (10¹ to 10⁹ components) → 1–10 µm individual MOSFET/BJT.
Prepared Note · Limitations of ICs

An IC cannot do everything a discrete circuit can. Six primary bottlenecks:

  1. Power and Heat: Total thermal dissipation is strictly capped by small die volume and package heat extraction capability.
  2. Unfriendly Integration Components: Inductors and large capacitors devour vast silicon area. Monolithic magnetic transformers cannot be fabricated.
  3. Poor Absolute Tolerance: On-chip resistors and capacitors match each other well ratio-wise, but absolute values vary by ±20% or worse across process corners.
  4. Voltage Ceilings: Thin gate oxides and shallow p-n junctions break down at modest voltages.
  5. Zero Post-Fab Repair: A single dead internal node scraps the whole chip; repair is impossible.
  6. High Fixed NRE Cost: Photomask sets and engineering design iterations run into millions of dollars; economical exclusively in high manufacturing volumes.

1.2 Levels of Abstraction & The Y-Chart

The Exam Line

"The Y-chart shows a design in three domains — behavioral, structural and physical — with the level of abstraction falling as one moves toward the centre."

Figure 1.48: Gajski-Kuhn Y Diagram — Behavioral, Structural, and Geometrical/Physical axes converging inward toward physical silicon masks.
Lucid Intuition · The 3 Design Domains
  • Behavioral Domain (What it does): Mathematical transfer algorithms → Finite State Machines (FSMs) → Boolean state equations → Differential equations.
  • Structural Domain (How it connects): Processors → Register-ALU data paths → Logic leaf cells (NAND/NOR) → Transistors.
  • Geometrical / Physical Domain (Where it sits): Chip floorplan → Module placement → Standard cell placement → Photomask layout geometry.

The design process spirals iteratively inward across these axes: a behavioral specification maps to a structural block, which maps to floorplan coordinates, then repeats at finer granularity.

Five Standard Levels of Digital VLSI Abstraction

Abstraction Level Core Role Real-World Example (x86 / Adder)
1. Architecture Defines functional external contract, instruction set (ISA), register set, and memory model. x86 Instruction Set Architecture specification.
2. Microarchitecture Partitions architecture into execution pipelines, registers, caches, and functional execution units. 80386, Pentium, Core, Atom, AMD Athlon, Phenom (tradeoffs in transistor count, power, frequency).
3. Logic Constructs functional blocks using gates, boolean expressions, and state elements. 32-bit Integer Adder designed via Ripple Carry, Carry Lookahead, or Carry Select.
4. Circuit Implements logic gates with specific transistor configurations tailored for power or speed. Carry lookahead adder built with Static CMOS, Domino logic, or Pass Transistors.
5. Physical Geometric layout, silicon layers, routing tracks, and pad placement. Polysilicon, diffusion, contact cuts, and metallization masks.
Lucid Intuition · "Tall & Skinny" vs "Short & Fat" Engineers

Short & Fat: Extremely deep expertise in a narrow layer (e.g., pure circuit device physics).

Tall & Skinny: Broad understanding spanning multiple abstraction layers. Digital VLSI strongly favors the tall, skinny engineer because decisions in one domain radically impact others (e.g., changing a cache circuit from 2 clock cycles to 1 cycle shifts the entire optimal microarchitecture).

1.3 The VLSI Design Flow

The Exam Line

"The design flow starts from the algorithm in the behavioral domain, is mapped to architecture and floorplan, and ends in mask layout; top-down and bottom-up approaches must be combined."

Lucid Intuition · The Dual Flow

Top-Down: High-level system requirements decompose into sub-blocks (Specification → Architecture → Logic → Circuits). Ensures architectural intent is maintained.

Bottom-Up: Physical limitations dictate feasibility (Transistor sizing → Standard cell libraries → Macro placement → Parasitic extraction). Feeds real delay, power, and noise numbers back to higher levels.

Design Trade-offs in Modern Flows

  • Clock Frequency vs Pipeline Depth: Deeper pipelines allow higher clock rates but consume greater dynamic power and incur heavy stall penalties on branch mispredictions.
  • Custom Layout vs Automated CAD: Hand-crafted full-custom layout yields dense, power-efficient silicon at massive engineering cost; automated synthesis/place-and-route trades some silicon efficiency for rapid time-to-market.

1.6.2 Structured Design & Quick Recap

Managing chips with billions of transistors requires structured design principles:

1. Hierarchy

Tree structure dividing chip into Cores → Units → Functional Blocks → Leaf Cells → Transistors. Simplifies verification through black-box interfaces.

2. Regularity

Minimize distinct block types. Once designed and verified, reuse identical cells across datapath and memory arrays.

3. Modularity

Enforce strict, well-defined I/O interfaces so changes within one block do not trigger unintended cross-talk or timing violations.

4. Locality

Confine interactions both physically and temporally. Keep communicating cells adjacent to avoid long high-capacitance wiring.

Integration Scale Milestones

Generation Component Count per Chip Historical Era
SSI (Small Scale Integration)≤ 100 componentsEarly 1960s (Basic logic gates)
MSI (Medium Scale Integration)≤ 1,000 componentsLate 1960s (Adders, counters, muxes)
LSI (Large Scale Integration)≤ 100,000 components1970s (Early 8-bit microprocessors)
VLSI (Very Large Scale Integration)≤ 10,000,000 components1980s–1990s (Complex 32-bit CPUs)
ULSI (Ultra Large Scale Integration)> 10,000,000 componentsModern multi-core processors, SoCs
Quick Recap · Core Exam Anchors
  • IC: Active + passive devices on single crystal, joined by metallization.
  • Cost Engine: Every device on every die made simultaneously via planar processing.
  • Y-Chart: 3 domains (Behavioral, Structural, Physical), abstraction shrinks inward.
  • Flow Sequence: Algorithm → Architecture → Logic → Circuit → Layout → Fabrication.

CMOS Logic: From One Switch to Every Gate

One pMOS and one nMOS make an inverter. Put them in series and parallel and you get NAND, NOR and compound gates. Add transmission gates and feedback and you get multiplexers, latches and flip-flops. Weste & Harris build all of this in nine pages; read them once slowly.

1.4.1 The MOS Transistor Stack

Every MOS transistor is built as a vertical three-layer sandwich: a conducting Gate, an insulating dielectric layer of Silicon Dioxide (\(\text{SiO}_2\), glass, typically only a few hundred molecules thick), and the single-crystal silicon wafer (known as the Substrate, Body, or Bulk).

Lucid History · Gate Material Evolution
  • 1960s (Metal Gates): Original transistors used aluminum gates, coining the term Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (MOS).
  • 1970s–2000s (Polysilicon Gates): Polycrystalline silicon replaced metal because it withstands high-temperature annealing and self-aligns source/drain diffusions.
  • 2007–Present (High-k Metal Gate / HKMG): At sub-45nm nodes, gate oxide thinned to ~1 nm (a few silicon atoms), causing extreme quantum tunneling leakage. Intel and foundries reintroduced Metal Gates with Hafnium-based High-k dielectrics to suppress leakage while preserving high gate capacitance.

1.4.2 Inversion Physics & Carrier Conduction

The gate acts as an electrostatic control valve, establishing an electric field that dictates carrier flow between Source and Drain (which are physically symmetrical and interchangeable):

nMOS Transistor

Built in a p-type body with n-type source/drain pockets. Body is tied to GND (0 V), reverse-biasing the p-n junctions.

  • Gate = 0 V (GND): Junctions remain reverse-biased; no channel exists → OFF.
  • Gate = \(V_{DD}\): Positive gate field repels holes and attracts minority electrons to the \(\text{Si–SiO}_2\) interface. When electrons outnumber holes, the surface inverts to n-type → ON.

pMOS Transistor

Built in an n-type body (or n-well) with p-type source/drain pockets. Body is tied to \(V_{DD}\).

  • Gate = \(V_{DD}\): Junctions reverse-biased; no channel forms → OFF.
  • Gate = 0 V (GND): Low potential attracts positive holes to the underside of the oxide, inverting the channel to p-type → ON.
  • Bubble Notation: The gate terminal features an inversion bubble denoting active-low behavior.

Supply Rail Voltage Scaling

Logic 1 is designated as \(V_{DD}\) (Power), and Logic 0 as GND / \(V_{SS}\) (0 V). Historically standardized at 5.0 V in the 1970s and 1980s, supply voltages have scaled down to 3.3 V, 2.5 V, 1.8 V, 1.5 V, 1.2 V, 1.0 V, and 0.8 V in modern sub-micron processes to prevent oxide breakdown and curb dynamic switching power (\(P \propto C V_{DD}^2 f\)).

1.4.3 The Switch-Level Model

In digital abstraction, we view transistors as simple voltage-controlled electronic switches:

FIGURE 1.10: Transistor symbols and switch-level models. nMOS is normally-open (closes on g=1); pMOS has an active-low bubble (closes on g=0).
Transistor Type Substrate / Well Source / Drain Gate = 0 (GND) Gate = 1 (\(V_{DD}\)) Carriers
nMOS p-type substrate (tied to GND) n+ diffusion pockets OFF (Open switch) ON (Closed switch) Electrons (Higher mobility \(\mu_n\))
pMOS n-well / n-substrate (tied to \(V_{DD}\)) p+ diffusion pockets ON (Closed switch) OFF (Open switch) Holes (Lower mobility \(\mu_p\))

1.4.3 Static CMOS Inverter

The Exam Line

"A static CMOS gate has an nMOS pull-down network between output and GND and a complementary pMOS pull-up network between output and VDD; exactly one of them conducts for every input." — Weste & Harris §1.4.3, pp. 9–11

FIGURE 1.11: Complementary CMOS Inverter: (a) Transistor schematic with pMOS pull-up and nMOS pull-down; (b) Gate logic symbol \(Y = \overline{A}\).

Circuit Operation & Conduction States

  • Input A = 0 (GND): The nMOS gate is at 0 V → nMOS is OFF. The pMOS gate is low with respect to its source (\(V_{DD}\)) → pMOS is ON. The pMOS connects output \(Y\) directly to \(V_{DD}\). Output is pulled to Logic 1.
  • Input A = 1 (\(V_{DD}\)): The pMOS gate is at \(V_{DD}\) → pMOS is OFF. The nMOS gate is high → nMOS is ON. The nMOS connects output \(Y\) directly to GND. Output is pulled to Logic 0.

Table 1.1: Inverter Conduction Truth Table

Input A pMOS Pull-Up nMOS Pull-Down Output Y
0 ON (Conducting to \(V_{DD}\)) OFF (Open circuit) 1 (\(V_{DD}\))
1 OFF (Open circuit) ON (Conducting to GND) 0 (GND)
Table 1.1: Inverter conduction states (Weste & Harris).
Lucid Intuition · The Zero Static Power Miracle

In classical nMOS or bipolar gates, current continually leaked from power to ground whenever the gate output was in a low state. In static CMOS, for every valid steady-state input (0 or 1), one network is strictly non-conducting. There is never a direct resistive path from \(V_{DD}\) to GND. Static power dissipation is virtually zero (limited only to picoamp leakage), enabling chips to pack billions of transistors without instantly incinerating.

1.4.3 General Static CMOS Gate Architecture

In general, every static CMOS logic gate is constructed by joining an nMOS pull-down network (PDN) to a complementary pMOS pull-up network (PUN):

FIGURE 1.14: General CMOS logic gate architecture: pMOS pull-up network (PUN) connects output to \(V_{DD}\) (1); nMOS pull-down network (PDN) connects output to GND (0).

Both networks attempt to exert a logic level at the output node. For any combination of inputs, the gate can reside in one of four operating states:

Table 1.3: Output States of CMOS Logic Gates

Pull-Up Network: OFF Pull-Up Network: ON
Pull-Down Network: OFF Z (High-Z)
Floating output node 		1 (Logic High)
Connected to \(V_{DD}\)
Pull-Down Network: ON 0 (Logic Low)
Connected to GND 		X (Crowbarred)
Contention / Short-circuit
Table 1.3: Output states of CMOS gates under PUN/PDN conduction (Weste & Harris).

State Z · High Impedance / Floating

Occurs when both PUN and PDN are OFF. The output wire is electrically disconnected from both supply rails and floats, holding its voltage solely on parasitic capacitance.

Applications: Crucial for multiplexers, shared tristate buses (allowing multiple drivers to share one wire by enabling only one at a time), and dynamic memory/latches.

State X · Crowbarred / Contention

Occurs when both PUN and PDN are simultaneously ON. A direct, low-resistance short-circuit path forms between \(V_{DD}\) and GND, causing a massive surge of "crowbar" short-circuit current.

Hazards: Produces an indeterminate output voltage (typically near \(V_{DD}/2\)), causes massive static power dissipation, and risks thermal destruction of thin oxide gates. In static CMOS logic, complementary design strictly guarantees State X is prevented.

1.4.4 Series and Parallel Transistor Networks

Combinational CMOS gates are constructed by connecting transistors in series and parallel. The conduction rules differ fundamentally between nMOS and pMOS:

FIGURE 1.15: Conduction behavior of series and parallel transistor networks: (a) Series nMOS; (b) Series pMOS; (c) Parallel nMOS; (d) Parallel pMOS.
Lucid Intuition · Switch Network Conduction Laws
  • Series nMOS (AND Logic): Both \(g_1 = 1\) AND \(g_2 = 1\) required to establish a path. Conducts only for input pattern 11.
  • Parallel nMOS (OR Logic): Either \(g_1 = 1\) OR \(g_2 = 1\) completes a path. Conducts for 01, 10, and 11.
  • Series pMOS (NOR / Negative-AND Logic): Both \(g_1 = 0\) AND \(g_2 = 0\) required to turn ON. Conducts only for pattern 00.
  • Parallel pMOS (NAND / Negative-OR Logic): Either \(g_1 = 0\) OR \(g_2 = 0\) completes a path. Conducts for 00, 01, and 10.

2-Input CMOS NAND Gate

FIGURE 1.12: Two-input CMOS NAND gate: (a) Transistor schematic showing 2 parallel pMOS pull-ups and 2 series nMOS pull-downs; (b) Gate logic symbol \(Y = \overline{A \cdot B}\).

Table 1.2: 2-Input NAND Gate Conduction Truth Table

Input A Input B Pull-Down Network (nMOS Series) Pull-Up Network (pMOS Parallel) Output Y
0 0 OFF (Both nMOS OFF → open to GND) ON (Both pMOS ON → dual path to \(V_{DD}\)) 1
0 1 OFF (nMOS A is OFF → series path broken) ON (pMOS A is ON → connects \(V_{DD}\)) 1
1 0 OFF (nMOS B is OFF → series path broken) ON (pMOS B is ON → connects \(V_{DD}\)) 1
1 1 ON (Both nMOS ON → conducting to GND) OFF (Both pMOS OFF → isolated from \(V_{DD}\)) 0
Table 1.2: 2-input NAND conduction states (Weste & Harris).

3-Input CMOS NAND Gate

Extending NAND to 3 inputs requires adding a third transistor to both networks: 3 nMOS in series in the pull-down network, and 3 pMOS in parallel in the pull-up network.

FIGURE 1.13: 3-input CMOS NAND gate schematic: (a) Transistor schematic showing 3 parallel pMOS pull-ups and 3 series nMOS pull-downs; Boolean equation: \(Y = \overline{A \cdot B \cdot C}\).
Lucid Intuition · 3-Input NAND Operation

Output \(Y\) pulls down to 0 (GND) if and only if all three inputs are high (\(A=1, B=1, C=1\)). If any single input drops to 0, that transistor breaks the series pull-down path while its complementary parallel pMOS immediately connects the output to \(V_{DD}\) (1).

1.4.5 The CMOS NOR Gate

A 2-input NOR gate performs the complementary dual function of the NAND gate: nMOS transistors are in parallel to pull the output low when either input is high, and pMOS transistors are in series to pull the output high only when both inputs are low.

FIGURE 1.16: 2-input CMOS NOR gate: (a) Transistor schematic with 2 series pMOS pull-ups and 2 parallel nMOS pull-downs; (b) Dual logic symbols: Standard NOR and DeMorgan equivalent bubble-AND \(Y = \overline{A + B} = \overline{A} \cdot \overline{B}\).

Table 1.4: 2-Input NOR Conduction Truth Table

A B Pull-Down Network (nMOS Parallel) Pull-Up Network (pMOS Series) Output Y
0 0 OFF (Both nMOS OFF) ON (Both pMOS ON → path to \(V_{DD}\)) 1
0 1 ON (nMOS B pulls to GND) OFF (pMOS B is OFF → path broken) 0
1 0 ON (nMOS A pulls to GND) OFF (pMOS A is OFF → path broken) 0
1 1 ON (Both nMOS conduct to GND) OFF (Both pMOS OFF → path broken) 0

3-Input CMOS NOR Gate

A 3-input NOR gate incorporates 3 series pMOS transistors in the pull-up network and 3 parallel nMOS transistors in the pull-down network.

FIGURE 1.17: 3-input CMOS NOR gate schematic: 3 series pMOS in pull-up and 3 parallel nMOS in pull-down. Boolean equation: \(Y = \overline{A + B + C}\).
Core Exam Concept · Why NAND Beats NOR in Silicon

In digital VLSI standard cell libraries, NAND gates vastly outnumber NOR gates. Why?

  • Mobility Difference: Electron surface mobility is roughly 2 to 3 times higher than hole mobility (\(\mu_n \approx 2.5 \mu_p\)). Therefore, nMOS transistors have much lower on-resistance per unit width than pMOS transistors.
  • The Series Stack Penalty: In NAND, the series stack consists of fast nMOS transistors. In NOR, the series stack consists of slow pMOS transistors.
  • Sizing Explosion: Connecting two pMOS transistors in series doubles effective pull-up resistance. To match standard rise times, series pMOS transistors must each be widened to \(2 \times 2.5 = 5\times\) the width of a minimum nMOS!
  • Capacitive Loading: Huge pMOS gates present massive input gate capacitance (\(C_{in} \propto W\)), slowing down previous driving stages and devouring valuable silicon area.

Weste & Harris 9-Page Synthesis Roadmap

  • Inverter (NOT): 1 pMOS + 1 nMOS.
  • NAND Gate: Parallel pMOS (PUN) + Series nMOS (PDN).
  • NOR Gate: Series pMOS (PUN) + Parallel nMOS (PDN).
  • Compound Gates (AOI / OAI): e.g., \(Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + C}\) formed systematically using graph duals.
  • Transmission Gates & Latches: Parallel complementary switches + back-to-back inverter feedback create registers and flip-flops.

1.4.5 CMOS Compound Gates & AOI22

A compound gate performs a complex Boolean function within a single inverting stage of logic by combining series and parallel switch structures. This drastically reduces transistor count, silicon area, and parasitic delay compared to cascading discrete gates.

The Conduction Complements Principle

"Transistors that appear in series in the pull-down network must appear in parallel in the pull-up network; transistors that appear in parallel in the pull-down network must appear in series in the pull-up network."

Derivation of AOI22: \( Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D)} \)

The AOI22 (AND-OR-INVERT-22) gate executes the NOR of two 2-input AND terms. The derivation proceeds systematically:

1. Pull-Down Network (PDN)

Take the uninverted Boolean expression \( (A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D) \) defining when output \(Y\) pulls down to 0 (GND):

  • AND term \(A \cdot B\) → Series nMOS (A and B).
  • AND term \(C \cdot D\) → Series nMOS (C and D).
  • OR operator → Connect the two series branches in parallel.

2. Pull-Up Network (PUN)

Apply DeMorgan's dual (conduction complements) using inverted-polarity pMOS switches:

  • Series in PDN → Parallel in PUN: A parallel with B; C parallel with D.
  • Parallel in PDN → Series in PUN: Connect pair (A || B) in series with pair (C || D).
FIGURE 1.18: Step-by-step derivation of the AOI22 compound gate \( Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D)} \): (a)-(b) Pull-down network; (c)-(d) Pull-up network; (e) Full 8-transistor schematic; (f) Standard logic symbol.
Lucid Application · AOI22 as a 2-Input Inverting Multiplexer

An AOI22 gate functions as a high-speed 2-to-1 inverting multiplexer simply by wiring input \(C = \overline{A}\) (where \(A\) acts as the select signal \(S\)):

Y = \(\overline{(S \cdot B) + (\overline{S} \cdot D)}\) → If \(S = 0 \Rightarrow Y = \overline{D}\); If \(S = 1 \Rightarrow Y = \overline{B}\).

This delivers a single-stage, fully-restored inverting multiplexer in just 8 transistors.

Transistor Efficiency: 8T Compound Gate vs 20T Discrete Gates

Building the same function \( Y = \overline{(A \cdot B) + (C \cdot D)} \) using discrete library gates requires two AND gates, one OR gate, and an inverting stage:

FIGURE 1.24: Inefficient discrete implementation of AOI22 using two 2-input ANDs, one OR, and an Inverter — consuming 20 transistors across 4 propagation delay stages.
Design Metric Compound CMOS Gate (Fig 1.18) Discrete Gate Chain (Fig 1.24) Engineering Advantage
Transistor Count 8 Transistors (4 nMOS, 4 pMOS) 20 Transistors (10 nMOS, 10 pMOS) 60% Area Reduction
Logic Delay Stages 1 Inverting Stage 4 Cascaded Stages (NAND→Inv→NOR→Inv) Drastically Faster Propagation
Internal Switching Nodes 0 intermediate nodes 3 internal wires switching Much Lower Dynamic Power (\(P \propto C V^2 f\))
Routing & Wire Parasitics Compact monolithic cell layout Heavy interconnect wiring between 4 gates Minimal Parasitic Capacitance

1.4.6 Pass Transistors & Signal Strength

The strength of an electrical signal is measured by how closely it approximates an ideal voltage source capable of sourcing or sinking current. The power supply rails (\(V_{DD}\) and GND) provide the strongest 1s and 0s.

FIGURE 1.20: Pass transistor signal transfer: nMOS passes a strong 0 and weak 1; pMOS passes a strong 1 and weak 0.

nMOS Pass Transistor

Passes a Strong 0, but a Degraded / Weak 1.

When pulling up, the source rises. As \(V_s\) approaches \(V_{DD} - V_{tn}\), gate-to-source voltage drops to \(V_{gs} = V_t\), abruptly choking off channel conduction. Output never exceeds \(V_{DD} - V_{tn}\).

pMOS Pass Transistor

Passes a Strong 1, but a Degraded / Weak 0.

When pulling down, as \(V_s\) drops toward \(|V_{tp}|\), gate-to-source potential shrinks to \(|V_{tp}|\), cutting off conduction. Output cannot pull below \(|V_{tp}|\).

Device Type Control Gate Input Voltage Output Voltage Signal Quality
nMOS \(g = 1\) (\(V_{DD}\)) 0 V (GND) 0 V (GND) Strong 0
nMOS \(g = 1\) (\(V_{DD}\)) \(V_{DD}\) \(V_{DD} - V_{tn}\) Degraded / Weak 1
pMOS \(g = 0\) (GND) 0 V (GND) \(|V_{tp}|\) Degraded / Weak 0
pMOS \(g = 0\) (GND) \(V_{DD}\) \(V_{DD}\) Strong 1

The CMOS Transmission Gate (Pass Gate)

By connecting an nMOS and a pMOS transistor in parallel, the weaknesses of each device cancel out: the nMOS efficiently passes 0s while the pMOS efficiently passes 1s, achieving an ideal switch with full rail-to-rail swing.

FIGURE 1.21: CMOS Transmission Gate: (a) Transistor schematic; (b) Switch model; (c) Conduction of strong 0 and strong 1; (d) Standard circuit symbols with double-rail control \(g\) and \(gb\).
Lucid Intuition · Double-Rail Control Logic

Because nMOS activates with a high gate voltage while pMOS activates with a low gate voltage, a transmission gate requires both the control input \(g\) and its complement \(gb = \overline{g}\). This dual-polarity driving requirement is termed double-rail logic.

Fatal Design Trap · The Bad Noninverting Buffer
FIGURE 1.22: The "Bad Buffer Trap" — NEVER invert supply connections.

Engineers are sometimes tempted to create a noninverting buffer by flipping transistors upside down (connecting nMOS to \(V_{DD}\) and pMOS to GND). This circuit is fundamentally flawed:

  • When pulling high, the nMOS produces a degraded 1 (\(V_{DD} - V_{tn}\)).
  • When pulling low, the pMOS produces a degraded 0 (\(|V_{tp}|\)).
  • Output swings only between \(|V_{tp}|\) and \(V_{DD} - V_{tn}\), completely obliterating noise margins and forcing downstream logic gates into heavy static short-circuit conduction.
The Golden Rule · Fully Restored Logic

In standard static CMOS gates (Fig 1.14), nMOS transistors only connect to GND and pMOS transistors only connect to \(V_{DD}\). Therefore, static CMOS gates are fully restored logic gates: logic levels are continuously refreshed to the true power rails without degradation.

How to Build Noninverting Functions: Noninverting functions (such as AND, OR, and Buffers) must ALWAYS be constructed by cascading multiple inverting stages (e.g., AND = NAND + Inverter).

FIGURE 1.23: Alternative two-level static CMOS implementations of a 4-input AND gate: (1) 4-input NAND + Inverter; (2) Inverted inputs + 4-input NOR; (3) Two 2-input NANDs + 2-input NOR. Each yields distinct area, speed, and capacitive trade-offs.

Tristate Logic & Multiplexer Architectures

From high-impedance isolation to restoring multiplexer synthesis. Weste & Harris §1.4.7–1.4.8.

1.4.7 Tristates: Controlled Output Isolation

A tristate buffer functions as an electronically controlled switch. Imagine EN is the master ON/OFF button:

  • EN = 1 (ON): The buffer is active; output faithfully copies the input: \(Y = A\).
  • EN = 0 (OFF): The output is electrically disconnected from both rails, entering the high-impedance floating state: \(Y = Z\).
CRUCIAL EXAM RULE · What State 'Z' Really Means

"State 'Z' does NOT mean 0! It means disconnected."

It represents an electrically isolated, high-impedance state where the output wire floats freely and only holds residual charge on parasitic capacitance.

FIGURE 1.25 & 1.26: Tristate buffer symbols (active-high EN, dual-rail EN/\(\overline{\text{EN}}\)) alongside the 2-transistor nonrestoring Transmission Gate equivalent.

Table 1.5: Truth Table for Tristate Buffer

EN \(\overline{\text{EN}}\) Input A Output Y Operating State
0 1 0 Z Disconnected (High-Z)
0 1 1 Z Disconnected (High-Z)
1 0 0 0 Enabled (Strong GND)
1 0 1 1 Enabled (Strong \(V_{DD}\))

Transmission Gate = An Electronic Switch 🔌

Imagine a normal physical switch connecting two wires, A and Y:

  • EN = 1: Switch ON. Signal can pass between A and Y.
  • EN = 0: Switch OFF. A and Y are completely disconnected.
EN \(\overline{\text{EN}}\) Physical Switch State
0 1 OFF (Open Circuit)
1 0 ON (Conducting Channel)
Core Difference · Mechanism of Connection

A tri-state buffer actively copies the input logic level to regenerate a new output.
A transmission gate physically makes or breaks a direct conductive path between the two wires.

🚪 Think of a Transmission Gate as a Door Between Two Rooms

• Door open: Signal travels freely from room A to room Y.
• Door closed: Signal cannot travel from A to Y.
• Crucial Limit: The door does not amplify, clean, or repair the person walking through it.

✨ Buffer = The Signal Cleaner

A buffer receives an incoming signal, looks at it, and restores a weak or noisy logic signal to a fresh, clean full-rail 0 or 1.

One-line memory trick: Transmission gate = door; buffer = signal cleaner.

Why It Matters · Non-Restoring Circuits Degrade Fast

A transmission gate passes the signal but does not clean it up. If the input voltage is weak or noisy, the output remains weak or noisy too. Furthermore, cascading multiple transmission gates causes the delay to skyrocket quadratically (\(t_{pd} \propto N^2\)) due to distributed \(RC\) resistance.

Remember: Buffer = restores signal. Transmission gate = passes signal.

Look at What Happens to the Electricity ⚡

Let's forget textbook jargon for a second and trace the real electrical charges:

1. Transmission Gate: Why Doesn't It Restore the Signal?

Suppose input \(A\) carries a degraded voltage of \(0.7\text{ V}\).

When the transmission gate turns ON, its parallel nMOS and pMOS transistors create a physical conducting channel between node A and node Y.

What happens next? Charge simply flows through that channel, and output Y settles to approximately the same voltage as A (\(Y \approx 0.7\text{ V}\)), depending on the load and channel resistance.

"The transistor doesn't ask, 'Is this supposed to be a logic 1? Should I push it to 1 V?' It simply provides a passive path for charge to flow."

That's the crucial part: there is no active mechanism inside the transmission gate that corrects Y toward a valid digital logic level.

2. Buffer: Where Does the Restoration Come From?

Now imagine feeding that exact same \(0.7\text{ V}\) voltage into a CMOS inverter stage. Its input voltage controls two separate gates:

  • The pMOS provides a path from the positive supply rail (\(V_{DD}\)) to the output.
  • The nMOS provides a path from the output to ground (GND).

The input voltage determines which transistor conducts strongly. If the input is recognized as HIGH, the nMOS turns ON and pulls the output hard towards GND. If recognized as LOW, the pMOS pulls it hard towards \(V_{DD}\).

Where does the clean output voltage come from? From the power supply, NOT from magically strengthening the incoming signal!

The input controls the process; the power supply provides the energy. (One inverter reverses polarity; two stages form a non-inverting restoring buffer.)

The Fundamental Distinction
Feature Transmission Gate Active Buffer
Electrical Role Connects two nodes. Output voltage passively follows input through a conductive path. Controls a brand-new output. Input gate controls switches that actively pull from rails.
Voltage Correction Does NOT correct voltage to a clean logic level. Actively regenerates full rail voltages (\(0\text{ V}\) and \(V_{DD}\)).
The Question to Ask "Does this circuit merely let the input voltage reach the output, or does it use the input to control a separate pull-up/pull-down stage that creates a new output voltage?"
Common Trap Clarification · It is NOT Feedback!

Restoration is NOT a feedback mechanism! It is pure transistor switching and power rail replenishment:

  • pMOS ON: Output load capacitance at \(Y\) charges towards \(V_{DD}\).
  • nMOS ON: Output load capacitance at \(Y\) discharges towards ground.
  • Buffer: Uses this transistor action and the power supply to regenerate a crisp, rail-to-rail digital signal.
Step-by-Step Electrical Walkthrough · Weak HIGH (0.7 V) in 1.0 V System

Suppose the input carries a weak logic HIGH of \(0.7\text{ V}\) in a \(1.0\text{ V}\) system:

  1. The buffer's input stage recognises \(0.7\text{ V}\) as above threshold (valid HIGH).
  2. The nMOS turns ON, rapidly discharging the output node capacitance to GND, while the pMOS is OFF.
  3. The first inverter output snaps to a clean LOW, close to \(0.0\text{ V}\). The second inverter stage inverts this back to a pure, sharp \(1.0\text{ V}\) (\(V_{DD}\)).

Boundary Condition: A buffer can clean up a valid but imperfect signal, but cannot fix voltages that fall outside noise margins into intermediate forbidden zones.

⚽ The Football Support Player Analogy

A buffer is like a support player on a football pitch:

  • The input gives the instruction / direction.
  • The transistors execute the play.
  • The power supply provides the physical athletic energy to launch a strong kick.

Transmission gate: passes the ball along as-is.
Buffer: receives the pass, traps it, and unleashes a fresh, powerful shot.

1.4.7 The Tristate Inverter (Restoring 4T Stack)

Think of the tri-state inverter as an inverter with a master switch. It can either invert the input or disconnect the output completely.

1. First, Understand What the Circuit Must Do

EN = 0 (DISABLED)

Input \(A\) can be 0 or 1, but output \(Y = Z\). The output is completely disconnected from both rails.

EN = 1 (ENABLED)

The circuit behaves like a normal inverter: \(Y = \overline{A}\). Strong and actively restored from the power rails!

Input A Enable EN Output Y Operating Mode
0 1 1 Active Inversion (\(V_{DD}\))
1 1 0 Active Inversion (GND)
X (0 or 1) 0 Z Disconnected (High-Z)
FIGURE 1.27: CMOS Tristate Inverter: (a) Transistor schematic; (b) EN = 0 (both enable switches open → Y = 'Z'); (c) EN = 1 (active inverter: Y = \(\overline{A}\)); (d) Circuit symbols.

2. Now Look at Diagram (a): Why Are the Pins Positioned Like That? 🔍

Imagine the transistor stack has two paths leading directly to output junction \(Y\):

Top Path: Pull-Up Network

\(V_{DD} \rightarrow\) two pMOS transistors \(\rightarrow Y\)

Role: Can pull node \(Y\) up to strong logic 1 (\(V_{DD}\)).

Bottom Path: Pull-Down Network

\(Y \rightarrow\) two nMOS transistors \(\rightarrow\) GND

Role: Can pull node \(Y\) down to strong logic 0 (GND).

Physical Pin Positioning Logic

Now, the position of each control pin matters:

  • Data Input A connects to one pMOS in the top path and one nMOS in the bottom path: This makes the circuit invert the data. When the gate is enabled, it determines whether \(Y\) discharges to GND or charges to \(V_{DD}\).
  • Complementary Enable \(\overline{EN}\) connects to the extra pMOS in the top path: It controls whether the pull-up path can operate. Since pMOS activates on 0 (LOW), applying \(\overline{EN} = 0\) opens the path from \(V_{DD}\) to \(Y\).
  • True Enable EN connects to the extra nMOS in the bottom path: It controls whether the pull-down path can operate. Since nMOS activates on 1 (HIGH), applying \(EN = 1\) opens the path from \(Y\) to GND.

Why use both EN and \(\overline{EN}\)? Because pMOS turns ON with a LOW gate signal, while nMOS turns ON with a HIGH gate signal. Dual-rail enable ensures both paths open simultaneously when enabled!

3. Watch What Happens in Each Case ⚡

Case A: EN = 0 (Disabled)

Then \(\overline{EN} = 1\).
• The extra pMOS is OFF.
• The extra nMOS is OFF.
Both paths are broken, regardless of the value of input \(A\).

Therefore, neither the supply nor ground can drive node \(Y\):

\[ \boxed{Y = Z} \]

That's how the circuit disconnects its output.

Case B: EN = 1 (Enabled)

Then \(\overline{EN} = 0\).
• The extra pMOS is ON.
• The extra nMOS is ON.
The master switch enables both paths. Now input A decides which path conducts:

  • When A = 0: The top pMOS controlled by A turns ON, while the bottom nMOS controlled by A turns OFF. So \(Y\) connects towards \(V_{DD}\).
    Output: Y = 1
  • When A = 1: The top pMOS controlled by A turns OFF, while the bottom nMOS controlled by A turns ON. So \(Y\) connects towards ground.
    Output: Y = 0

Notice how A controls the inversion, while EN controls whether the circuit operates at all.

4. What Do Diagrams (b), (c), and (d) Mean?

  • (b) EN = 0: Both paths are disabled, so \(\boxed{Y = Z}\). Both enable switches are open.
  • (c) EN = 1: The inverter is enabled, so \(\boxed{Y = \overline{A}}\). Both enable switches are closed and conceptually disappear.
  • (d) The Compact Logic Symbol: The triangle represents the inverter. The small circle at the output means inversion (NOT). A small circle on the enable pin indicates an active-LOW enable (\(\overline{\text{EN}}\)).
THE MEMORY TRICK · Master vs Worker

"A controls the answer; EN controls whether an answer is allowed out."

That's the whole purpose of a tri-state inverter: it can output an inverted 0 or 1, or output neither and leave the connection free for another device.

Rule Exception · Conduction Complements Violation

Unlike static CMOS gates (such as NAND, NOR, and AOI), the tristate inverter deliberately violates the conduction complements rule. When \(EN = 0\), both pull-up and pull-down networks are deliberately turned OFF simultaneously to produce the floating high-impedance state 'Z'.

Architectural Note: Tristate Buses vs Modern Multiplexers

Historically, wide processor buses were shared using tristates. In modern sub-micron chips, tristate buses are largely replaced with static multiplexers to eliminate bus contention crowbar currents (if two drivers switch simultaneously) and floating bus receiver leakage.

1.4.8 Multiplexers: Core Data Manipulation Blocks

Let's break this down from the beginning. Multiplexers are key building blocks in CMOS memory elements and data manipulation structures.
Figure 1.29 shows an Inverting Multiplexer (MUX), implemented using transistors in (a) and (b), and represented using a logic symbol in (c).

The key idea is simple: choose one input, then invert it.

1. First, Understand What a Multiplexer Does

Imagine you have two data inputs, \(D_0\) and \(D_1\), but only one output wire, \(Y\).
A select signal \(S\) decides which input gets through:

  • \(S = 0\) chooses \(D_0\) (Input 0).
  • \(S = 1\) chooses \(D_1\) (Input 1).

The ordinary (non-inverting) multiplexer follows this rule:

Select S Selected Data Input Output of Ordinary MUX
0 \(D_0\) \(D_0\)
1 \(D_1\) \(D_1\)

Notice that the output is simply whichever input is selected. There is no inversion yet.

2. What Makes It an Inverting Multiplexer?

Look carefully at part (c) of Figure 1.29. There is a small circle at output \(Y\). That circle is called an inversion bubble. It means the output is complemented, or NOT-ed.

So the circuit performs two sequential operations:

  1. Select \(D_0\) or \(D_1\).
  2. Invert the selected value!

Table 1.6: Inverting 2:1 Multiplexer Truth Table

Select S \(\overline{S}\) \(D_1\) \(D_0\) Selected Input Output Y
0 1 X 0 \(D_0 = 0\) 1
0 1 X 1 \(D_0 = 1\) 0
1 0 0 X \(D_1 = 0\) 1
1 0 1 X \(D_1 = 1\) 0
Core Exam Takeaway

Remember: \(S = 0\) selects \(D_0\), while \(S = 1\) selects \(D_1\). The output is always the opposite of the selected data bit.

3. The Most Important Equation

For an ordinary 2-to-1 multiplexer, the standard Boolean sum-of-products expression is:

\(M = \overline{S} \cdot D_0 + S \cdot D_1\)

Let's decode what each symbol means:

  • \(\overline{S} \cdot D_0\): Select \(D_0\) when \(S = 0\) (so \(\overline{S} = 1\)).
  • \(S \cdot D_1\): Select \(D_1\) when \(S = 1\).
  • \(+\) means logical OR.
  • Multiplication (\(\cdot\)) means logical AND.
THE MAIN RESULT TO REMEMBER FOR EXAMS

Since your circuit is an inverting multiplexer, the entire output expression is complemented:

\[ \boxed{Y = \overline{\overline{S} \cdot D_0 + S \cdot D_1}} \]

That boxed expression is the master equation to write in any exam!

Interactive Walkthrough · Try the Inverting MUX

Change the inputs below and watch how the select signal routes and flips the bit:

Selected Input

D₀ = 0

Inverted Output Y

Y = 1

4. Now, What Are Circuits (a) and (b)?

They are transistor-level implementations.
Instead of drawing the MUX as a single abstract logic symbol, they use physical MOS transistors to perform the selection and inversion electrically.

FIGURE 1.28: Transmission gate 2:1 multiplexer: (a) Schematic using 2 transmission gates; (b) Compact circuit symbol.
FIGURE 1.29: Restoring inverting multiplexer implementations: (a) AOI22 compound gate; (b) Two ganged tristate inverters; (c) Logic symbol with inversion bubble.
Transistor-Level Operation

The transistor networks use complementary select signals \(S\) and \(\overline{S}\) (always having opposite values: \(S=0 \implies \overline{S}=1\); \(S=1 \implies \overline{S}=0\)). This complement is required to activate the chosen data path while completely isolating the other.

  • pMOS pull-up network: Connects output toward the positive supply voltage (\(V_{DD}\)), producing logic 1.
  • nMOS pull-down network: Connects output toward ground (GND), producing logic 0.

The transistor arrangement determines whether the selected data bit makes \(Y\) high or low. In this inverting MUX, a selected 0 produces output 1, and a selected 1 produces output 0.

Comparison: AOI22 Compound Gate (a) vs Ganged Tristate Inverters (b)

Notice that the schematics of (a) and (b) in Figure 1.29 are nearly identical! The only distinction is that the pull-up network has been slightly simplified and permuted in Figure 1.29(b). This optimization is valid because select signal \(S\) and its complement \(\overline{S}\) are mutually exclusive.

Implementation Style Transistor Count Restoring? Key Characteristics & Trade-offs
1. Transmission Gate MUX (Fig 1.28) 4T (+2T for \(\overline{S}\)) No (Nonrestoring) Smallest silicon area, but signal is not refreshed and delay degrades quadratically (\(N^2\)) when cascaded.
2. AOI22 Compound MUX (Fig 1.29a) 8T (+2T for \(\overline{S}\)) Yes (Full Restoring) Clean full-rail drive; implements \(Y = \overline{(S \cdot D_1) + (\overline{S} \cdot D_0)}\). Requires more internal routing wire.
3. Ganged Tristate MUX (Fig 1.29b) 8T (+2T for \(\overline{S}\)) Yes (Full Restoring) Industry Favorite: Re-orders the pull-up stack; requires less internal wiring, offers lower parasitic output capacitance, and switches faster than AOI22.

5. What Should You Remember for Your Exam? 🎓

1. Selection Rule

• \(S = 0 \Rightarrow D_0\) is selected.
• \(S = 1 \Rightarrow D_1\) is selected.

2. Inversion Rule

The output bubble at \(Y\) means NOT.
The selected input is inverted: \(0 \rightarrow 1\), \(1 \rightarrow 0\).

Final Boolean Expression

\[ \boxed{Y = \overline{\overline{S} \cdot D_0 + S \cdot D_1}} \]

One Final Memory Trick

"A normal MUX chooses; an inverting MUX chooses and flips." 😂

Part Two · Module 2 & Part One §1.4.9

Sequential Circuits, Bistability & Static Timing Analysis

Combinational vs regenerative circuits, bistable operating points, metastability physics, SR/JK latches, master-slave flip-flops, CMOS TG & tristate D-latches, and static sequencing constraints (setup, hold, time borrowing, and clock skew). Kang & Leblebici Chapter 8, Weste & Harris Chapters 1 & 10.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE DOUBLE WELL & THE KNIFE-EDGE BALANCING ACT (ACHA BRO)

"In combinational logic, transistors are like obedient light switches — turn on the input, and the output responds immediately without memory. But cross-couple two inverters back-to-back, and something miraculous happens: positive feedback creates a memory element! Their voltage transfer curves cross at three points: two deep valleys (stable logic 0 and logic 1 where loop gain is zero) and one razor-sharp hill crest in between (the metastable point \(V_m\) where gain is maximum). If data arrives too late and misses the setup aperture, the circuit balances on the peak of that hill like a marble on a knife-edge — lingering in an illegal voltage state before noise randomly knocks it down!"

1. Combinational vs. Sequential Circuits (Kang §8.1)

In combinational circuits (non-regenerative), output voltages at any instant are strictly determined as direct Boolean functions of current input voltages (neglecting propagation delay). In sequential circuits (regenerative), outputs depend on both current inputs and the historical sequence of past inputs, enabled by a feedback loop containing a memory element.

WORKED 2X.36 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

Combinational versus Sequential Circuits: Rigorous Comparison

Attribute Combinational Logic Circuits Sequential Logic Circuits
Memory Capability No memory; outputs are memoryless and stateless. Has memory; stores previous states and historical sequences.
Feedback Mechanism Strictly feedforward (non-regenerative; no output-to-input loop). Regenerative feedback loops (direct or indirect) between output and input.
Clock Synchronization Asynchronous; output updates as soon as inputs propagate. Synchronous (clocked latches/FFs) or asynchronous (level/edge gating).
Primitive Elements NAND, NOR, Inverters, AOI/OAI, multiplexers. Bistable latches, flip-flops, registers, counters, SRAM cells.
Timing Dependencies Governed by gate propagation delay \(t_{pd}\) and hazards (glitches). Governed by setup time \(t_{setup}\), hold time \(t_{hold}\), and clock period \(T_c\).

2. The Three Classes of Regenerative Circuits (Kang §8.1, Fig. 8.1)

Based on temporal behavior and stable equilibrium states, regenerative circuits are classified into three distinct categories:

1. Bistable Circuits

• Possess two stable operating states (Logic 0 and Logic 1).
• Preserves state indefinitely as long as DC supply voltage is maintained.
• Examples: Latches, flip-flops, registers, static RAM (SRAM) memory cells. By far the most critical class in digital VLSI.

2. Monostable Circuits

• Possess only one stable operating state.
• When triggered by an external pulse, the circuit enters a quasi-stable state for a predefined duration, then autonomously returns to its single stable point.
• Examples: One-shot pulse generators, glitch filters, delay generators.

3. Astable Circuits

• Possess no stable operating states.
• The circuit continuously oscillates back and forth between two quasi-stable states without ever settling into a fixed DC operating point.
• Examples: CMOS ring oscillators, multivibrators, on-chip clock generators.

3. Static Behavior of Bistable Elements & Operating Points (Kang §8.2)

Consider two identical CMOS inverters connected in a closed cross-coupled loop (Kang Fig. 8.2(a)): $$v_{o1} = v_{i2} \quad \text{and} \quad v_{o2} = v_{i1}$$ Superimposing the Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC) of Inverter 1 (\(v_{o1}\) vs \(v_{i1}\)) and Inverter 2 (\(v_{i1}\) vs \(v_{o1}\)) reveals exactly three intersection points (Kang Fig. 8.2(b)):

Operating Point A · Stable State (\(Q=1, \overline{Q}=0\))

\(v_{o1} = V_{OH} \approx V_{DD}, \quad v_{o2} = V_{OL} \approx 0\text{ V}\).
At this point, the voltage gain of both inverters is essentially zero: $$|A_{v1}| = \left|\frac{dv_{o1}}{dv_{i1}}\right| \ll 1, \quad |A_{v2}| = \left|\frac{dv_{o2}}{dv_{i2}}\right| \ll 1 \implies A_{loop} = A_{v1} \cdot A_{v2} < 1$$ Small voltage disturbances are attenuated; potential energy is at a minimum (\(E_{pot} = 0\)).

Operating Point B · Stable State (\(Q=0, \overline{Q}=1\))

\(v_{o1} = V_{OL} \approx 0\text{ V}, \quad v_{o2} = V_{OH} \approx V_{DD}\).
Identical to Point A by symmetry. Voltage gain is zero, loop gain is far below unity (\(A_{loop} \ll 1\)), and potential energy is at a local minimum. The state is firmly locked.

Operating Point C · Unstable / Metastable Point (\(V_m\))

\(v_{o1} = v_{o2} = V_m\) (the inverter switching threshold voltage).
At this point, both inverters operate in the high-gain transition region: $$|A_{v1}| > 1, \quad |A_{v2}| > 1 \implies \mathbf{A_{loop} = A_{v1} \cdot A_{v2} \gg 1}$$ Any microscopic noise perturbation is exponentially amplified, immediately driving the circuit into stable Point A or Point B. Potential energy is at a global maximum!

WORKED 2X.40 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

The Bistability Principle & The Metastable State

• The Bistability Principle: A circuit has two stable equilibrium states when its loop gain is less than unity (\(A_{loop} < 1\)) at both states, accompanied by potential energy minima. To force the circuit to transition from one stable state to the other, an external voltage trigger must be applied that temporarily drives the loop gain larger than unity (\(A_{loop} > 1\)).

• The Metastable State (Weste & Harris §10.6.1): When a latch goes opaque, its internal nodes are connected back-to-back (\(A = B\)). If the data signal transitions inside the forbidden setup/hold aperture (\(t_m\)), the internal nodes can become trapped at \(A = B = V_m\). Because \(V_m\) is electrically self-consistent, the voltages could theoretically remain there indefinitely.

• The Ball-on-a-Hill Analogy (Weste Fig. 10.42(d)): Stable states are like two valleys on either side of a hill. The metastable state is the exact knife-edge apex. A ball balanced at the top is in equilibrium; however, thermal noise or stray electrons will eventually cause the ball to roll down into either the 0-valley or 1-valley.

• Resolution Delay \(t_{DQ}\) (Weste Fig. 10.43): As the arrival time of data approaches the critical metastable time \(t_m\) (\(t_{DC} \to t_m\)), the clock-to-Q delay grows exponentially: $$t_{DQ} = \tau_s \ln\left(\frac{V_{DD}}{\Delta V_0}\right) \propto -\tau_s \ln|t_{DC} - t_m|$$ where \(\tau_s\) is the small-signal time constant of the cross-coupled inverter pair.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · EXTERNAL LEVERS ON THE CROSS-COUPLED CORE (ACHA BRO)

"The two-inverter bistable element from Tab 1 holds a bit forever, but it has no external steering wheel — you can't tell it when to switch! To give it an external trigger, we insert pull-down or pull-up transistors that can overpower the feedback. Put parallel nMOS switches at the bottom, and you get the active-HIGH NOR-based SR Latch (positive logic). Put series nMOS or parallel pMOS switches at the top, and you get the active-LOW NAND-based SR Latch (negative logic). Each has a forbidden state where both outputs collapse to the same voltage!"

1. The CMOS NOR-Based SR Latch (Kang §8.3, Figs. 8.7 & 8.8)

The basic CMOS SR latch consists of two cross-coupled CMOS NOR2 gates with two triggering inputs: \(S\) (Set) and \(R\) (Reset), and two complementary outputs: \(Q\) and \(\overline{Q}\).

WORKED 2B.7 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

Design & Transistor Operation of the Conventional CMOS SR Flip-Flop / Latch

The circuit contains four nMOS transistors (\(M_1, M_2, M_3, M_4\)) and four complementary pMOS transistors:
• Set State (\(S = V_{OH}, R = V_{OL}\)): Parallel nMOS \(M_1\) and \(M_2\) are turned ON. Node \(\overline{Q}\) is pulled down to \(V_{OL} = 0\text{ V}\). This \(0\text{ V}\) turns ON the pMOS pull-up of the opposite NOR gate, charging \(Q \to V_{OH} = V_{DD}\). Both \(M_3\) and \(M_4\) are cut OFF.
• Reset State (\(S = V_{OL}, R = V_{OH}\)): nMOS \(M_3\) and \(M_4\) turn ON, pulling node \(Q \to V_{OL} = 0\text{ V}\). This turns ON the pMOS pull-up of the first gate, charging \(\overline{Q} \to V_{OH} = V_{DD}\). Both \(M_1\) and \(M_2\) are cut OFF.
• Hold State (\(S = V_{OL}, R = V_{OL}\)): Both external trigger transistors \(M_1\) and \(M_4\) are cut OFF. Feedback holds the state: if \(Q=1\), \(M_2\) conducts keeping \(\overline{Q}=0\); if \(Q=0\), \(M_3\) conducts keeping \(Q=0\).
• Not-Allowed State (\(S = V_{OH}, R = V_{OH}\)): Both trigger transistors \(M_1\) and \(M_4\) conduct simultaneously, forcing \(\mathbf{Q = \overline{Q} = 0}\). This violates complementarity! Furthermore, when both inputs are simultaneously released back to \(0\), the circuit enters a metastable race, randomly falling into \(0\) or \(1\).

NOR-Based SR Latch Static Conduction Modes (Kang Table 8.2)

\(S\)\(R\)\(Q_{n+1}\)\(\overline{Q}_{n+1}\)OperationConducting Transistor States
\(0\) (\(V_{OL}\))\(0\) (\(V_{OL}\))\(Q_n\)\(\overline{Q}_n\)Hold\(M_1, M_4\) OFF; \(M_2\) ON (if \(Q=1\)) or \(M_3\) ON (if \(\overline{Q}=1\))
\(1\) (\(V_{OH}\))\(0\) (\(V_{OL}\))\(1\)\(0\)Set\(M_1, M_2\) ON; \(M_3, M_4\) OFF
\(0\) (\(V_{OL}\))\(1\) (\(V_{OH}\))\(0\)\(1\)Reset\(M_1, M_2\) OFF; \(M_3, M_4\) ON
\(1\) (\(V_{OH}\))\(1\) (\(V_{OH}\))\(0\)\(0\)Not Allowed\(M_1, M_4\) both ON (violates \(Q \neq \overline{Q}\))

2. The CMOS NAND-Based SR Latch (Kang §8.3, Figs. 8.11 & 8.12)

Inverting the logic produces the cross-coupled NAND2 SR latch. Because a NAND gate produces a \(1\) whenever any input is \(0\), the NAND SR latch responds to Active-LOW inputs (\(\overline{S}\) and \(\overline{R}\)) — also referred to as Negative Logic.

WORKED 2X.38 [10 MARKS] · CHECKED

SR Latch with Positive and Negative Logic: Comprehensive Contrast

Attribute NOR-Based SR Latch (Positive Logic) NAND-Based SR Latch (Negative Logic)
Active Input Polarity Active-HIGH (\(S=1\) sets, \(R=1\) resets) Active-LOW (\(\overline{S}=0\) sets, \(\overline{R}=0\) resets)
Quiescent Hold State \(\mathbf{S = 0, \quad R = 0}\) \(\mathbf{\overline{S} = 1, \quad \overline{R} = 1}\)
Forbidden Input Condition \(S = 1, R = 1 \implies \mathbf{Q = \overline{Q} = 0}\) \(\overline{S} = 0, \overline{R} = 0 \implies \mathbf{Q = \overline{Q} = 1}\)
Schematic Symbol Plain input pins \(S\) and \(R\) Input pins marked with inversion bubbles (\(\overline{S}\), \(\overline{R}\))
Depletion-Load Option Requires pull-down nMOS with depletion load Cross-coupled depletion-load NAND (Kang Fig. 8.13); higher static DC power than CMOS
PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · SOLVING THE FORBIDDEN STATE & THE SLUICE GATE (ACHA BRO)

"The SR latch has an annoying flaw: what if both inputs accidentally hit 1? The output goes crazy! The JK latch fixes this genius-style: it feeds back the outputs \(Q\) and \(\overline{Q}\) to the inputs so that when \(J = K = 1\), it cleanly toggles! But wait — if the clock stays high too long, the latch races around, oscillating like a broken alarm. The cure? Build a Master-Slave Flip-Flop! Master catches the input while the clock is high, and locks the door; Slave releases the output only when the clock falls. Master and slave never open together, completely killing the oscillation hazard!"

1. Clocked SR Latch: Synchronous Gating (Kang §8.4, Figs. 8.14 & 8.16)

Unclocked latches respond to inputs immediately at any delay-dependent instant. Synchronous systems add a gating clock (\(\text{CK}\)). When \(\text{CK} = 0\), the input gates are locked and the latch holds state. When \(\text{CK} = 1\), inputs are sampled.
• CMOS AOI Realization (Kang Fig. 8.16): Rather than using two separate AND2 and two NOR2 gates (which requires 16 transistors), CMOS implements the clocked NOR SR latch using two cross-coupled AND-OR-Invert (AOI21) gates, drastically slashing transistor count to just 12 transistors.
• Glitch Sensitivity: The circuit is strictly level-sensitive while \(\text{CK} = 1\). Any noise spike on \(S\) or \(R\) during this window can corrupt the stored bit.

2. The Clocked JK Latch & Race-Around Oscillation (Kang §8.4, Figs. 8.19 & 8.21)

The JK latch removes the forbidden state by cross-feeding the outputs back into the input gating logic: $$S = J \cdot \overline{Q}, \quad R = K \cdot Q$$

WORKED 2X.37 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

Advantage of the JK Flip-Flop over the SR Flip-Flop

In an SR latch, the input condition \(S = R = 1\) is strictly forbidden because it forces \(Q = \overline{Q}\) and creates an unpredictable race condition when clock falls. In a JK latch, feedback from the complementary outputs ensures that when \(\mathbf{J = K = 1}\), the latch toggles (\(Q_{n+1} = \overline{Q}_n\)). Every single input combination is valid and deterministic (Kang Table 8.3).

JK Latch Detailed Truth Table (Kang Table 8.3)

\(Q_n\)\(J\)\(K\)\(Q_{n+1}\)\(\overline{Q}_{n+1}\)Operation Mode
00001Hold (\(Q_{n+1} = Q_n\))
10010
00101Reset (\(Q_{n+1} = 0\))
10101
01010Set (\(Q_{n+1} = 1\))
11010
01110Toggle (\(Q_{n+1} = \overline{Q}_n\))
11101
THE RACE-AROUND PROBLEM · CONTINUOUS OSCILLATION

When \(J = K = 1\) during an active clock pulse (\(\text{CK} = 1\)), the output toggles. But because the latch is level-sensitive, the toggled output immediately feeds back to the inputs, toggling it again! The output oscillates continuously with frequency \(f_{osc} = 1 / (2 t_{pd})\) as long as \(\text{CK} = 1\).
• Theoretical Constraint: To toggle exactly once per cycle (acting as a toggle switch), the clock pulse width \(t_{pw}\) must be strictly smaller than the latch propagation delay: $$t_{pw} < t_{pd}$$ • Practical Difficulty: Because gate delays vary wildly with PVT corners (typically \(50\text{--}200\text{ ps}\)), designing a clock pulse that is guaranteed narrower than \(t_{pd}\) without failing is virtually impossible in digital VLSI.

3. The Master-Slave JK Flip-Flop (Kang §8.4, Figs. 8.23 & 8.24)

The race-around problem is permanently eliminated by cascading two stages clocked on opposite clock phases:

WORKED 2X.39 [10 MARKS] · CHECKED

Circuit Operation of the Master-Slave JK Flip-Flop

• Phase 1 (\(\text{CK} = 1\)): The Master stage is enabled and samples primary inputs \(J\) and \(K\), setting internal nodes \(Q_m\) and \(\overline{Q}_m\). Meanwhile, the inverter on \(\text{CK}\) holds the Slave stage disabled (\(\overline{\text{CK}} = 0\)). The slave isolates the primary output \(Q_s\), holding its previous value.

• Phase 2 (\(\text{CK} = 0\)): The Master stage is disabled (\(\text{CK} = 0\)), freezing its state and ignoring all further changes on \(J\) and \(K\). Simultaneously, the Slave stage becomes active (\(\overline{\text{CK}} = 1\)), transferring the stored master values \(Q_m\) to primary outputs \(Q_s\).

• Non-Transparency Property: Because the master and slave are never transparent at the same time, there is no direct combinational path from inputs \(J, K\) to outputs \(Q_s\). This breaks the continuous feedback loop, guaranteeing that when \(J=K=1\), the flip-flop toggles exactly once per clock cycle.

• The "One's Catching" Hazard: Because the master latch is level-sensitive while \(\text{CK} = 1\), a temporary noise spike or glitch on the \(J\) line (or \(K\) line) will flip the master latch. Once set, the master cannot be cleared during that clock phase, and the erroneous bit propagates to the slave on the falling clock edge. This vulnerability motivates the move to true edge-triggered flip-flops.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE OPEN DOOR VS SNAPSHOT CAMERA & TG EFFICIENCY (ACHA BRO)

"Direct CMOS gates like AOI latches are fine, but they use too many transistors. In advanced VLSI, we build latches directly with CMOS Transmission Gates (TGs)! A TG D-latch uses just two inverters and two transmission gates: input TG opens the door when clock is high (transparent loading), and feedback TG closes the holding circle when clock is low. Cascading two such stages on opposite clocks creates the famous negative-edge-triggered master-slave D flip-flop — advancing data by exactly one clock cycle and making timing analysis clean and race-free!"

1. How Transmission Gates & Inverters Create Physical Memory

A transmission gate alone does not remember anything. An inverter alone does not remember anything either.
Together, inverters and feedback create a bi-stable holding state, while transmission gates decide when that state is allowed to change.

Route 1: The Input Route (Brings New Data)

\(D \rightarrow\) Input Inverter \(\rightarrow\) Input TG1 (controlled by \(CLK\)) \(\rightarrow\) Node X.

When \(CLK=1\), TG1 opens the door and forces new external data onto node X.

Route 2: The Feedback Route (Preserves Old Data)

\(Q \rightarrow\) Feedback Inverter \(\rightarrow\) Feedback TG2 (controlled by \(\overline{CLK}\)) \(\rightarrow\) Node X.

When \(CLK=0\), TG2 closes the feedback loop, locking node X into a continuous self-reinforcing circle.

CMOS Transmission-Gate D Latch Schematic

Figure: TG1 and TG2 are clocked on opposite phases (\(CLK\) and \(\overline{CLK}\)). They take turns controlling Node X.

Why Doesn't the Feedback Overwrite the Input? 🚫

Because the two transmission gates are controlled in strictly opposite clock phases:

Clock Phase Input Gate (TG1) Feedback Gate (TG2) What Physically Happens
Transparent Phase (\(CLK=1\)) ON OFF New input \(D\) dictates node X. Feedback is cut off so there is zero competition.
Hold Phase (\(CLK=0\)) OFF ON Input \(D\) is disconnected. Feedback loop closes, preserving and reinforcing \(Q\).
Critical Timing Hazard · Bus Fight Prevention

If both routes were enabled at the same time, the input inverter and feedback inverter would fight each other directly at node X, producing invalid intermediate voltages and heavy crowbar power dissipation. Modern ICs enforce non-overlapping clocks to ensure TG1 turns OFF before TG2 turns ON!

2. CMOS D-Latch: Version 1 (TG) vs. Version 2 (Tristate Inverters) (Kang §8.5)

Version 1: Transmission-Gate D-Latch (Kang Fig. 8.27)

• Built with two CMOS inverters and two transmission gates.
• Input TG is enabled by \(CK\); loop TG is enabled by \(\overline{CK}\).
• Setup & Hold Requirements (Kang Fig. 8.28): Input \(D\) must be valid and stable for a brief duration before (\(t_{setup}\)) and after (\(t_{hold}\)) the negative clock transition during which the input TG closes and the loop TG completes the feedback ring. Violating these timing specs leads directly into metastability!

Version 2: Tristate Inverter D-Latch (Kang Fig. 8.29)

• Contains two clocked tristate inverters and one standard inverter.
• Operation: When \(CK = 1\), the first tristate inverter drives input \(D\) into the core while the second tristate inverter is in High-Z. When \(CK = 0\), the input buffer enters High-Z while the second tristate completes the two-inverter regenerative holding loop.
• Eliminates separate pass-gate routing, achieving a compact standard-cell layout.

WORKED 2X.41 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

Clocked CMOS D-Latch Architecture & Transparency

A clocked CMOS D-latch (Kang Figs. 8.26–8.29) is a level-sensitive memory element. When the clock is active (\(CK = 1\)), the latch is completely transparent — any logic changes on \(D\) propagate directly to output \(Q\). When \(CK = 0\), the input switch opens and the internal bistable loop locks the captured state. Because it is level-sensitive, it cannot prevent race-through across multiple cascaded logic stages during an active clock phase.

3. CMOS Master-Slave Negative Edge-Triggered D Flip-Flop (Kang §8.5, Fig. 8.30)

Cascading two D-latches with complementary clocks creates the fundamental industrial storage element of digital VLSI:

WORKED 2X.35 [10 MARKS] · CHECKED

D Flip-Flop using Transmission Gates (Kang Fig. 8.30)

• Circuit Topology: Consists of two cascaded stages — a Master D-latch and a Slave D-latch, requiring four inverters and four transmission gates (or equivalent tristates):
  • Master Stage: Input TG clocked by \(CK\), feedback TG clocked by \(\overline{CK}\). (Positive level-sensitive).
  • Slave Stage: Input TG clocked by \(\overline{CK}\), feedback TG clocked by \(CK\). (Negative level-sensitive).

• Step-by-Step Operation Across Clock Phases:
  • When \(CK = 1\): Master input TG is ON, loop TG is OFF; the master latch is transparent, following input \(D\) (\(Q_m = D\)). Meanwhile, the slave input TG is OFF and slave loop TG is ON; the slave holds the previous output value \(Q_s\). Changes at \(D\) are strictly prevented from reaching \(Q_s\).
  • At Negative Transition (\(CK: 1 \to 0\)): Master input TG turns OFF, freezing and storing the exact state of \(D\) present at that instant. Concurrently, slave input TG turns ON, transferring the master's captured bit \(Q_m\) cleanly to the output \(Q_s\).
  • When \(CK = 0\): Master remains opaque and disconnected from \(D\); slave loop holds the captured bit at \(Q_s\).
• Conclusion: The output updates strictly at the negative (falling) clock edge. This is a robust negative edge-triggered D flip-flop!

WORKED 2X.42 [5 MARKS] · CHECKED

Why Edge-Triggered Flip-Flops are Preferred over Level-Sensitive Latches

• Elimination of Race-Through Hazards: A level-sensitive latch is transparent for the entire duration of the active clock phase. If combinational logic between two latches is fast, a data signal can race through multiple successive pipeline stages in a single clock cycle, corrupting the system state.
• Clean Cycle-by-Cycle Advancement: An edge-triggered flip-flop samples its input strictly at the infinitesimal clock edge and remains completely opaque for the remainder of the clock period. This guarantees that data tokens advance by exactly one register stage per clock cycle.
• Deterministic Static Timing Analysis: Flip-flop pipelines sharply delineate cycle boundaries, making max-delay (setup) and min-delay (hold) timing closure straightforward and robust against race conditions (Kang §8.4–8.5, Weste §10.2.1).

Physically Following a '1' Through the Transistors ⚡

Assume \(D = 1\) and the latch enters the transparent phase (\(CLK = 1\)):

Phase 1: Transparent Loading (\(D=1 \rightarrow Q=1\))

  1. First Inverter (Inv1): Receives \(D = 1\). Its nMOS turns ON, discharging output node capacitance to GND → Output becomes 0.
  2. Input TG1 Opens: With \(CLK = 1\), both nMOS and pMOS in TG1 conduct. The \(0\text{ V}\) passes straight through to internal node X (\(X = 0\)). (Note: Transmission gates do not invert!)
  3. Main Inverter (Inv2): Sees \(X = 0\). Its pMOS turns ON, pulling output node \(Q\) to \(V_{DD}\) → Output becomes \(Q = 1\).

\[ D = 1 \quad\longrightarrow\quad X = 0 \quad\longrightarrow\quad Q = 1 \]

So far, that's pure signal routing: two inversions cancel out, preserving the original bit.

Phase 2: Clock Closes the Door. How Does the '1' Survive?

Now clock falls: \(CLK = 0\). Watch how the loop locks the bit:

  1. TG1 turns OFF: The conductive channel severs. External changes at \(D\) can no longer enter node X.
  2. TG2 turns ON: The feedback bridge closes!
  3. Feedback Inverter (Inv3): Receives stored output \(Q = 1\), inverts it, and drives 0.
  4. TG2 passes 0 back to Node X: Node X is held pinned at \(0\text{ V}\).
  5. Main Inverter (Inv2) recreates Q = 1: Seeing \(X = 0\), it continuously drives \(Q\) towards \(V_{DD}\) (logic 1).

\[ Q = 1 \xrightarrow{\text{Inv3}} 0 \xrightarrow{\text{TG2}} X = 0 \xrightarrow{\text{Inv2}} Q = 1 \]

The cycle endlessly reinforces the stored state!

CRUCIAL PHYSICAL INSIGHT · No Perpetual Motion

"The feedback inverter does not send the original current circulating around in a circle forever! Rather, the output voltage controls the gates of the transistors, and the power supply (\(V_{DD}\) / GND) continuously replenishes charge to maintain the node voltages."

Symmetric Operation · What If We Stored a '0'?

If \(D = 0\), the exact mirror image occurs:

\[ D = 0 \quad\longrightarrow\quad X = 1 \quad\longrightarrow\quad Q = 0 \]

During hold, Inv3 turns \(Q=0\) into \(1\), TG2 passes \(1\) back to node X, and Inv2 keeps driving \(Q = 0\). Fully symmetrical and stable!

Building an Edge-Triggered Flip-Flop from Two Latches

An edge-triggered D flip-flop is built by connecting two D latches in series (Master-Slave structure) driven by opposite clock phases:

  • Master Latch (Clocked with \(\overline{CLK}\)): Transparent when \(CLK=0\), opaque when \(CLK=1\).
  • Slave Latch (Clocked with \(CLK\)): Opaque when \(CLK=0\), transparent when \(CLK=1\).

Result: At the exact rising edge \(0 \rightarrow 1\), the Master closes and hands its captured bit to the Slave, which opens and drives \(Q\). Neither latch is ever transparent at the same time, producing true edge-triggered isolation!

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE CANAL LOCK OF COMPUTATION (ACHA BRO)

"Imagine a canal with two watertight gates (locks) like in the Panama Canal. Boats (data tokens) must pass from one reservoir to the next without crashing! If both gates opened at once, the water would rush through uncontrollably and destroy everything (that is a hold-time race condition!). In two-phase latch sequencing, one gate is always tightly closed when the other is open. In flip-flop sequencing, we use a single hard edge. If your combinational logic is too slow, you miss the gate closing (Setup Violation — fix it by slowing the clock!). But if your logic is too fast and runs into the next stage during the hold aperture, you cause a Hold Violation — a fatal silicon death that NO clock change can ever cure!"

1. The Three Methods of Sequencing Static Circuits (Weste & Harris §10.2.1)

Combinational logic blocks must be partitioned and synchronized to ensure tokens advance in orderly cycles without racing ahead. Weste & Harris define three primary sequencing methods (Fig. 10.2):

1. Flip-Flop-Based Sequencing

• Uses one edge-triggered flip-flop at each clock cycle boundary (period \(T_c\)).
• Tokens advance sharply on the rising edge. Early-arriving tokens must wait idly until the next rising edge.
• Characteristics: Imposes a "hard edge"; simple timing models; no race hazards if minimum delay is met.

2. 2-Phase Transparent Latches

• Divides each full clock cycle into two half-cycles using back-to-back latches clocked by non-overlapping phases \(\phi_1\) and \(\phi_2\).
• Like canal locks: at any given time, at least one clock is low and its latch opaque, preventing one token from catching up to another.
• Characteristics: Enables time borrowing; highly tolerant to clock skew.

3. Pulsed Latch Sequencing

• Eliminates one latch per cycle; applies a brief pulse (\(t_{pw}\)) to the remaining transparent latch.
• If pulse width \(t_{pw}\) is strictly shorter than logic delay, tokens advance exactly one cycle per pulse.
• Characteristics: Lowest power and lowest sequencing overhead, but sensitive to pulse-width distortion.

2. Sequencing Element Timing Notation (Weste Table 10.1)

Parameter Name Physical Description
\(t_{pd}\) Logic Propagation Delay Maximum time from inputs switching to all outputs reaching valid stable logic levels.
\(t_{cd}\) Logic Contamination Delay Minimum time from inputs switching until any output first begins to change.
\(t_{pcq}\) Clock-to-Q Propagation Delay Maximum time from clock triggering edge until latch/flip-flop output \(Q\) is valid.
\(t_{ccq}\) Clock-to-Q Contamination Delay Minimum time from clock triggering edge until latch/flip-flop output \(Q\) first starts to change.
\(t_{pdq}\) Latch D-to-Q Propagation Delay Propagation delay through a transparent latch from input \(D\) to output \(Q\).
\(t_{setup}\) Setup Time Minimum time that data \(D\) must remain stable before the capturing clock edge.
\(t_{hold}\) Hold Time Minimum time that data \(D\) must remain stable after the capturing clock edge.

3. Max-Delay Constraints: Setup Time Verification (Weste §10.2.2)

THE SETUP CONSTRAINT · MAX-DELAY FAILURE

Data departs launching flip-flop \(F_1\) on the rising clock edge, propagates through \(F_1\) (\(t_{pcq}\)), traverses the combinational logic path (\(t_{pd}\)), and must settle at receiving flip-flop \(F_2\) at least \(t_{setup}\) before the next rising edge: $$T_c \ge t_{pcq} + t_{pd} + t_{setup} \quad (10.1)$$ Solving for maximum allowable logic delay: $$\mathbf{t_{pd} \le T_c - (t_{pcq} + t_{setup})} \quad (10.2)$$ where \(\mathbf{(t_{pcq} + t_{setup})}\) represents the unavoidable sequencing overhead introduced by the flip-flops.

• Remedy for Setup Failure: If \(t_{pd}\) is too large, data misses setup time. This failure can be cured by simply increasing the clock period \(T_c\) (slowing down the operating frequency) or pipelining the combinational logic.

4. Min-Delay Constraints: Hold Time Verification (Weste §10.2.3)

THE HOLD CONSTRAINT · MIN-DELAY FAILURE · FATAL SILICON BUG

When the clock rises, data begins to change at \(Q_1\) after contamination delay \(t_{ccq}\) and arrives at \(D_2\) after logic contamination delay \(t_{cd}\). However, \(D_2\) must NOT change until at least \(t_{hold}\) after the clock edge, otherwise \(F_2\) will capture the newly updated data instead of holding the old data: $$t_{ccq} + t_{cd} \ge t_{hold} \implies \mathbf{t_{cd} \ge t_{hold} - t_{ccq}} \quad (10.7)$$
CRITICAL CHIP-KILLER DISTINCTION:
Notice that clock period \(T_c\) does NOT appear anywhere in the hold inequality (EQ 10.7)!
A hold-time failure CANNOT be fixed by slowing down the clock! Even at \(1\text{ Hz}\), the new data will still arrive too fast and destroy the old state. A fabricated chip with a hold violation is completely non-functional and must be re-masked and refabricated at catastrophic cost.

• Remedy for Hold Failure: Insert delay elements (pairs of inverters or buffers) along the fast combinational logic path to intentionally increase \(t_{cd}\).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · CYCLE STEALING & THE SKEW TAX (ACHA BRO)

"In flip-flop pipelines, the clock edge is like a strict train schedule — if your logic finishes early, you sit idle on the platform wasting precious cycle slack; if you're one picosecond late, you miss the train completely! Transparent latches are much smarter: they allow a heavy logic stage to 'borrow time' from a lighter downstream stage, seamlessly balancing pipeline workloads. But real silicon chips suffer from clock skew: clock trees don't arrive everywhere at the exact same picosecond. Skew taxes your maximum frequency like an unwanted toll, and inflates hold time requirements!"

1. Time Borrowing (Cycle Stealing) through Transparent Latches (Weste §10.2.4)

In a flip-flop system, the clock imposes a hard edge: computation cannot cross the cycle boundary. If data arrives early, the remaining cycle time is lost. If it arrives late, the system fails.

When using transparent latches, data can depart the first latch on the rising edge, but does not have to set up until the falling edge of the receiving latch's clock. If one half-cycle has too much logic, it can borrow time into the next half-cycle (Weste Fig. 10.12(a)).

MAXIMUM TIME BORROWING FORMULA (EQ 10.10)

Because data does not have to set up until the falling edge of the receiving latch's clock, one phase can borrow up to half a cycle of time from the next (less setup time and nonoverlap delay): $$\mathbf{t_{borrow} \le \frac{T_c}{2} - (t_{setup} + t_{nonoverlap})} \quad (10.10)$$ • Self-Bypass Loops (Weste Fig. 10.12(b)): Time borrowing can accumulate across multiple pipeline stages. However, in execution self-bypass loops (such as an ALU computing an operation and feeding the result back for a dependent instruction in the very next cycle), the entire loop must strictly complete within the cycle time \(T_c\).

2. Clock Skew Analysis in Digital VLSI (Weste §10.2.5)

In physical silicon, clock distribution networks suffer from spatial interconnect RC differences, buffer variations, and thermal gradients. The arrival time of clock edges varies by an uncertainty termed Clock Skew (\(t_{skew}\)) (Weste Fig. 10.15).

Worst-Case Max-Delay (Setup) under Skew

• Scenario: Launching flip-flop \(F_1\) receives its clock late; receiving flip-flop \(F_2\) receives its clock early.
• Available computation time is reduced by \(t_{skew}\): $$\mathbf{t_{pd} \le T_c - (t_{pcq} + t_{setup} + t_{skew})} \quad (10.12)$$ • Clock skew acts as a direct timing penalty, cutting into allowable logic delay and forcing a longer cycle period \(T_c\).

Worst-Case Min-Delay (Hold) under Skew

• Scenario: Launching flip-flop \(F_1\) receives its clock early; receiving flip-flop \(F_2\) receives its clock late.
• Data changes early at \(D_2\); required contamination delay is increased: $$\mathbf{t_{cd} \ge t_{hold} - t_{ccq} + t_{skew}} \quad (10.13)$$ • Clock skew directly widens the hold-time hazard window, requiring heavier delay padding!

SKEW TOLERANCE OF TRANSPARENT LATCHES (EQ 10.14)

Unlike flip-flop systems where skew directly subtracts from allowable logic delay, transparent latch-based systems are skew-tolerant for max delay (Weste Fig. 10.16): $$\mathbf{t_{pd} \le T_c - 2t_{pdq}} \quad (10.14)$$ Because data can arrive at any point while the latch is transparent, clock skew does not cut into the total cycle time available for computation! However, clock skew still degrades min-delay and borrowing slack: $$t_{cd1}, t_{cd2} \ge t_{hold} - t_{ccq} - t_{nonoverlap} + t_{skew} \quad (10.15)$$ $$t_{borrow} \le \frac{T_c}{2} - (t_{setup} + t_{nonoverlap} + t_{skew}) \quad (10.16)$$

3. Clock Distribution & Physical vs. Logical Clocks (Weste §13.4.1, Das pp. 61–63)

A digital synchronous system is conceived by the architect using one or more logical clocks — idealized signals with zero skew used in RTL descriptions (e.g., a single clk for flip-flops, or complementary phases \(\phi_1, \phi_2\) for two-phase latches). However, due to mismatched wire lengths, process variations (\(V_t\), channel length), supply gradients, and thermal hot spots, physical clock networks deliver distorted, delayed waveforms called physical clocks.

Logical vs. Physical Clocks

• Logical Clock: Mathematical abstraction with zero skew. Used in HDLs (Verilog/VHDL) for RTL synthesis and functional simulation.
• Physical Clock: The real, noisy signal arriving at the gate terminal of each storage element across silicon. Suffers from systematic spatial skew and cycle-to-cycle temporal jitter.
• Single Global Clock Distribution: Distributing multiple low-skew clock trees across an entire die is physically intractable. Modern chips route one global clock (using H-trees, grids, or resonant meshes) and synthesize local phases using local clock gaters placed adjacent to the leaf cells.

Local Clock Gaters & Drivers

Located right next to clocked storage elements, local gaters produce specialized physical waveforms driven over very short wires:

  • Inverters: Generate local complementary clocks (\(\overline{\text{clk}}\)) for transmission gates.
  • Integrated Clock Gaters (ICG): Latch-free or latch-based AND gates that halt clock switching to inactive units to eliminate dynamic power \(P_{dyn} = \alpha C V_{DD}^2 f\).
  • Pulse Generators: Synthesize brief pulses (~100–200 ps) for pulsed latches.
  • Buffers: Sharpen clock transition edges to reduce short-circuit overlap dissipation.

4. Formal Definition of Clock Skew & Weste Fig. 13.18 Examples

FORMAL SKEW DEFINITION · WESTE §13.4.1
