  • Industrial Uniformity Requirement: The process must deliver consistent etch rates across the entire wafer, from wafer to wafer, between batches, and across varying feature dimensions and pattern densities.

Immersion Etching

The wafer is submerged directly into a wet chemical bath. Mechanical agitation (nitrogen bubbling or ultrasonic megasonics) is required to maintain etch consistency, but local stagnation often leads to variations.

Spray Etching (Modern Fab Standard)

Fresh etchant solution is continuously atomized and sprayed onto the rotating wafer surface. This dynamic renewal dramatically improves etch rate and cross-wafer uniformity, largely replacing static immersion baths.

3. Etch-Rate Uniformity: Master Formula & Textbook Numerical

Industrial wafer etching defines etch-rate uniformity across multiple radial test sites:

Etch-Rate Uniformity Equation: $$\mathbf{U = \frac{R_{\max} - R_{\min}}{R_{\max} + R_{\min}} \times 100\%}$$

Where \(R_{\max}\) is maximum measured etch rate and \(R_{\min}\) is minimum measured etch rate.
Crucial Math Note: Notice that the denominator is the sum \((R_{\max} + R_{\min})\), not the average rate! Under this definition, a smaller percentage signifies superior uniformity across the wafer.

WORKED TEXTBOOK NUMERICAL · 300 MM ALUMINUM WAFER

Problem: A 300 mm wafer has the following measured Aluminum etch rates at 5 locations:
• Centre: \(750\,\text{nm/min}\)
• Left: \(812\,\text{nm/min}\)
• Right: \(765\,\text{nm/min}\)
• Top: \(743\,\text{nm/min}\)
• Bottom: \(798\,\text{nm/min}\)

Part A: Calculate Average Etch Rate (\(R_{\text{avg}}\)):
$$R_{\text{avg}} = \frac{750 + 812 + 765 + 743 + 798}{5} = \frac{3868}{5} = \mathbf{773.6\,\text{nm/min}}$$
Part B: Calculate Etch-Rate Uniformity (\(U\)):
Identify extremes: \(R_{\max} = 812\,\text{nm/min}\), \(R_{\min} = 743\,\text{nm/min}\).
$$U = \frac{812 - 743}{812 + 743} \times 100\% = \frac{69}{1555} \times 100\% \approx \mathbf{4.44\% \approx 4.4\%}$$
Exam Presentation Tip: Write down formula, identify \(R_{\max}\) and \(R_{\min}\), substitute correctly, and include the percentage sign (%)!

4. Wet Chemical Etching of Silicon (Oxidation + Dissolution)

Wet chemical etching of semiconductor silicon fundamentally involves oxidation followed by dissolution of the oxide. The etchant is a mixture of nitric acid (\(HNO_3\)) and hydrofluoric acid (\(HF\)), diluted with water or acetic acid.

$$\mathbf{Si + 4HNO_3 \rightarrow SiO_2 + 2H_2O + 4NO_2}$$

Step 1: Nitric acid (\(HNO_3\)) oxidizes the exposed silicon into solid silicon dioxide (\(SiO_2\)).
Step 2: Hydrofluoric acid (\(HF\)) simultaneously dissolves and complexes the resulting \(SiO_2\) into soluble \(H_2SiF_6\).

Chemical Distinction: The equation above depicts the oxidation stage. Silicon is etched away completely through the combined sequence of oxidation plus oxide dissolution, not merely by stopping at an oxide passivation layer.

5. Dry Etching: Why Use a Plasma? (Isotropic vs Anisotropic)

The fatal limitation of wet chemical etching on amorphous or polycrystalline thin films is that it is purely isotropic — the lateral etch rate (\(R_l\)) approximately equals the vertical etch rate (\(R_v\)).

Isotropic Etching (Wet Chemical)

Material is dissolved sideways beneath the photoresist at the same rate as downwards. This creates a severe circular undercut. The etched trench becomes wider than the mask opening, making features < 3 μm impossible to control.

Anisotropic Etching (Dry Plasma / RIE)

Energetic ions accelerated vertically by an electric field bombard the wafer surface. Material is removed preferentially in the vertical direction with negligible sideways etching, producing crisp vertical sidewalls that mirror the resist pattern with high fidelity.

6. Degree of Anisotropy (\(A_f\)): Derivation & Worked Numerical

The directional fidelity of an etching process is quantified by the Degree of Anisotropy (\(A_f\)):

$$\mathbf{A_f = 1 - \frac{l}{h_f} = 1 - \frac{R_l}{R_v}}$$

Where \(h_f\) is film thickness being etched, \(l\) is lateral undercut beneath the mask, \(R_l\) is lateral etch rate, and \(R_v\) is vertical etch rate.

Why Does the Formula Equate Both Forms?

If etching proceeds for a duration \(t\), the lateral undercut is \(l = R_l \cdot t\) and the vertical etch depth is \(h_f = R_v \cdot t\). Therefore:
$$\frac{l}{h_f} = \frac{R_l \cdot t}{R_v \cdot t} = \frac{R_l}{R_v}$$ The etching duration \(t\) cancels out completely!

Case Condition Degree of Anisotropy (\(A_f\)) Physical Meaning
Case 1: Purely Isotropic \(R_l = R_v\) $$\mathbf{A_f = 1 - \frac{R_v}{R_v} = 0}$$ Lateral etch equals vertical etch. Severe circular undercut. Typical of wet chemical baths.
Case 2: Partially Anisotropic \(0 < R_l < R_v\) $$\mathbf{0 < A_f < 1}$$ Slanted or tapered sidewalls. Acceptable for non-critical wide contacts or bonding pads.
Case 3: Ideal Vertical Etch \(R_l = 0\) $$\mathbf{A_f = 1 - \frac{0}{R_v} = 1}$$ Zero lateral undercut. Perfect 90° vertical profiles. Mandatory for deep sub-micron CMOS gates and vias.
WORKED TEXTBOOK NUMERICAL · DEGREE OF ANISOTROPY

Problem: An etching process has a measured lateral etch rate \(R_l = 20\,\text{nm/min}\) and a vertical etch rate \(R_v = 100\,\text{nm/min}\). Calculate the degree of anisotropy.

Solution:
$$A_f = 1 - \frac{R_l}{R_v} = 1 - \frac{20}{100} = 1 - 0.20 = \mathbf{0.80}$$
Interpretation: The lateral etch rate is only one-fifth (\(20\%\)) of the vertical etch rate. A value approaching 1 confirms highly directional, vertically dominated anisotropic etching.

Interactive Degree of Anisotropy (\(A_f\)) Simulator

Adjust the lateral etch rate \(R_l\) relative to a fixed vertical etch rate \(R_v = 100\text{ nm/min}\) to see the etch profile morph from purely isotropic (\(A_f = 0\)) to perfectly anisotropic (\(A_f = 1\)).

0 nm/min

Etch Profile Analysis

Degree of Anisotropy (\(A_f\)): 1.00
Etch Classification: COMPLETELY ANISOTROPIC (RIE)
Vertical sidewalls. Zero lateral undercut. Pattern transfers from photoresist with 100% geometric fidelity.
EXAM CHECKPOINTS · ESSENTIAL SHORT ANSWERS

1. Define Lithography. State Its Requirements. [10 Marks]

Definition: Lithography is the process of transferring geometric patterns from a mask to a thin, radiation-sensitive resist layer covering a semiconductor wafer.
10 Essential Requirements & Characteristics:

  1. A mask containing the required circuit geometry.
  2. A suitable radiation source and exposure equipment (optical exposure wavelength \(\approx 0.2\) to \(0.4\,\mu\text{m}\)).
  3. A radiation-sensitive resist coating of suitable thickness.
  4. A clean processing environment to minimise particle contamination.
  5. Adequate resolution to print the minimum required feature sizes.
  6. Accurate registration between successive mask patterns.
  7. Sufficient throughput, measured in wafers exposed per hour for a mask level.
  8. Development and pattern-transfer steps that reproduce the intended geometry in the underlying material.
  9. Reliable masks with low defect density to improve chip yield.
  10. Appropriate exposure and resolution-control techniques for the required feature sizes.

2. What is the Use of a Spinner? [2 Marks]

A spinner rotates a photoresist-coated wafer at several thousand revolutions per minute to spread liquid photoresist into a thin, reasonably uniform film. Film thickness depends on spin speed and resist viscosity.

3. What Technology is Used to Produce Photomasks? [2 Marks]

Electron-beam lithography is primarily used to write patterns into an electron-sensitive resist on a fused silica substrate. The pattern is then transferred to the underlying chromium layer to form the finished photomask.

4. Dry-Etching Numerical Concept [2 Marks]

Given \(R_l = 20\,\text{nm/min}\) and \(R_v = 100\,\text{nm/min}\):
$$A_f = 1 - \frac{R_l}{R_v} = 1 - \frac{20}{100} = \mathbf{0.80}$$
The etching is highly anisotropic because the vertical etch rate exceeds the lateral etch rate by \(5\times\).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · BULLET DOPING (ACHA BRO)

"Old thermal diffusion furnace-e dopant atoms high temperature-e random bhabe surface die diffuse korto, jar karone lateral spread khub beshi hoto ar exact boundary pawa jeto na. Ion Implantation holo dopant atoms ke subatomic 'bullet' banie high voltage acceleration die wafer-er modhye shoot kora! Analyzing magnet die pure mass select kora hoy, jar fole 100% exact depth ar dopant count pawa jaye. Modern CMOS fab-e shob narrow region doping ion implantation die kora hoy!"

1. Physical Problem & Fundamental Controls (Sze & Lee §14.4)

To fabricate MOSFETs, specific regions of silicon must be doped n-type or p-type with precise dopant quantities. Ion implantation introduces energetic charged dopant ions into the semiconductor lattice. Incoming ions penetrate the silicon crystal, collide with electrons and host nuclei, shed kinetic energy, and come to rest at a controlled average depth.

Control 1: Implantation Energy → Depth

Energy ranges from 300 eV to 5 MeV, producing average penetration depths ranging from 10 nm to 10 μm. Accelerating voltage precisely governs depth.

Control 2: Monitored Current → Dose (\(\Phi\))

Dose ranges from \(10^{12}\text{ ions/cm}^2\) (for MOSFET \(V_T\) adjustment) to \(10^{16}\text{ ions/cm}^2\) (for buried insulating oxide layers or source/drain formation).

Implants Per Wafer in CMOS

Basic CMOS uses 15 to 17 ion implants. Leading-edge CMOS uses 20 to 23 implants, and specialized memories (Flash/EEPROM) use up to 30 implantation steps!

What Exactly is Dose (\(\Phi\))? $$\mathbf{\text{Dose } (\Phi) = \frac{\text{Number of Implanted Ions}}{\text{Wafer Surface Area}} = \int \frac{I_{\text{ion}}}{q \cdot A} dt}$$

Expressed in \(\text{ions/cm}^2\) (surface area concentration), not \(\text{ions/cm}^3\) (volume concentration). Monitored beam current \(I_{\text{ion}}\) and exposure time \(t\) deliver exact electrical control.

2. Schematic of a Medium-Energy Ion Implantor (Figure 13)

Figure 13 illustrates the beamline architecture of a commercial ion implantation system. To prevent unwanted beam collisions and scattering by gas molecules, the entire interior is maintained at ultra-high vacuum: below \(10^{-4}\text{ Pa}\).

Figure 13: Schematic of a medium-energy ion implantor showing ion source, extraction aperture, mass analyzer magnet, acceleration tube, and electrostatic scanning deflection plates.

Stage 1: Ion Source Chamber

A heated tungsten filament produces electron impact that breaks up source gases (\(BF_3\) for boron, \(AsH_3\) for arsenic, \(PH_3\) for phosphorus) into charged dopant ions (\(B^+, As^+, P^+\)).

Stage 2: Extraction & Mass Analysis Magnet

An extraction voltage of ~40 kV extracts positive ions into a 90° magnetic analyzer. The magnetic field \(B\) bends ions into a circular arc: $$\mathbf{R = \frac{1}{B}\sqrt{\frac{2mV}{q}}}$$ Only ions with the exact desired mass-to-charge ratio pass through the resolving aperture, filtering out all impurities and unwanted isotopes!

Stage 3: Acceleration Tube

Selected ions enter a multi-electrode column and accelerate from high voltage to ground, acquiring final kinetic energy (300 eV to 5 MeV). Apertures ensure the beam is tightly collimated.

Stage 4: Electrostatic Scanning Plates

Orthogonal X and Y electrostatic deflection plates scan the focused ion beam across the entire wafer surface with high frequency, achieving exceptional cross-wafer dose uniformity (±1%).

Stage 5: Implantation & Subsurface Profile

Ions lose energy via nuclear and electronic stopping, coming to rest in a Gaussian profile with projected range \(R_p\) and straggle \(\Delta R_p\): $$N(x) = \frac{\Phi}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Delta R_p} \exp\left(-\frac{(x - R_p)^2}{2\Delta R_p^2}\right)$$

CRITICAL PHENOMENON · LATTICE DAMAGE & THERMAL ANNEALING

Why is Post-Implant Annealing Mandatory?
Energetic ion collisions violently displace host silicon atoms from their crystalline positions, creating point defects, dislocation loops, and at high doses amorphizing the lattice. Furthermore, as-implanted dopants reside primarily in interstitial sites where they are electrically inactive. A subsequent Rapid Thermal Annealing (RTA) treatment (900°–1050°C for 10–30 seconds) heats the wafer under controlled inert atmosphere to recrystallize the damaged lattice and drive dopant atoms into substitutional sites, making them electrically active.

EXAM 2-MARK MODEL ANSWER · QUESTION 1X.7

Question: Name the doping technique for controlled doping in a narrow region. [2 Marks]
Answer: Ion Implantation. It is a doping technique in which energetic charged dopant ions are accelerated by an electric field and directed into a semiconductor substrate. It offers precise and reproducible control over dopant dose and penetration depth, operates at a lower processing temperature than diffusion, and is followed by annealing to repair implantation-induced lattice damage.

CMOS Fabrication: Physical Manufacturing & Technology

From wafer oxidation to self-aligned sub-micron transistor synthesis. Weste & Harris §1.5.2 & §3.2.3–3.2.4.

3.2.4 Silicon Dioxide (\(\text{SiO}_2\)): Properties & Manufacturing

Think of silicon as the land where we build our transistors. Silicon dioxide (\(\text{SiO}_2\)) is the deliberate, high-purity insulating layer we create on that land.

Industry Dominance · Why Silicon Won

"Unlike competing materials (like GaAs or Germanium), silicon has dominated the semiconductor industry because it possesses an easily processable, high-quality native oxide that can be thermally grown and chemically etched with extreme precision."

Three Critical Roles of \(\text{SiO}_2\) by Thickness

1. Thin Gate Oxide

Situated directly between the polysilicon gate and the silicon channel. Must be ultra-thin (a few nanometers) and defect-free to maximize gate capacitance and electrostatic control over channel inversion.

2. Thicker High-Voltage Oxide

Required for I/O buffers and higher operating voltage transistors to prevent dielectric breakdown under elevated electric fields.

3. Thick Field Oxide (FOX)

Grown between active device areas. Heavily attenuates fringing electric fields beneath polysilicon and metal interconnects, preventing unintentional parasitic transistor formation.

Three Methods of Forming \(\text{SiO}_2\)

Method Atmosphere & Temp Growth Rate Quality & Primary Application
Wet Oxidation Water vapor (\(\text{H}_2\text{O}\) vapor / pyrogenic \(2:1\ \text{H}_2:\text{O}_2\))
900 – 1000 °C		 Rapid Porous, lower dielectric quality. Ideal for growing thick field oxides and masking layers.
Dry Oxidation Pure dry Oxygen (\(\text{O}_2\))
≈ 1200 °C		 Slow & Highly Controlled Highest density and electrical breakdown quality. Used for critical thin gate oxides.
Atomic Layer Deposition (ALD) Alternating chemical precursor pulses (Precursor A then Precursor B)
Lower Temp (200 – 400 °C)		 Very Slow (Atomic Layer by Layer) Perfect atomic conformality, pinhole-free high-\(\kappa\) dielectrics and gate stacks.
Crucial Physical Mechanism · Substrate Consumption vs Deposition

Thermal oxidation consumes silicon wafer material! The reaction \(\text{Si} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{SiO}_2\) incorporates silicon atoms from the substrate.

Because \(\text{SiO}_2\) occupies roughly twice the molar volume of silicon (\(\approx 2.2\times\)), the grown oxide expands both upwards and downwards:

  • ≈ 45% of the oxide thickness consumes the original silicon substrate downwards.
  • ≈ 55% of the oxide thickness projects upwards above the original wafer plane.

In contrast, ALD and chemical vapor deposition (CVD) are purely additive: they deposit layers atop the wafer without consuming the underlying silicon crystal!

3.2.3 Well and Channel Formation

A CMOS inverter requires both an nMOS (requires p-type body) and a pMOS (requires n-type body) on a single wafer. Four primary CMOS technologies evolved to solve this substrate sharing problem:

Visual breakdown of CMOS well technologies: p-well, n-well, twin-well, and triple-well architectures.

1. n-Well Process (Industry Mainstay)

• Starts with p-type silicon wafer.
• nMOS is built directly in the p-substrate.
• pMOS is fabricated inside an implanted/diffused n-well.
• Advantage: Excellent nMOS performance; easy retrofit to legacy nMOS fab lines.
• Limitation: All nMOS share the same substrate body, coupling digital switching noise into substrate.

2. p-Well Process (Historical Predecessor)

• Starts with n-type silicon wafer.
• pMOS is built in the n-substrate; nMOS inside a diffused p-well.
• Historically used to optimize pMOS performance, but superseded because nMOS electrons have 2-3× higher mobility than pMOS holes.

3. Twin-Well / Twin-Tub Process

• Starts with lightly doped substrate or epitaxial layer on wafer.
• Both separate n-wells and p-wells are formed.
• Major Advantage: Allows independent optimization of channel doping, carrier mobility, and threshold voltages (\(V_{Tn}\) and \(V_{Tp}\)) without compromising either device!

4. Triple-Well Process (Mixed-Signal Standard)

• Adds a third deep n-well beneath the p-well.
• Why It Exists: Provides complete electrical isolation between noisy digital logic blocks and sensitive analog/RF or dynamic RAM (DRAM) circuits.
• Manufacturing Efficiency: Upgrades a baseline twin-well line with just one single additional mask level!

5. BiCMOS Technology: Merging Bipolar & CMOS on Silicon

BiCMOS technology combines high-speed, high-transconductance Bipolar Junction Transistors (BJTs) with low-power, high-input-impedance CMOS logic on the same monolithic chip. CMOS excels at dense, zero-static-power logic, while npn bipolar transistors deliver enormous output current to charge heavy capacitive bus loads rapidly without requiring huge silicon area.

BiCMOS Physical Cross-Section

Figure: Physical cross-section of BiCMOS structure with heavily doped buried \(n^+\) subcollector layer, p-base diffusion, and poly emitter alongside standard CMOS n-well.

Bipolar NPN Mask Layout

Figure: Top-view geometric layout of integrated npn transistor showing emitter, base contacts, collector ring, and deep buried contact (BCCD) routing.

BODY BIASING · Substrate & Well Contacts (Taps)

To prevent forward-biasing the parasitic p-n junctions between substrate/wells and transistor source/drain regions:

  • The p-type substrate must be tied to the lowest potential: GND via a heavily doped \(p^+\) substrate tap.
  • The n-well must be tied to the highest potential: \(V_{DD}\) via a heavily doped \(n^+\) well tap.
Schottky Diode vs Ohmic Contact Physics

When metal touches lightly doped semiconductor, it forms a Schottky diode that conducts current in only one direction. But when metal contacts heavily doped silicon (\(n^+\) or \(p^+\)), the depletion barrier becomes so microscopically narrow that carriers quantum-tunnel straight through, forming a low-resistance, bidirectional Ohmic contact. That is why all source, drain, and body contacts require heavily doped diffusions!

p-Well Process Sequence

Figure: Step 1 — Initial deep p-well diffusion (4–5 μm) into lightly doped n-substrate.

Fabrication Sequence Cross-Sections

Figure: Successive masking steps — Gate oxidation, polysilicon patterning, \(p^+\) source/drain implant, and \(n^+\) source/drain implant.

Historical: p-Well Inverter Structure

Figure: Cross-section of p-well CMOS inverter with VDD and VSS connections.

Standard: n-Well Inverter Cross-Section

Figure: Cross-section of standard n-well inverter showing n-well, polysilicon gates, and metal routing.

3.11 CMOS Latch-up: Origin, Physics & Prevention (Weste & Harris §7.3.6)

THE EXAM LINE · DEFINITION (WESTE & HARRIS §7.3.6)

Latch-up is the turning-on of the parasitic pnpn (thyristor) path formed by the wells and substrate of a CMOS circuit, which shorts \(V_{DD}\) to GND, causing massive current flow and catastrophic chip meltdown.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE DANGEROUS INTERNAL PAIR

"Bhabo, amra inverter baniechi containing 1 nMOS ar 1 pMOS. Kintu semiconductor-er physical layers gulo automatically 2-to parasitic bipolar junction transistors (BJT) toiri kore fele: akta pnp transistor (p+ diff, n-well, p-sub) ar akta npn transistor (n+ diff, p-sub, n-well)! Ei duto transistor cross-coupled thake, forming a Silicon-Controlled Rectifier (SCR). Substrate-e transient current dhukle parasitic npn ON hoy → n-well theke current tene well voltage komie parasitic pnp ke ON kore → pnp abar substrate-e current pathiye npn ke aro beshi ON kore! Ei positive feedback loop-e VDD theke GND-te short circuit hoye chip ghole jete pare!"

1. Origin of Latch-up: The Parasitic SCR Model (Figure 7.12)

As shown in Figure 7.12(a), a standard bulk CMOS inverter inherently embeds two parasitic bipolar transistors interconnected with substrate resistance \(R_{\text{sub}}\) and well resistance \(R_{\text{well}}\):

Figure 7.12: (a) CMOS inverter cross-section showing parasitic bipolar transistors Q1 (pnp) and Q2 (npn); (b) Equivalent regenerative silicon-controlled rectifier (SCR) circuit between VDD and GND.

Parasitic PNP Transistor (\(Q_1\))

• Emitter: \(p^+\) source diffusion of pMOS (tied to \(V_{DD}\)).
• Base: \(n\)-well.
• Collector: \(p\)-type substrate.

Parasitic NPN Transistor (\(Q_2\))

• Emitter: \(n^+\) source diffusion of nMOS (tied to GND).
• Base: \(p\)-type substrate.
• Collector: \(n\)-well.

Five-Step Positive Feedback Trigger Sequence:
  1. Trigger Disturbance: A transient current spikes in the substrate (during chip power-up, ESD, or external I/O pad overshoot/undershoot).
  2. Substrate Voltage Rise: Current flowing through substrate resistance \(R_{\text{sub}}\) produces an Ohmic drop (\(V_{\text{sub}} = I_{\text{sub}} \cdot R_{\text{sub}}\)). When \(V_{\text{sub}} \ge 0.7\text{ V}\), the base-emitter junction of parasitic \(Q_2\) (npn) becomes forward-biased and turns ON.
  3. Well Voltage Drop: The collector of \(Q_2\) pulls current out of the \(n\)-well through well resistance \(R_{\text{well}}\), pulling down local well voltage (\(V_{\text{well}} < V_{DD} - 0.7\text{ V}\)), which forward-biases the base-emitter junction of \(Q_1\) (pnp) and turns it ON.
  4. Regenerative Reinforcement: The collector of \(Q_1\) supplies additional current directly into the substrate, further elevating \(V_{\text{sub}}\) and pumping more base current into \(Q_2\).
  5. Latching State (\(\beta_1 \cdot \beta_2 \ge 1\)): The loop gain exceeds unity. The thyristor locks into an unbreakable low-resistance ON state. A torrential current flows between \(V_{DD}\) and GND that persists even after the initial trigger is removed, stopping only when power is disconnected or the metal tracks melt!

2. How Latch-up is Prevented in Physical Layout

Because latchup requires \(V_{\text{sub}} \ge 0.7\text{ V}\) and \(V_{\text{well}} \le V_{DD} - 0.7\text{ V}\), prevention focuses on minimizing \(R_{\text{sub}}\) and \(R_{\text{well}}\) and collecting injected minority carriers before they reach parasitic bases.

A. Low Substrate Resistance

High-performance processes grow a thin, lightly doped epitaxial silicon layer on top of a heavily doped (\(p^+\)) substrate wafer. The heavily doped substrate provides a low-resistance path to ground, shunting transient substrate currents harmlessly.

B. Substrate & Well Tap Rules

• Place a substrate/well tap adjacent to every transistor source connected to \(V_{DD}\) or GND.
• Place at least one tap for every 5 to 10 transistors.
• All taps must connect directly to supply rails in metal (not poly).
• Cluster nMOS near GND and pMOS near \(V_{DD}\), avoiding checkerboard layouts.

3. Guard Rings for I/O Pads (Figure 7.13)

Input/Output pads are especially vulnerable to latchup because external cables experience inductive ringing, static discharge, and noise that pull voltages outside \(0\text{ to }V_{DD}\). Guard rings are continuous diffusions tied to supply rails that completely enclose sensitive transistors:

Figure 7.13(a): p+ Guard Ring (GND)

An \(n^+\) diffusion connected to an external pin can inject electrons into the substrate if voltage rings below GND (−0.7 V). A continuous \(p^+\) guard ring connected to GND collects these stray electrons before they trigger nearby circuits.

Figure 7.13(b): n+ Guard Ring (VDD)

A \(p^+\) diffusion connected to an external pin can inject holes into the n-well if voltage rings above \(V_{DD}\) (\(+0.7\text{ V}\)). A continuous \(n^+\) guard ring connected to \(V_{DD}\) collects these injected holes safely.

Processes and Conditions Immune to Latch-up

  • Silicon-On-Insulator (SOI): Completely immune to latchup! Transistors are built on an insulating buried oxide (\(SiO_2\)) layer, eliminating bulk wells and physical substrate continuity; no parasitic bipolar structures can exist.
  • Low Power Supply Voltages (\(V_{DD} < 1.4\text{--}2.0\text{ V}\)): In modern nanometer CMOS nodes operating at 1.0 V or below, the supply voltage is less than the sum of two forward-biased diode drops (\(V_{BE1} + V_{BE2} \approx 1.4\text{ V}\)). The positive feedback loop cannot sustain latchup.
EXAM MODEL ANSWERS · QUESTIONS 1X.43 & 1X.44

1X.43: What is CMOS latch-up? [5 Marks]
CMOS latch-up is the unwanted turning-on of parasitic pnp and npn bipolar transistors formed by the wells, substrate, and diffusion regions of a CMOS circuit. Together with substrate and well resistances (\(R_{\text{sub}}\) and \(R_{\text{well}}\)), these transistors form a cross-coupled Silicon-Controlled Rectifier (SCR). A transient current into the substrate or well forward-biases one transistor, which turns on the other, creating regenerative positive feedback (\(\beta_1 \cdot \beta_2 \ge 1\)) and establishing a low-resistance short-circuit between \(V_{DD}\) and GND that can destroy the chip.

1X.44: How can latch-up be prevented? [2 Marks]
1. Minimize substrate and well resistance (use epitaxial wafers with heavily doped substrate).
2. Place frequent substrate and well taps close to transistor sources (every 5–10 transistors) tied directly to supply rails with metal.
3. Surround vulnerable I/O pad diffusions with continuous \(p^+\) (tied to GND) or \(n^+\) (tied to \(V_{DD}\)) guard rings.
4. Use SOI technology or operating voltages below 1.4 V.

1.5.2 CMOS Inverter Fabrication: From Raw Wafer to Chip

Chips are amazingly inexpensive because all the transistors and wires can be printed like books using photolithography. Because an entire wafer full of chips is processed simultaneously at each step, chip cost is proportional to silicon area, not transistor count!

The 6 Hypothetical Inverter Masks
  1. n-Well Mask: Defines regions where Group V dopants enter to form n-wells.
  2. Polysilicon Mask: Patterns the gate electrodes and local interconnects.
  3. n+ Diffusion Mask: Defines nMOS source/drain regions and n-well contact taps.
  4. p+ Diffusion Mask: Defines pMOS source/drain regions and substrate contact taps.
  5. Contact Cut Mask: Opens contact windows through field oxide down to diffusion/poly.
  6. Metal Mask: Patterns aluminum/copper wires for \(V_{DD}\), GND, Input \(A\), and Output \(Y\).
Figure: Sequential mask processing flowchart for n-well CMOS fabrication.

The 8 Sequential Fabrication Steps

Step 1: Start with Bare Silicon Wafer

High-purity single-crystal p-type substrate wafer sliced from a grown boule.

Step 2: Form the n-Well

Wafer is oxidized in furnace (\(900-1200^\circ\text{C}\)) to grow protective \(\text{SiO}_2\). Organic photoresist is spun on, exposed through the n-well mask, and developed. Hydrofluoric acid (HF) etches oxide openings. Photoresist is stripped with piranha etch. Phosphorus dopants are introduced via diffusion/implantation. Lateral diffusion causes well to spread slightly wider than oxide opening. Oxide stripped with HF.

Step 3: Grow Thin Gate Oxide & Deposit Polysilicon

Ultra-thin gate oxide is grown in dry oxygen. Polysilicon is deposited via Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD) using silane gas (\(\text{SiH}_4\)), heavily doped to become conductive, patterned with photoresist and gate mask, and etched.

Step 4: Self-Aligned Gate Action (The Ruler Analogy)

The polysilicon gate physically blocks dopants from entering the silicon directly beneath it. Like placing a ruler on paper and drawing along both sides, the gate itself automatically aligns the inner edges of source and drain without requiring a separate precision mask alignment!

Step 5: LDD & Sidewall Spacer Formation

Light dose of dopants forms shallow Lightly Doped Drain (LDD) extensions. Insulating sidewall spacers are formed on the vertical sides of the gate to offset the subsequent heavy implant.

Step 6: Separate n+ and p+ Source/Drain Implantation

Patterned with n-diffusion mask: implant phosphorus/arsenic into nMOS active areas and n-well contact taps. Patterned with p-diffusion mask: implant boron into pMOS active areas and substrate contact taps. Two separate masks are required because nMOS and pMOS require opposing donor/acceptor dopants.

Step 7: Field Oxide & Contact Cuts

Thick insulating field oxide is grown over the entire wafer. Patterned with contact cut mask and etched down to diffusion regions and polysilicon gates.

Step 8: Metallization (Sputtering & Plasma Etch)

Aluminum is sputtered into vapor to coat the wafer and fill contact cuts. Patterned with metal mask and plasma-etched to leave the wiring network for \(V_{DD}\), GND, Input \(A\), and Output \(Y\).

Figure 1.38: CMOS Inverter Manufacturing Steps — (f) p-diffusion mask and boron implant for pMOS and substrate taps; (g) contact cut mask and oxide etch; (h) metal-1 deposition and patterning for VDD, GND, Input A, and Output Y.

The Semiconductor Construction Crew Mental Model 🏗️

Translating mysterious fab terminology into real-world physical site operations:

🚧 Field Oxide = The Border Fence

Keeps neighboring transistor plots isolated so they don't accidentally interact. Prevents stray wires from creating unintended parasitic channels.

📐 Photoresist Mask = The Construction Stencil

Decides which regions are protected from chemical attack and which regions receive processing.

🌱 Doping = Planting the Right Seeds

Plants donor (Group V) or acceptor (Group III) atoms directly into the silicon crystal lattice. It changes electrical properties without excavating physical trenches!

✂️ Etching = Removing Material

Hydrofluoric acid (HF) dissolves glass (\(\text{SiO}_2\)); piranha etch dissolves organic resist stencils.

The Steep Cliff vs Gentler Slope: Sidewall Spacer & LDD Physics ⛰️

The Problem · Hot-Carrier Effect & Peak Electric Field

Imagine a ball falling from a height. If it drops off a vertical cliff, all kinetic energy impacts abruptly in one spot. In an abrupt transistor drain junction, the full voltage drop (\(V_{DD}\)) occurs across a tiny fraction of a nanometer. This creates a colossal peak electric field (\(E = -\Delta V / \Delta x\)). Highly accelerated electrons ("hot carriers") smash into and permanently degrade the thin gate oxide!

Steep Edge (No LDD)

Voltage drops over a very short distance near the drain → creates an intense peak electric field → causes hot-carrier degradation and gate oxide breakdown over time.

Gentler Slope (With LDD)

Voltage drop is spread out over a longer physical distance through the lightly doped extension → dramatically reduces peak electric field!

Sidewall Spacer = Gate's Protective Collar / Distance Setter

The sidewall spacer is a tiny insulating collar deposited along the vertical sides of the gate. Its specific physical job is to offset the heavy Source/Drain implant farther away from the gate edge, keeping the lightly doped "gentle slope" intact between the channel and the heavily doped region!

Note: We are not changing the physical ground into a ramp; we are grading the electric potential profile through the silicon lattice!

Physical Distinction · LDD vs Schottky Diode

The closest physics mechanism behind LDD is electric field spreading via doping grading, NOT a Schottky diode. A Schottky diode is a metal–semiconductor junction governed by an energy barrier. LDD is purely semiconductor profile engineering.

THE EXAM MEMORY LINE · Sidewall Spacer

"A sidewall spacer is an insulating structure formed on the vertical sides of the gate to offset the heavy Source/Drain implant from the gate edge, leaving lightly doped extensions (LDD) near the channel to reduce peak electric field and prevent hot-carrier damage."

Device Isolation & Gate Metallurgy: LOCOS vs STI

From 660°C aluminium gate limits and self-aligned polysilicon to bird's beak encroachment and CMP shallow trench isolation. Sze & Lee p. 8, Weste & Harris §3.2.7.

Gate Metallurgy: The 660 °C Aluminium Bottleneck vs Self-Aligned Polysilicon

In early MOS manufacturing, metal gates were fabricated from pure Aluminium (Al). While conductive and abundant, aluminium introduced a fundamental manufacturing constraint that severely limited circuit speed and packing density until the invention of the self-aligned polysilicon gate.

Standard Exam Definition · Sze & Lee p. 8 / Weste & Harris §3.2.7

"Aluminium melts near 660 °C, so an Al gate can only be deposited after the source and drain have been diffused at high temperature. Its mask must then be aligned to diffusions that already exist, and to allow for misalignment the gate is drawn to overlap them. The overlap adds gate–drain and gate–source capacitance and wastes area.

Polysilicon survives the high-temperature steps, so it is deposited first and then used as the mask for the source/drain implant. The source and drain edges line up with the gate automatically — the gate is self-aligned — which removes the overlap and its capacitance."

1. The 660 °C Thermal Bottleneck

• Source and Drain formation requires high-temperature furnace diffusion or implant anneals at 900 – 1200 °C to activate dopants and repair crystal lattices.
• Aluminium melts near 660 °C (and forms destructive Al-Si eutectics at ~577 °C).
• The consequence: An Al gate could never be in place during Source/Drain diffusion. Source and Drain had to be formed first, and the Al gate patterned last.

2. Misalignment & Overlap Capacitance

• Photolithography steppers carry misalignment tolerances (\(\pm \Delta x\)).
• To ensure the gate completely spans between Source and Drain despite stepper shifts, the Al gate mask was drawn deliberately oversized, creating substantial lateral overlap.
• The penalty: Heavy parasitic gate-drain (\(C_{gd}\)) and gate-source (\(C_{gs}\)) overlap capacitances. Via the Miller effect (\(C_{in} \approx C_{gs} + C_{gd}(1 + |A_v|)\)), switching frequencies were severely degraded!

3. The Self-Aligned Polysilicon Solution

• Polycrystalline silicon (polysilicon) withstands temperatures well above 1000 °C.
• Polysilicon is deposited and etched first, defining the gate electrode atop the thin gate oxide.
• The polysilicon gate then acts as an impenetrable hard mask during heavy Source/Drain ion implantation.
• Source and Drain edges line up with the gate automatically and perfectly. Overlap capacitance drops to near zero!

Architectural Comparison: Al Gate vs Self-Aligned Polysilicon

Parameter / Feature Aluminium Metal Gate (Older) Polysilicon Gate (Self-Aligned)
Melting / Processing Limit Melts near 660 °C (Eutectic ~577 °C) Survives > 1000 °C high-temp anneals
Deposition Sequence Deposited after Source/Drain diffusion Deposited before Source/Drain implantation
Alignment Tolerance Must allow generous mask misalignment overlay Self-aligned: Gate electrode acts as the implant mask
Overlap Capacitance (\(C_{ov}\)) Large (\(C_{gd}\) and \(C_{gs}\)) → Severe Miller delay Negligible (Only sub-nanometer fringing/lateral scatter)
Silicon Area Footprint Wasted area due to large alignment safety margins Minimum allowable channel length (\(L\)) and area footprint

1. The Problem: Neighbouring Transistors Must Stay Separate

Imagine building millions of MOSFETs next to each other on a single silicon wafer. Both devices share the same monolithic substrate, but they must operate with absolute electrical independence.

Reason 1: Prevent Parasitic Channel Inversion

Metal and polysilicon wires carrying high potentials (\(V_{DD}\)) route over the field areas between devices. Without thick isolation, these wires act as parasitic gates, inverting the underlying substrate and forming unintended conductive channels between unrelated nodes.

Reason 2: Eliminate Crosstalk & S/D Leakage

Adjacent Source/Drain diffusion junctions must never conduct or capacitive-couple directly to one another through the silicon substrate. Physical isolation blocks leakage currents, latchup triggers, and cross-device interference.

2. LOCOS: When the Insulating Fence Spreads Sideways ("The Oxide Giant")

LOCOS (Local Oxidation of Silicon) was the dominant isolation architecture throughout the 1970s and 1980s. It uses thermal oxidation locally to grow thick field oxide (FOX) in isolation regions while protecting active transistor areas with an oxidation barrier.

The 3-Step LOCOS Workflow:

  1. Pad Oxide & Nitride Mask: A thin pad oxide (\(\sim 20\text{ nm}\)) is grown to relieve mechanical stress, followed by a deposited silicon nitride (\(\text{Si}_3\text{N}_4\)) layer. Nitride is an impermeable barrier to oxygen and water vapor.
  2. Lithography: Nitride is patterned and etched away over field isolation areas, leaving hard-mask caps strictly over intended active device regions.
  3. Thermal Oxidation: The wafer is subjected to high-temperature steam (\(\approx 1000\text{ °C}\)). Thick field oxide (\(0.5 – 1.0\ \mu\text{m}\)) grows in the exposed silicon.

LOCOS Cross-Section: Lateral Diffusion & Bird's Beak Formation

Figure: Cross-section of LOCOS isolation. Lateral diffusion of oxidizing species beneath the edges of the \(\text{Si}_3\text{N}_4\) mask produces the characteristic tapered Bird's Beak.
Verbatim Real-World Analogy · The Shrinking Plot

"Imagine you reserved a 100 m wide plot for a building, but the neighbouring boundary walls spread inward by 10 m on each side. Your usable plot is now only 80 m wide."

The silicon wafer faces the exact same physical crisis:
• Intended Active Area: The full silicon width originally patterned by the mask.
• After LOCOS Oxidation: Lateral oxide encroachment eats \(\sim 0.1 – 0.2\ \mu\text{m}\) into the active zone from each side.
• The Scaling Bottleneck: In sub-\(0.35\ \mu\text{m}\) technology nodes where transistors themselves are narrower than \(0.3\ \mu\text{m}\), the bird's beak swallows the active area entirely! This hard geometric limit forced the semiconductor industry to abandon LOCOS for STI.

1. STI: Dig First, Fill Afterwards (≈ 0.35 μm and Beyond)

Shallow Trench Isolation (STI) fundamentally overturned the LOCOS paradigm. Instead of letting the oxide giant grow outwards from the substrate silicon, STI etches a narrow trench into the silicon first, then fills that trench with deposited dielectric.

The Core Distinction:
• LOCOS: Creates isolation mainly by growing oxide out of the silicon (consuming silicon substrate and expanding sideways).
• STI: Creates isolation mainly by etching a defined trench and filling it additively, keeping the isolation boundary strictly vertical and narrow!

The 5-Step STI Manufacturing Flow

Step 1: Protect Active Areas

Grow thin thermal pad oxide (\(\text{SiO}_2\)) and deposit a hard masking layer of silicon nitride (\(\text{Si}_3\text{N}_4\)). Pattern with lithography to expose the isolation grid.

Step 2: Etch the Trench

Anisotropic dry reactive-ion etching (RIE) removes exposed silicon to carve steep, narrow trenches (\(\sim 0.25 – 0.5\ \mu\text{m}\) deep) with near-vertical sidewalls.

Step 3: Grow Liner Oxide

Thermally grow a very thin sacrificial liner oxide (\(\sim 10 – 20\text{ nm}\)) on trench sidewalls. This repairs crystal damage from dry etching and rounds trench bottom corners.

Step 4: Fill the Trench via CVD

Deposit thick \(\text{SiO}_2\) dielectric via Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD) (e.g. TEOS or High-Density Plasma CVD). Purely additive; fills the trench without consuming substrate silicon.

Step 5: Flatten Wafer via CMP

Use Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP) to polish away all overburden oxide above the trench. The \(\text{Si}_3\text{N}_4\) layer acts as a polish stop, producing a globally flat surface.

Crucial Distinction: Thermal Oxidation vs Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD)

Mechanism Physical Action on Silicon Role in STI Sequence
Thermal Oxidation Consumes Substrate: \(\text{Si} + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{SiO}_2\). Grows partly down into substrate (~45%) and partly upwards (~55%). Used for Step 3 (thin liner oxide) to heal dry-etch crystalline lattice defects and round sharp corners.
Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD) Additive Gas Phase: Precursor gases react atop the surface to deposit \(\text{SiO}_2\). Does NOT consume underlying silicon! Used for Step 4 (bulk trench fill) to rapidly fill high-aspect-ratio trenches without lateral swelling.

What is CMP, and Why Flatten the Wafer?

CMP (Chemical Mechanical Polishing) combines mechanical abrasion and chemical reactivity:

  • Mechanical Action: The wafer is held face down against a rapidly rotating polyurethane polishing pad under controlled downward pressure.
  • Chemical Action: A chemical polishing slurry containing nanoscale abrasive particles (silica/alumina) and chemical agents softens and hydrolyzes surface oxide bonds.
  • The Polish Stop: The underlying silicon nitride (\(\text{Si}_3\text{N}_4\)) is significantly harder and polishes much slower than \(\text{SiO}_2\), acting as an automatic barrier that stops polishing at the exact intended planar boundary.
The Road Network Analogy · Why Surface Bumps Are Fatal

"Imagine building a multi-lane highway or road network on uneven, hilly ground. One section is high, another has a deep rut, and another has a mound. The next layer laid over that surface will inherit and magnify every single height variation."

In modern sub-micron CMOS, chips feature 10 to 15 stacked layers of copper metal interconnects. Non-planar bumps cause two catastrophic fabrication failures:
1. Photolithography Depth of Focus (DOF): Advanced DUV/EUV projection optics have depth of focus under \(100\text{ nm}\). If the wafer surface has step heights, lithography patterns blur out of focus.
2. Metal Thinning & Bridging Shorts: Metal films deposited over unplanarized steps thin out over ridges (causing open circuits) or pool in valleys (causing short circuits). CMP planarization provides the flat foundation needed for multi-tier interconnect scaling.

Deep Isolation & Junction Breakdown Physics

A reverse-biased Source/Drain-to-well junction acts as a non-conductive potential barrier. However, if reverse voltage exceeds the junction breakdown voltage (\(V_{BR}\)), avalanche or Zener multiplication causes massive current conduction. Deep trench isolation structures establish physical dielectric boundaries that block lateral subsurface punchthrough, prevent CMOS latchup triggering, and ensure reliable reverse-bias breakdown isolation between closely packed adjacent transistors.

Master Summary: LOCOS vs Shallow Trench Isolation (STI)

Feature LOCOS (Historical) STI (Modern Standard)
Basic Method Thermally grow thick oxide locally Etch a trench, grow liner, fill with CVD oxide
Main Limitation Sideways oxide growth → The Bird's Beak Requires trench etching, CVD filling & CMP planarization
Transistor Packing Density Severely limited by lateral encroachment Narrow isolation enables maximum packing density
Post-Isolation Surface Raised, uneven oxide topography Flat planarized surface via CMP
Technology Milestone Dominant down to \(\sim 0.5\ \mu\text{m}\) Universal standard from \(\sim 0.35\ \mu\text{m}\) to sub-3nm
Substrate Si Consumption Large (Entire FOX volume consumes Si) Minimal (Only thin thermal liner; fill is deposited CVD)

🎯 Quick Recall Check

Test your retention of device isolation and CMP planarization with instant verification:

1. What is the "Bird's Beak" in LOCOS technology?
2. What is the fundamental architecture of Shallow Trench Isolation (STI)?
3. Why is Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP) necessary after filling an STI trench?

CMOS Physical Layout, Design Rules & Stick Diagrams

Mead-Conway scalable \(\lambda\)-rules, standard cell track routing (\(P = 8\lambda\)), stick diagrams, layer color codes, and Euler path graph optimization for area-minimized complex gates. Weste & Harris §1.5.3–1.5.5, Kang & Leblebici §2.4 & §7.4.

THE EXAM LINE · DESIGN RULES & SCALABLE LAYOUT (WESTE & HARRIS §1.5.3)

Design rules are the minimum permissible widths, spacings, and overlaps of each physical mask layer; \(\lambda\)-based rules express them all as integer multiples of a single parameter \(\lambda\), defined as half the minimum drawn transistor channel length, enabling effortless layout scaling across compatible fabrication processes.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · FROM ABSTRACT SCHEMATIC TO SILICON REALITY (ACHA BRO)

"Acha bro! A circuit schematic only tells us what connects to what in abstract theory. But a semiconductor foundry cannot manufacture an abstract diagram — it needs exact physical coordinates, layer widths, and spacings to carve into silicon! If a metal wire is drawn too narrow, it snaps during manufacturing, causing an open circuit. If two wires are drawn too close, process variations merge them together, causing a fatal short circuit. Design rules are the foundry's manufacturing speed limits that guarantee high yield while keeping silicon area compact!"

1. Purpose & Trade-offs of Layout Design Rules (Kang & Leblebici §2.4)

The physical mask layout of any integrated circuit must conform to geometric constraints known as Layout Design Rules. These rules specify minimum allowable line widths, minimum feature separations, and minimum layer overlaps:

The Economic Objective: Yield vs Area

The primary goal of design rules is to achieve high overall manufacturing yield and long-term reliability using the smallest possible silicon area. Yield is the ratio of fully functional chips to total manufactured chips on a wafer.

The Engineering Trade-off

• Conservative Rules: Wider lines and generous spacings increase defect tolerance and boost yield, but consume more silicon area, increasing per-die cost.
• Aggressive Rules: Maximize circuit packing density, but increase vulnerability to dust particles and photolithographic distortions.

Crucial Conceptual Qualification: Design rules do not represent strict physical boundaries separating "correct" circuits from "broken" ones. A layout violating a rule might still function, while one obeying every rule could fail due to random defects. Observing design rules is a probabilistic guarantee that drastically maximizes the likelihood of high-yield fabrication.

2. Micron Rules vs. Lambda (\(\lambda\))-Based Scalable Rules

Design Rule Style Definition & Characteristics Advantages & Limitations
Micron-Based Rules All layout constraints (line widths, spacings, contact sizes) are specified in absolute physical units (micrometers or nanometers). E.g., metal width = \(0.25\,\mu\text{m}\), spacing = \(0.30\,\mu\text{m}\). Pro: Squeezes maximum density out of a specific foundry process.
Con: Process migration is brutal; moving to a new node requires manually redrawing and resizing every geometry individually.
Lambda (\(\lambda\))-Based Rules Popularized by Mead & Conway. Expresses all dimensions as integer multiples of a single scaling parameter \(\lambda\): $$\mathbf{\lambda = \frac{\text{Minimum Drawn Feature Size}}{2} = \frac{L_{\min}}{2}}$$ Pro: Trivial scaling! Rescaling a layout to an advanced process only requires setting a new numerical value for \(\lambda\).
Con: Conservative; rounds up dimensions to integer multiples of \(\lambda\), slightly sacrificing density. Below 180 nm, non-linear physical effects make pure \(\lambda\)-scaling difficult.
WORKED EXAMPLE · LAMBDA CALCULATION (180 NM PROCESS)

For a \(180\text{ nm}\) CMOS process, the minimum drawn transistor channel length is \(L_{\min} = 180\text{ nm} = 0.18\,\mu\text{m}\).
$$\lambda = \frac{L_{\min}}{2} = \frac{0.18\,\mu\text{m}}{2} = \mathbf{0.09\,\mu\text{m} = 90\text{ nm}}$$
Therefore, a \(4\lambda\) metal wire has a physical width of \(4 \times 0.09\,\mu\text{m} = \mathbf{0.36\,\mu\text{m}}\).
Foundry Mask-Shrink Nuance: Some 180 nm processes set \(\lambda = 0.10\,\mu\text{m}\) during layout, and then shrink the gate by 20 nm while generating the final masks to retain a 180 nm physical gate length while giving wider margins for metal and diffusion!

Figure 1.3: Historical scaling curve of minimum lithographic linewidth (\(2\lambda\)) over time from 10μm down to deep submicron regimes.

3. The MOSIS Scalable Design Rules (Figure 1.39)

MOSIS (MOS Implementation System) is an industrial-academic prototyping service that aggregates multiple customer designs onto shared multi-project wafers (MPW) to spread mask costs. MOSIS established a conservative, universally recognized \(\lambda\)-rule set for n-well processes:

Figure 1.39: Summary of MOSIS scalable λ-based design rules for a two-metal layer n-well CMOS process showing layer widths, spacings, overlaps, and well clearances.
Layer / Feature Minimum Width Minimum Spacing Overlap / Enclosure Rule
Metal 1 & Diffusion \(4\lambda\) \(4\lambda\) Diffusion must be surrounded by \(1\lambda\) at contact cut
Polysilicon (Gate) \(2\lambda\) \(3\lambda\) (from poly or contact) Must overlap diffusion by \(\mathbf{2\lambda}\) where transistor is formed; space \(\mathbf{1\lambda}\) away where no transistor is intended.
Contact Cuts \(2\lambda \times 2\lambda\) \(2\lambda\) (from another contact) Must be enclosed by \(\mathbf{1\lambda}\) of metal/poly/diffusion above and below
N-Well — — Must surround pMOS transistors by \(\mathbf{6\lambda}\); must avoid nMOS transistors by \(\mathbf{6\lambda}\)
Metal 2 \(4\lambda\) \(4\lambda\) Via 1 is \(2\lambda \times 2\lambda\), surrounded by \(1\lambda\) on M1 and M2
Metal 3 (Top Level) \(6\lambda\) \(4\lambda\) Wider and thicker to reduce electrical resistance and electromigration
Why Must Polysilicon Overlap Diffusion by \(2\lambda\)? A MOSFET is formed where a polysilicon gate line crosses an active diffusion region. If the polysilicon ended exactly at the diffusion edge, optical misalignments during lithography could cause the gate to pull back inside the diffusion, creating a raw un-gated source-to-drain short circuit! The \(2\lambda\) gate extension ensures complete channel coverage under worst-case alignment tolerances.

4. Transistor Sizing & The \(W/L\) Ratio (Figure 1.40)

A MOSFET's electrical drive strength and current capability are governed by its Width-to-Length (\(W/L\)) ratio:

Figure 1.40: (a) Unit inverter layout showing unit nMOS (4/2) and double-sized pMOS (8/2); (b) Schematic with W/L annotations; (c) Shorthand notation showing multiples of unit width.

The Unit Transistor (\(W/L = 4/2\))

The minimum contacted transistor has width \(W = 4\lambda\) and length \(L = 2\lambda\). In a \(0.6\,\mu\text{m}\) process (\(\lambda = 0.3\,\mu\text{m}\)), this represents an actual physical width \(W = 1.2\,\mu\text{m}\) and length \(L = 0.6\,\mu\text{m}\). Digital designs universally choose minimum channel length \(L_{\min}\) for maximum switching speed, minimum silicon area, and lowest dynamic power.

Why pMOS is \(2\times\) to \(3\times\) Wider

In silicon inversion layers, holes have significantly lower carrier mobility than electrons (\(\mu_n \approx 2.5 \mu_p\)). To deliver symmetrical pull-up and pull-down currents (\(I_{\text{sat}} \propto \mu \frac{W}{L}\)) and balanced rise/fall switching delays, a unit inverter pairs a unit nMOS (\(W/L = 4/2\)) with a double-sized pMOS (\(W/L = 8/2\))!

Industrial Reliability Note: Extremely small unit transistors (\(4\lambda\) wide) have only a single source/drain contact. If a single particle defect damages that contact, the entire transistor fails! High-reliability industrial cell libraries often define the unit transistor wide enough to fit two contacts (\(9\lambda\) wide) for defect redundancy.
PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE FOUR-STRIP STANDARD CELL (ACHA BRO)

"To automate chip design, standard cells are organized exactly like modular bricks with four horizontal strips: Metal VDD supply rail on top → p-diffusion → n-diffusion → Metal GND supply rail at the bottom. When multiple gates are placed side by side, their power and ground rails automatically abut and snap together into continuous power grids! Polysilicon wires run vertically as transistor gates, and internal metal tracks connect the terminals."

1. Standard Cell "Line of Diffusion" Architecture (Figures 1.41 & 1.42)

Standard cell digital libraries use a highly modular 4-strip layout template:

Figures 1.41 & 1.42: (a) Inverter standard cell layout with vertical poly gate; (b) Inverter with well and substrate taps under supply rails; (c) 3-input NAND gate standard cell layout showing series nMOS and parallel pMOS.
Figure: CMOS Inverter Tri-View Architecture — (Top Left) Physical mask layout and dimensional alignment; (Top Right) 3D perspective wafer view showing oxide isolation and polysilicon gates; (Bottom) Cross-sectional layer stack from p-substrate through n-well to metal routing.

Cell Height & Abutment Geometry

Power and ground rails extend \(2\lambda\) beyond the boundary on each side. When two adjacent standard cells abut, their rails merge and their internal diffusions remain separated by exactly \(4\lambda\), satisfying design rules! The standard cell height is \(36\lambda\) (or \(40\lambda\) when counting the \(4\lambda\) inter-cell routing space above).

Substrate & Well Taps Under Rails

As shown in Figure 1.41(b), heavily doped \(p^+\) substrate taps and \(n^+\) well taps are placed directly underneath the GND and \(V_{DD}\) metal rails. This saves substantial area while ensuring all transistor bodies are clamped securely to the supplies to eliminate latchup.

2. Routing Tracks & The Track Pitch Formula (\(P = 8\lambda\)) (Figures 1.44 & 1.45)

While schematics focus on transistors, interconnect wires occupy the vast majority of physical layout area. Transistors are merely small widgets that fit underneath the metal routing grid!

Figures 1.44 & 1.45: (a) Routing track pitch P = 8λ (4λ wire + 4λ space); (b) Room for transistor between tracks; (c) 12λ well separation between nMOS and pMOS requiring an extra routing track.
Master Track Pitch Formula: $$\mathbf{P = \text{Wire Width} + \text{Inter-Wire Spacing} = 4\lambda + 4\lambda = 8\lambda}$$

A routing track is the repeating horizontal or vertical pitch required to route one metal wire safely alongside its neighbor. One \(8\lambda\) track also provides sufficient clearance for a contacted transistor to sit underneath!

The \(12\lambda\) Well Isolation Penalty: Design rules mandate at least \(12\lambda\) separation between nMOS and pMOS diffusions due to the n-well boundary (\(6\lambda\) enclosure + \(6\lambda\) avoidance). Because \(12\lambda > 8\lambda\), one extra routing track must always be allocated between nMOS and pMOS, even if no wire physically runs in that track!

3. Layout Area Estimation Using Track Counting (Figures 1.46 & 1.47)

Designers estimate layout dimensions by counting the number of vertical and horizontal metal routing tracks and multiplying by \(8\lambda\):

Three-Input NAND Area Estimation (Figure 1.46)

• Vertical Tracks: 4 tracks → \(\text{Width} = 4 \times 8\lambda = \mathbf{32\lambda}\).
• Horizontal Tracks: 5 tracks → \(\text{Height} = 5 \times 8\lambda = \mathbf{40\lambda}\).
• Total Cell Area: \(32\lambda \times 40\lambda = \mathbf{1280\lambda^2}\). (Matches actual layout dimensions exactly!).

Example 1.3: Compound AOI Gate (Figure 1.47)

For the compound gate \(Y = \overline{(A + B + C) \cdot D}\):
• Vertical Tracks: 5 tracks → \(\text{Width} = 5 \times 8\lambda = \mathbf{40\lambda}\).
• Horizontal Tracks: 6 tracks → \(\text{Height} = 6 \times 8\lambda = \mathbf{48\lambda}\).
• Estimated Cell Size: \(40\lambda \times 48\lambda = \mathbf{1920\lambda^2}\).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE ARCHITECTURAL FLOORPLAN (ACHA BRO)

"Before spending days drawing thousands of exact micron polygons in CAD, layout engineers sketch a Stick Diagram using colored markers! A stick diagram is a simplified, non-to-scale topological map. It captures the essential layer connectivity: where red poly crosses green/yellow diffusion to create transistors, where blue metal routes power, and where black X's mark contact cuts."

1. Stick Diagram vs. Geometric Mask Layout

Feature Stick Diagram Physical Mask Layout
Purpose Topological planning, transistor placement, and area estimation. Exact geometric data used to generate lithographic manufacturing masks.
Scale Not to scale. Lines represent wires of zero thickness. Strictly to scale. Every polygon has exact width, length, and coordinates.
Design Rules Captures layer overlaps and crossings, but ignores exact micron margins. Must satisfy 100% of the foundry design rules (DRC clean).
Speed Can be sketched on paper in 60 seconds. Requires complex CAD software and extensive DRC/LVS verification.

2. Inverter & NAND3 Stick Diagrams (Figure 1.43)

Figure 1.43: Stick diagrams for CMOS Inverter and 3-input NAND gate showing layer conventions, supply rails, poly gate crossings, and contact cuts.
Exam Model 1B.4 [5 Marks]: Step-by-step schematic to stick diagram translation of CMOS Inverter — blue metal rails (VDD/GND), orange/yellow p-diffusion, green n-diffusion, vertical red polysilicon gate, and black contact cuts.

3. Layer Color Codes: CMOS vs. Single-Poly nMOS Conventions

Color coding is a universal graphical convention used to distinguish physical semiconductor layers on stick diagrams and CAD monitors:

Weste & Harris CMOS Color Convention

n-Diffusion:Green
p-Diffusion:Yellow
Polysilicon:Red
Metal 1:Blue
Metal 2:Purple / Magenta
Contact Cut:Black (or X)

Mead-Conway Single-Poly nMOS Convention

Diffusion:Green
Polysilicon:Red
Metal:Blue
Depletion Implant:Yellow
Contact Cut:Black
Buried Contact:Brown
EXAM TRAP ALERT · THE COLOR "YELLOW" CONFLICT

Beware of this textbook conflict on exams:
In the Weste & Harris CMOS convention, Yellow designates p-diffusion.
In the Mead & Conway single-poly nMOS convention, Yellow designates the Depletion Implant (since there is no p-diffusion in pure nMOS technology)! Always verify which process convention your exam question specifies.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · AVOID THE DIFFUSION BREAK TRAP (ACHA BRO)

"When building complex CMOS logic gates, the order in which you arrange the vertical polysilicon gate columns makes or breaks your layout area! If you choose an arbitrary gate order, the source and drain nodes don't line up. You are forced to chop the diffusion strip into pieces, leaving gaps for diffusion-to-diffusion clearance and double metal contacts. Graph theory comes to the rescue: an Euler Path traverses every transistor edge exactly once. Finding a shared Euler path for both pull-down and pull-up networks lets us build the entire gate using single, continuous, unbroken diffusion strips!"

1. The Diffusion Break Problem: Arbitrary Poly Ordering (Figure 7.20)

In a complex gate, if polysilicon columns are placed arbitrarily, transistor terminals do not align sequentially. The diffusion strip must be broken, requiring additional silicon area to satisfy diffusion-to-diffusion spacing (\(4\lambda\)) and fit two metal-to-diffusion contacts (\(2 \times 2\lambda\) plus enclosures):

Arbitrary Gate Ordering (Figure 7.20)

• Multiple breaks in both p-diffusion and n-diffusion strips.
• Large column-to-column separation \(\Delta d\) required for isolation and extra contact cuts.
• Bloated layout area, tangled metal routing, and excessive parasitic capacitances!

Euler-Path Optimized Ordering (Figure 7.22)

• Zero diffusion breaks! Completely uninterrupted p-type and n-type diffusion strips.
• Separation \(\Delta d\) needs room for only one shared metal-to-diffusion contact.
• Drastically smaller layout area, straightforward signal routing, and minimum parasitics.

2. Constructing the Dual Pull-Up Graph (Figure 7.18)

Every static CMOS gate consists of complementary pull-down (nMOS) and pull-up (pMOS) networks. The pull-up graph is constructed as the mathematical dual of the pull-down graph:

Figure 7.18: Step-by-step construction of the dual pull-up (pMOS) graph from the pull-down (nMOS) graph using the dual-graph topological method.

The 4-Step Dual Graph Construction Algorithm

  1. Draw Pull-Down Graph (PDN): Represent each nMOS transistor as a labeled edge, and represent each electrical circuit node (GND, internal nodes, Output \(Y\)) as a vertex.
  2. Identify Faces: Identify each enclosed region (loop) in the pull-down graph, plus one external vertex representing the outer region.
  3. Place Dual Vertices: Place a new dual vertex inside each enclosed region, and one outside (corresponding to \(V_{DD}\) and internal pMOS nodes).
  4. Draw Dual Edges: Connect adjacent dual vertices with edges that cross each corresponding pull-down edge exactly once. This new graph represents the Pull-Up Network (PUN)!

3. What is an Euler Path? Common Path Sequence (Figure 7.19 & 7.21)

EXAM DEFINITION · EULER PATH

An Euler Path is an uninterrupted path through a graph that traverses every edge (branch) exactly once. Vertices (nodes) may be revisited, but no edge may be repeated.

Figures 7.19 & 7.21: (a) Complex CMOS logic gate realizing \(f\); (b) Finding the common Euler path E-D-A-B-C in both pull-down and pull-up graphs; (c) Optimized stick diagram layout with uninterrupted diffusion.
$$\mathbf{\text{Common Euler Path Sequence: } E - D - A - B - C}$$

In both PDN and PUN graphs, the path starts at node \((x)\) and terminates at node \((y)\). Arranging polysilicon gate columns in this identical sequence enables 100% continuous diffusion!

4. Full-CMOS XOR Implementation (12 Transistors)

The exclusive-OR (XOR) function is \(A \oplus B = \overline{A}B + A\overline{B}\). To implement this in static complementary CMOS:

Transistor Count Breakdown

• Core complex gate: 4 nMOS in PDN + 4 pMOS in PUN = 8 transistors.
• 2 input inverters to generate inverted literals \(\overline{A}\) and \(\overline{B}\) = 4 transistors.
• Total: 12 transistors. (Transmission gate implementations can reduce this to 4–6 transistors).

Limitation of the Euler Method

Not every complex gate has a common Euler path covering both networks. When a common path does not exist, a completely uninterrupted diffusion strip is mathematically impossible, and at least one diffusion break must be included.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · 5-STEP EXAM DERIVATION WORKFLOW (ACHA BRO)

"Exam-e stick diagram astei amra ei 5-step master recipe follow korbo: 1. Read Boolean function → 2. Build Pull-Down Network (nMOS: AND mane series, OR mane parallel) → 3. Build Pull-Up Network (pMOS: dual opposite arrangement) → 4. Find common Euler path sequence → 5. Draw top VDD, bottom GND, continuous diffusion strips, and cross them with vertical poly gates!"

1. Fundamental Standard Gates: Inverter, NAND2, NOR2

Gate & Function Pull-Down Network (nMOS) Pull-Up Network (pMOS) Stick Diagram Features
Inverter
\(Y = \overline{A}\)		 Single nMOS connected between Output \(Y\) and GND. Single pMOS connected between \(V_{DD}\) and Output \(Y\). One vertical poly line \(A\) crosses both diffusion strips. Output metal joins drains.
2-Input NAND
\(Y = \overline{A \cdot B}\)		 Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in series between Output \(Y\) and GND. Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in parallel between \(V_{DD}\) and Output \(Y\). Output LOW only when both \(A=1\) AND \(B=1\). Parallel pMOS sources tied to \(V_{DD}\).
2-Input NOR
\(Y = \overline{A + B}\)		 Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in parallel between Output \(Y\) and GND. Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in series between \(V_{DD}\) and Output \(Y\). Output HIGH only when both \(A=0\) AND \(B=0\). Series pMOS stack causes weaker pull-up.

2. Worked Exam Gates: Euler Path & Node Tracing

Gate 1: \(f = (A \cdot B + C)'\) · [Exam Question 1X.32, 5 Marks]

• Pull-Down Network: \((A \text{ in series with } B)\) in parallel with \(C\).
• Pull-Up Network: \((A \text{ in parallel with } B)\) in series with \(C\).
• Common Euler Path: C — A — B
• PDN Node Path: \(\text{GND} \xrightarrow{C} Y \xrightarrow{A} n_1 \xrightarrow{B} \text{GND}\).
• PUN Node Path: \(V_{DD} \xrightarrow{C} p_1 \xrightarrow{A} Y \xrightarrow{B} p_1\).
Layout Tip: Both ends of the pull-up diffusion strip terminate at node \(p_1\), so they are strapped together in metal!

Figure: Full Worked Infographic for 1X.32 — CMOS schematic, pull-up/pull-down dual graphs, common Euler path (C-A-B), complete colored stick diagram, and uncompacted silicon layout.

Gate 2: \(g = (a \cdot b \cdot c + d)'\) · [Exam Question 1X.49, 5 Marks]

• Pull-Down Network: \(a, b, c\) in series, the entire branch in parallel with \(d\).
• Pull-Up Network: \(a, b, c\) in parallel, the entire bank in series with \(d\).
• Euler Path Derivation: The pull-down graph is a single loop (\(Y \rightarrow n_1 \rightarrow n_2 \rightarrow \text{GND} \rightarrow Y\)). Any ordering around this loop is an Euler path.
• Common Euler Path: d — a — b — c
• PDN Node Path: \(\text{GND} \xrightarrow{d} Y \xrightarrow{a} n_1 \xrightarrow{b} n_2 \xrightarrow{c} \text{GND}\).
• PUN Node Path: \(V_{DD} \xrightarrow{d} p_1 \xrightarrow{a} Y \xrightarrow{b} p_1 \xrightarrow{c} Y\).

Gate 3: \(f = (a \cdot b + c + d)'\) · [Exam Question 1X.50, 5 Marks]

• Pull-Down Network: \((a \text{ in series with } b)\) in parallel with \(c\) and in parallel with \(d\).
• Pull-Up Network: \((a \text{ in parallel with } b)\) in series with \(c\) and in series with \(d\).
• Common Euler Path: c — d — a — b
• PDN Node Path: \(Y \xrightarrow{c} \text{GND} \xrightarrow{d} Y \xrightarrow{a} n_1 \xrightarrow{b} \text{GND}\).
• PUN Node Path: \(V_{DD} \xrightarrow{c} p_2 \xrightarrow{d} p_1 \xrightarrow{a} Y \xrightarrow{b} p_1\).

Gate 4: 2-Input NOR Gate · [Exam Question 1C.3, 10 Marks]

• Pull-Down Network: Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in parallel between \(Y\) and GND.
• Pull-Up Network: Transistors \(A\) and \(B\) in series between \(V_{DD}\) and \(Y\).
• Transistor Sizing: \(W_p / W_n \approx 4\) to compensate for 2-in-series pMOS stack (\(\mu_n \approx 2\text{--}3\mu_p\)).
• Geometric Mask Layout & Design Rules: Drawn for \(0.18\,\mu\text{m}\) process with \(1.80\,\mu\text{m}\) cell width, \(4\lambda\) metal rails, \(2\lambda\) poly lines, and \(1\lambda\) contact enclosure rules.

Figure: 10-Mark Model Solution for 1C.3 — (a) Transistor circuit schematic with sizing ratios; (b) Colored stick diagram; (c) Geometric mask layout with full design-rule dimensions in 0.18μm technology.
