Part One · Module 1 Core

MOS Characteristics, Capacitance Models & Non-Ideal Effects

Complete I-V conduction regimes, charge-based gate and junction capacitance models, mobility degradation, velocity saturation, channel length modulation (CLM), charge sharing, subthreshold leakage, high-field breakdown, and sheet resistance. Weste & Harris §2.3–2.4 & §6.2, Kang & Leblebici §3.5, Sze & Lee §2.3–3.6.

\( I_{DS}-V_{DS} \) Characteristics of MOS Transistors

The graphs below illustrate the output \( I_D \) versus \( V_{DS} \) characteristic families for both depletion-mode and enhancement-mode nMOS transistors across multiple gate-source bias voltages (\( V_{GS} \)):

(a) Depletion-Mode nMOS Transistor

Figure: Depletion-mode nMOS conducts at \(V_{GS} = 0\) due to implanted channel; requires negative \(V_{GS} < V_{td}\) to pinch off.

(b) Enhancement-Mode nMOS Transistor

Figure: Enhancement-mode nMOS is OFF at \(V_{GS} = 0\); requires positive gate bias \(V_{GS} > V_t\) to invert channel and conduct.

The Three Fundamental Operating Regimes

1. Cutoff Region (\(V_{gs} < V_t\))

The gate voltage is insufficient to invert the silicon surface beneath the gate oxide. In the classical long-channel model, \(I_{ds} = 0\). In real nanometer transistors, weak inversion produces exponential subthreshold leakage.

2. Linear / Ohmic Region (\(V_{ds} < V_{gs} - V_t\))

An continuous inversion channel extends from source to drain. The channel behaves as a voltage-controlled resistor: current increases almost linearly with \(V_{ds}\) before parabolic bending: $$I_{ds} = \beta \left( (V_{gs} - V_t)V_{ds} - \frac{V_{ds}^2}{2} \right)$$

3. Saturation Region (\(V_{ds} \ge V_{gs} - V_t\))

The channel pinches off at the drain end (\(V_{gd} \le V_t\)). Carriers are swept across the drain depletion electric field; \(I_{ds}\) becomes independent of \(V_{ds}\) and the transistor behaves as a constant current source: $$I_{dsat} = \frac{\beta}{2} (V_{gs} - V_t)^2$$

PINCH-OFF ONSET · \(V_{DSAT}\)

Figure: Channel profiles (a) Uniform channel at \(V_{ds}=0\), (b) Linear regime channel tapering at \(V_{ds} < V_{gs}-V_t\), (c) Channel pinch-off point at drain when \(V_{ds} \ge V_{gs}-V_t\).

The boundary separating the linear and saturation regions occurs at the channel pinch-off voltage: $$\mathbf{V_{DSAT} = V_{gs} - V_t = V_{GT}}$$ \(V_{DSAT}\) marks the exact point where the local gate-to-channel inversion potential at the drain edge drops to zero: $$V_{channel}(L) = V_d - V_s = V_{ds}$$ $$V_{ox}(L) = V_{gs} - V(L) = V_t$$ Beyond this point (\(V_{ds} > V_{dsat}\)), the inverted channel no longer touches the drain diffusion; any further increase in \(V_{ds}\) drops across the pinched-off depletion region, and electrons are swept across the high electric field into the drain.

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE SHIFTING BOTTOM PLATE (ACHA BRO)

"In high school physics, a capacitor has two fixed metal plates. In a MOSFET, the top plate is fixed polysilicon/metal gate, but the bottom plate is ALIVE! In cutoff, the bottom plate is the silicon substrate body. In linear, the bottom plate turns into a conducting inversion channel split between source and drain. In saturation, pinch-off snaps the drain connection, and the bottom plate belongs 100% to the source! That is why capacitance changes dramatically with bias voltages!"

1. Detailed MOS Gate Capacitance Model (Weste & Harris §2.3.2, Question 1C.6 & 1X.26)

The gate capacitance consists of two components: the intrinsic channel capacitance \(C_{gc}\) (over the active channel) and the overlap capacitances \(C_{gol}\) (fringing and physical overlap with source and drain diffusions).

Nominal parallel-plate gate oxide capacitance is:

$$C_0 = W \cdot L \cdot C_{ox} = W \cdot L \cdot \frac{\varepsilon_{ox}}{t_{ox}}$$

The intrinsic capacitance is split across three physical terminals connected to the bottom plate: \(C_{gb}\) (gate-to-body), \(C_{gs}\) (gate-to-source), and \(C_{gd}\) (gate-to-drain). Their distribution changes across the three operating regimes:

Operating Regime Gate-to-Body (\(C_{gb}\)) Gate-to-Source (\(C_{gs}\)) Gate-to-Drain (\(C_{gd}\)) Total Gate Capacitance (\(C_g\))
1. Cutoff (\(V_{gs} < V_t\)) \(\mathbf{C_0}\) (Accumulation) → drops in depletion \(0\) \(0\) \(\mathbf{C_0}\)
2. Linear (\(V_{ds} < V_{dsat}\)) \(0\) (Channel shields body) \(\mathbf{C_0 / 2}\) \(\mathbf{C_0 / 2}\) \(\mathbf{C_0}\)
3. Saturation (\(V_{ds} \ge V_{dsat}\)) \(0\) \(\mathbf{\frac{2}{3} C_0}\) \(0\) (Pinch-off isolates drain) \(\mathbf{\frac{2}{3} C_0}\)
QUESTION 1X.26 [5 MARKS] · VARIATION OF \(C_{gs}\) WITH \(V_{gs}\)

How does gate-source capacitance vary with gate-source voltage?
• For \(V_{gs} < V_t\) (Cutoff): No inversion channel exists; gate charge couples purely to substrate bulk → \(\mathbf{C_{gs} = 0}\).
• At \(V_{gs} = V_t\) (Inversion Threshold): The channel abruptly forms, shielding the substrate. In the linear region with small \(V_{ds}\), channel charge is shared equally between terminals → \(\mathbf{C_{gs} = C_0 / 2}\).
• In Saturation (\(V_{ds} > V_{dsat}\)): The pinch-off region isolates the drain completely (\(C_{gd} \to 0\)). Due to the tapered inversion layer charge profile, all intrinsic gate capacitance couples to the source, settling at \(\mathbf{C_{gs} = \frac{2}{3} C_0}\)!

Overlap & Fringing Capacitances (\(C_{gol}\))

In actual self-aligned fabrication, lateral dopant diffusion causes the source and drain pockets to extend under the polysilicon gate by distance \(L_{ol}\). This creates voltage-independent overlap capacitances proportional to transistor width \(W\):

$$C_{gsol(\text{overlap})} = C_{gsol} \cdot W, \quad C_{gdol(\text{overlap})} = C_{gdol} \cdot W \quad (2.15)$$

Typical values are \(C_{gsol} = C_{gdol} \approx 0.2\text{ to } 0.4\text{ fF}/\mu\text{m}\). Total effective gate capacitance for delay modeling is: $$C_g = C_{gs} + C_{gd} + C_{gb} + 2 C_{gol} W = C_0 + 2 C_{gol} W$$

2. Detailed MOS Diffusion Capacitance Model (§2.3.3)

The reverse-biased p-n junction between source/drain diffusion and substrate/well forms a parasitic depletion capacitor. It consists of two geometric components:

  • Bottom Junction Area (\(AS\)): Area \(AS = W \times D\), where \(D\) is diffusion length.
  • Sidewall Perimeter (\(PS\)): Perimeter \(PS = 2W + 2D\). Length \(W\) abuts the channel; the remaining \(W + 2D\) abuts field oxide or Shallow Trench Isolation (STI).

Total parasitic source-body diffusion capacitance:

$$\mathbf{C_{sb} = AS \times C_{jbs} + PS \times C_{jbssw}} \tag{2.16}$$

Voltage Dependence of Junction Capacitance:

$$C_{jbs} = C_j \left( 1 + \frac{V_{sb}}{\psi_0} \right)^{-M_j} \tag{2.17}$$ $$C_{jbssw} = C_{jSW} \left( 1 + \frac{V_{sb}}{\psi_{SW}} \right)^{-M_{jsw}} \tag{2.19}$$
  • \(C_j\): Zero-bias area junction capacitance per unit area (\(\text{fF}/\mu\text{m}^2\)).
  • \(M_j, M_{jsw}\): Junction grading coefficients (\(0.5\) for abrupt junction, \(0.33\) for linearly graded junction).
  • \(\psi_0\): Built-in contact potential: $$\psi_0 = v_T \ln \left( \frac{N_A N_D}{n_i^2} \right) \tag{2.18}$$ where \(v_T = kT/q = 26\text{ mV}\) at 300 K, \(n_i = 1.45 \times 10^{10}\text{ cm}^{-3}\).
PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE ALL-NIGHTER COFFEE CART RUN (ACHA BRO)

"Weste & Harris give the ultimate analogy: Imagine you've pulled an all-nighter in the lab and run across the courtyard to the coffee cart! How many hours you've been awake is the LATERAL field (drives you forward). But a brutal crosswind is blowing — that's the VERTICAL gate field slamming you into the wall (mobility degradation)! And right then, a lecture hall empties with confused freshmen you collide with (optical phonons). No matter how desperate you are for coffee, your top speed is strictly capped! In short-channel MOSFETs, carriers hit a hard ceiling: Velocity Saturation (\(v_{sat} \approx 10^7\text{ cm/s}\))!"

1. Mobility Degradation & Velocity Saturation (Weste & Harris §2.4.1)

In long-channel devices, carrier drift velocity is proportional to electric field: \(v = \mu E\). In sub-micron transistors, two severe high-field effects destroy this assumption:

Mobility Degradation (Vertical Field)

High gate voltage attracts carriers tightly to the \(\text{Si–SiO}_2\) interface, causing surface roughness scattering that reduces effective mobility \(\mu_{eff}\): $$\mu_{eff\text{-}n} = \frac{540}{1 + \left(\frac{V_{gs} + V_t}{0.54 \cdot t_{ox}}\right)^{1.85}} \tag{2.23}$$ In fully-ON 65nm nMOS (\(V_{gs}=1.0\text{V}, t_{ox}=1.05\text{nm}\)), mobility plummets from \(540\) to \(96\text{ cm}^2/\text{V}\cdot\text{s}\)! (Example 2.3).

Velocity Saturation (Lateral Field)

At high lateral fields (\(E > E_c\)), carriers collide frequently with optical phonons and reach a maximum velocity: $$v_{sat} \approx 10^7\text{ cm/s (electrons)}, \quad 8 \times 10^6\text{ cm/s (holes)}$$ Critical electric field: $$E_c = \frac{2 v_{sat}}{\mu_{eff}}, \quad V_c = E_c L \tag{2.25}$$ In 65nm nMOS, \(V_{c\text{-}n} = \mathbf{1.04\text{ V}}\) → heavily velocity-saturated at normal \(V_{DD}\)! (Example 2.4).

2. Modified I-V Equations & The Linear Saturation Regime (Eqs. 2.26–2.29)

Accounting for velocity saturation, the saturation voltage compresses below the long-channel value:

$$V_{dsat} = \frac{V_{GT} \cdot V_c}{V_{GT} + V_c} = V_{GT} \parallel V_c \tag{2.27}$$

Substituting into current equations yields the modified saturation current:

$$I_{dsat} = W C_{ox} v_{sat} \frac{V_{GT}^2}{V_{GT} + V_c} \tag{2.28}$$
  • Long-Channel Limit (\(V_{GT} \ll V_c\)): \(I_{dsat} \propto V_{GT}^2\) — classical quadratic dependence.
  • Velocity-Saturated Limit (\(V_{GT} \gg V_c\)): \(I_{dsat} \approx \mathbf{W C_{ox} v_{sat} V_{GT}}\) — LINEAR dependence on gate overdrive!
  • \(\alpha\)-Power Law Model (Sakurai90 Eq. 2.30): \(I_{dsat} \propto V_{GT}^\alpha\) where \(\alpha\) drops from 2 (long-channel) down to \(1.3\text{--}1.0\) (deep velocity saturation).

3. Channel Length Modulation (CLM) (§2.4.2)

In saturation, the pinch-off point moves away from the drain toward the source as \(V_{ds}\) increases. The depletion layer width \(L_d\) shortens the effective channel length:

$$L_{eff} = L - L_d \tag{2.33}$$

Because \(I_{ds} \propto 1/L_{eff}\), current increases with \(V_{ds}\) in saturation instead of remaining perfectly flat. Modeled using the Early Voltage \(V_A\):

$$\mathbf{I_{ds} = \frac{\beta}{2} V_{GT}^2 \left( 1 + \frac{V_{ds}}{V_A} \right)} \tag{2.34}$$
Analog vs Digital Impact: CLM degrades the output resistance \(r_o = V_A / I_D\), severely reducing the open-circuit voltage gain of analog amplifiers (\(A_v = -g_m r_o\)). In digital logic, it causes a slight increase in saturation drive current.

4. Drain-Induced Barrier Lowering (DIBL) & Subthreshold Conduction (§2.4.3.2 & §2.4.4.1)

In short-channel devices, high drain voltage creates an electric field that penetrates deep into the channel, lowering the source-channel potential barrier even when \(V_{gs} = 0\):

$$\mathbf{V_t = V_{t0} - \eta V_{ds}} \tag{2.41}$$

where \(\eta\) is the DIBL coefficient (typically \(0.05\text{ to } 0.15\text{ V/V} = 100\text{ mV/V}\)). DIBL causes threshold voltage to drop as drain voltage rises!

Subthreshold Leakage Formula:

$$I_{ds} = I_{off} \cdot 10^{\frac{V_{gs} + \eta(V_{ds} - V_{DD}) - k_\gamma V_{sb}}{S}} \left( 1 - e^{-V_{ds}/v_T} \right) \tag{2.45}$$

The subthreshold slope \(S\) is typically \(\mathbf{100\text{ mV/decade}}\) at room temperature: $$S = \left[ \frac{d(\log_{10} I_{ds})}{dV_{gs}} \right]^{-1} = n v_T \ln 10 \tag{2.44}$$ This means gate voltage must drop by 100 mV to decrease leakage by 10× (one order of magnitude).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · CHARGE ROBBERY AT SHORT CHANNELS (ACHA BRO)

"In a long-channel transistor, the gate is the king: it pays for 100% of the depletion charge underneath it to turn the transistor ON. But when the channel is shrunk down to nanometers, the source and drain diffusion pockets step in and 'share' the burden by creating their own massive depletion zones. The gate now only needs to support a small trapezoid of charge! Because the gate supports less charge, the threshold voltage drops — this is \(V_t\) roll-off by charge sharing!"

1. Short-Channel MOSFET Definition (Kang & Leblebici §3.5)

DEFINITION · SHORT-CHANNEL DEVICE

"A MOSFET is classified as a short-channel device if its channel length \(L\) is on the same order of magnitude as the depletion region thicknesses of the source and drain junctions, or if effective channel length \(L_{eff}\) is approximately equal to junction depth \(x_j\)."

2. Threshold Reduction by Charge Sharing (Kang Fig. 3.25, Question 1X.24)

In long-channel transistors, the gate-induced depletion region is modeled as a simple rectangle of length \(L\). In short-channel transistors, the depletion regions of the source and drain junctions extend significantly into the channel space:

  • A large portion of the total bulk charge under the gate is supported by the built-in fields of the source and drain p-n junctions rather than the gate voltage!
  • The gate-induced charge shrinks from a rectangle to an asymmetric trapezoid of average length \(\left(1 - \frac{\Delta L_S + \Delta L_D}{2L}\right) \cdot L\).

Bulk Depletion Charge Formula (Kang Eq. 3.80):

$$\mathbf{Q_{B0} = -\left(1 - \frac{\Delta L_S + \Delta L_D}{2L}\right) \cdot \sqrt{2 q \varepsilon_{Si} N_A |2\phi_F|}} \tag{3.80}$$

Since the gate must support less bulk charge, the zero-bias threshold voltage is reduced:

$$\mathbf{V_{T0}(\text{short channel}) = V_{T0} - \Delta V_{T0}} \tag{3.79}$$

where \(\Delta V_{T0}\) is the threshold voltage shift (roll-off) caused by charge sharing.

3. Punch-Through & Channel-Length Modulation (Kang p. 96, Question 1X.23)

EXAM MASTER JUSTIFICATION · QUESTION 1X.23 [5 MARKS]

"Punch-through is an extreme case of channel-length modulation" — Justify.

Justification:
1. In Channel-Length Modulation (CLM), applying drain voltage \(V_{DS} > V_{dsat}\) causes the drain depletion region to expand into the channel by length \(L_d\), shortening the effective channel length to \(L_{eff} = L - L_d\). The gate still maintains control over the remaining channel.
2. In a short-channel device, as \(V_{DS}\) increases to large values, the drain depletion region widens aggressively toward the source.
3. When the drain depletion edge physically touches and merges with the source depletion region, the effective channel length becomes zero: $$\mathbf{L_{eff} = L - L_d \longrightarrow 0}$$ 4. At this point, the potential barrier at the source junction is completely obliterated by the drain field. Space-charge-limited current surges directly through the bulk substrate from source to drain independent of the gate voltage! The gate loses complete electrostatic control.
5. Therefore, punch-through is physically and mathematically the limiting extreme case of channel-length modulation, where the depletion region extends across the entire physical length of the device (\(L_d = L\)).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · DIELECTRIC LIMITS & AVALANCHE (ACHA BRO)

"A transistor can only take so much voltage before the silicon crystal snaps! If you reverse-bias the drain junction too hard, you get quantum tunneling (Zener) or an explosive chain-reaction of electron impacts (Avalanche). If you push the gate too hard, lightning bolts punch through the nanometer gate glass (Oxide Breakdown). And if punch-through connects source to drain in the bulk, the gate becomes a useless spectator!"

1. Junction Breakdown Mechanisms (Sze & Lee §3.6)

When a large reverse bias is applied to a semiconductor p-n junction, two distinct physical mechanisms produce sudden high conduction:

1. Band-to-Band Tunneling (Zener Effect)

• Voltage Regime: Dominates for breakdown voltages \(V_B < 4 E_g/q\) (\(< 4.5\text{ V}\) in silicon).
• Physics: Requires heavy doping on both sides (\(> 5 \times 10^{17}\text{ cm}^{-3}\)) creating an ultra-narrow depletion barrier (\(< 10\text{ nm}\)).
• Electric field exceeds \(10^6\text{ V/cm}\); valence electrons quantum-tunnel directly through the forbidden bandgap into the conduction band.

2. Avalanche Multiplication

• Voltage Regime: Dominates for breakdown voltages \(V_B > 6 E_g/q\) (\(> 7\text{ V}\) in silicon) with moderate doping (\(N \le 10^{17}\text{ cm}^{-3}\)).
• Physics — Impact Ionization: Thermally generated carriers gain kinetic energy from the high field (\(> 2\times 10^5\text{ V/cm}\)). On colliding with lattice atoms, they shatter covalent bonds, creating new electron-hole pairs.
• Multiplication Factor: \(M_n = \frac{I_n(W)}{I_{n0}}\). At breakdown, \(M_n \to \infty\).

2. Four Voltage Breakdown Mechanisms in MOSFETs (Question 1X.25, 5 Marks)

EXAM MASTER SUMMARY · QUESTION 1X.25 [5 MARKS]

Four distinct voltage breakdown mechanisms limit the maximum operating voltage of a MOSFET:

  1. Drain-Substrate Junction Avalanche Breakdown: High \(V_{DS}\) across the reverse-biased drain-to-body p-n junction causes avalanche multiplication, generating large electron-hole currents.
  2. Source-to-Drain Punch-Through: High \(V_{DS}\) causes the drain depletion region to expand across the channel and merge with the source depletion region, destroying gate control.
  3. Gate-Oxide Dielectric Breakdown: If vertical electric field exceeds the dielectric strength of \(\text{SiO}_2\) (\(\approx 10\text{ MV/cm}\)), Fowler-Nordheim tunneling or Time-Dependent Dielectric Breakdown (TDDB) punctures localized conductive pinholes through the thin oxide, causing permanent gate-to-channel short circuits.
  4. Parasitic BJT Snapback: Avalanche multiplication near the drain creates a large substrate hole current \(I_{sub}\). Flowing through substrate resistance \(R_{sub}\), this produces a voltage drop \(V_{sub} = I_{sub} R_{sub}\) that forward-biases the source-substrate p-n junction. This turns ON the parasitic lateral npn bipolar transistor (Source = Emitter, Substrate = Base, Drain = Collector), initiating positive feedback that snaps the drain voltage down to a destructive low-impedance state!
PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE LIFE OF A MINORITY CARRIER & THE OHMIC SQUARE (ACHA BRO)

"When an electron is injected into a p-type semiconductor, it is a stranger in a strange land! It diffuses randomly through the lattice until it meets a hole and recombines. The average time it survives is Carrier Lifetime (\(\tau_p\)). The average distance it wanders before vanishing is Diffusion Length (\(L_p = \sqrt{D_p \tau_p}\)). Meanwhile, in on-chip metal wires, sheet resistance abstracts away thickness: any square piece of a uniform metal film has the exact same resistance — whether it is 1 micron wide or 1 kilometer wide: \(R = R_\square (l/w)\)!"

1. Carrier Lifetime & Diffusion Length (Sze & Lee §2.3.1 & §2.4.1, Question 1X.34)

Minority Carrier Lifetime (\(\tau_p\))

• Definition: The average time an excess minority carrier survives in a semiconductor before recombining with a majority carrier.
• Recombination Decay: If uniform light generation \(G_L\) is suddenly extinguished at \(t = 0\), excess carrier concentration decays exponentially: $$p_n(t) = p_{n0} + \tau_p G_L \exp(-t/\tau_p) \tag{47}$$ At \(t = \tau_p\), excess carriers drop to \(1/e\) (\(\approx 36.8\%\)) of their initial value.

Carrier Diffusion Length (\(L_p\))

• Definition: The average distance an injected excess minority carrier travels by Brownian thermal diffusion before recombining.
• Fundamental Mathematical Relation: $$\mathbf{L_p = \sqrt{D_p \tau_p}} \tag{63}$$ where \(D_p\) is the minority carrier diffusion coefficient (\(\text{cm}^2/\text{s}\)) and \(\tau_p\) is minority carrier lifetime. Under steady-state injection at \(x = 0\), carrier density decays as \(e^{-x/L_p}\).

2. Sheet Resistance (\(R_\square\)) (Weste & Harris §6.2.1, Question 1X.31)

THE EXAM DEFINITION · SHEET RESISTANCE (QUESTION 1X.31, 2 MARKS)

"Sheet resistance \(R_\square = \rho / t\) is the electrical resistance of a uniform slab of conducting material having equal length and width (\(l = w\)), expressed in units of \(\Omega/\text{square}\). It abstracts resistivity and film thickness into a single geometry-independent parameter for circuit layout."

The resistance of any rectangular conductor of length \(l\), width \(w\), thickness \(t\), and resistivity \(\rho\) is:

$$R = \frac{\rho}{t} \cdot \frac{l}{w} = \mathbf{R_\square \cdot \frac{l}{w}} \tag{6.2}$$
  • The Dimensionless "Square": Since \(l\) and \(w\) have identical units, the ratio \(l/w\) represents the number of squares in series. A \(10\,\mu\text{m} \times 1\,\mu\text{m}\) wire has 10 squares; its resistance is \(10 R_\square\).

Bulk Electrical Resistivities of Metals at 22°C (Table 6.2):

Metal Conductor Bulk Resistivity (\(\mu\Omega\cdot\text{cm}\)) Common VLSI Application
Silver (Ag) 1.6 Lowest resistivity, but tarnishes and electromigrates.
Copper (Cu) 1.7 Industry standard for multi-level back-end metallization (Dual Damascene).
Gold (Au) 2.2 Wire bonding and corrosion-resistant contact pads.
Aluminum (Al) 2.8 Historical standard interconnect; top bond pad metallization.
Tungsten (W) 5.3 Contact plugs and via fills.
Titanium (Ti) 43.0 Silicide contacts (\(\text{TiSi}_2\)) and adhesion barriers.

Nanometer Wire Corrections (Eq. 6.3):

In sub-45nm nodes, copper wires must be clad with a low-conductivity diffusion barrier (\(t_{barrier} \approx 5\text{--}10\text{ nm}\) of TaN/TiN) to prevent Cu atoms from poisoning the dielectric, and chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP) causes surface dishing (\(t_{dish}\)). The corrected resistance is:

$$R = \frac{\rho}{(t - t_{dish} - t_{barrier})} \cdot \frac{l}{(w - 2t_{barrier})} \tag{6.3}$$

Drain-to-Source Current \( I_{DS} \) versus Voltage \( V_{DS} \) Relationships:

The working of a MOS transistor is based on the principle that the use of a voltage on the gate induce a charge in the channel between source and drain, which may then be caused to move from source to drain under the influence of an electric field created by voltage \( V_{ds} \) applied between drain and source. Since the charge induced is dependent on the gate to source voltage \( V_{gs} \) then \( I_{ds} \) is dependent on both \( V_{gs} \) and \( V_{ds} \).

Let us consider the diagram below in which electrons will flow source to drain .So, the drain current is given by

Click to expand diagram: Channel with L, W, D
\[ I_{ds} = -I_{sd} = \frac{\text{Charge induced in channel } (Q_c)}{\text{Electron transit time } (\tau)} \]

Where the transit time is given by

\[ \tau_{sd} = \frac{\text{Length of the channel}}{\text{Velocity } (v)} \]

14

But velocity \( v= \mu e_{ds} \)

Where \( \mu = \) electron or hole mobility and \( E_{ds} = \) Electric field

Also , \( E_{ds} = V_{ds}/L \)

So, \( v = \mu . V_{ds}/L \)

And \( \tau_{ds} = L^2 / \mu . V_{ds} \)

The typical values of \( \mu \) at room temperature are given below

Click to expand diagram: Mobility values

The Non-saturated Region:

Let us consider the \( I_d \) vs \( V_d \) relationships in the non-saturated region .The charge induced in the channel due to due to the voltage difference between the gate and the channel, \( V_{gs} \) (assuming substrate connected to source). The voltage along the channel varies linearly with distance X from the source due to the IR drop in the channel .In the non-saturated state the average value is \( V_{ds}/2 \). Also the effective gate voltage \( V_g = V_{gs} – V_t \) where \( V_t \), is the threshold voltage needed to invert the charge under the gate and establish the channel.

Hence the induced charge is \( Q_c = E_g \varepsilon_{ins} \varepsilon_o W . L \)

15

Where
\( E_g = \) average electric field gate to channel
\( \varepsilon_{ins} \) – relative permittivity of insulation between gate and channel
\( \varepsilon_o \) = permittivity of free space.

So, we can write that

Click to expand diagram: Equations Block 1
$$E_g = \frac{(V_{gs} - V_t) - \frac{V_{ds}}{2}}{D}$$

Here D is the thickness of the oxide layer. Thus

$$Q_c = \frac{WL\varepsilon_{ins}\varepsilon_o}{D} \left((V_{gs} - V_t) - \frac{V_{ds}}{2}\right)$$

So, by combining the above two equations ,we get

$$I_{ds} = \frac{\varepsilon_{ins}\varepsilon_o\mu}{D} \frac{W}{L} \left((V_{gs} - V_t) - \frac{V_{ds}}{2}\right)V_{ds}$$

Or the above equation can be written as

$$I_{ds} = K\frac{W}{L}\left((V_{gs} - V_t)V_{ds} - \frac{V_{ds}^2}{2}\right)$$

In the non-saturated or resistive region where Vds < Vgs – Vt and

$$K = \frac{\varepsilon_{ins}\varepsilon_o\mu}{D}$$

Generally ,a constant \(\beta\) is defined as

$$\beta = K\frac{W}{L}$$

So that ,the expression for drain –source current will become

$$I_{ds} = \beta\left((V_{gs} - V_t)V_{ds} - \frac{V_{ds}^2}{2}\right)$$

The gate /channel capacitance is

Click to expand diagram: Equations Block 2
$$C_g = \frac{\varepsilon_{ins}\varepsilon_o WL}{D} \text{ (parallel plate)}$$

Hence we can write another alternative form forthe drain current as

$$I_{ds} = \frac{C_g\mu}{L^2} \left((V_{gs} - V_t)V_{ds} - \frac{V_{ds}^2}{2}\right)$$

Some time it is also convenient to use gate –capacitance per unit area ,\( C_g \)

So,the drain current is

$$I_{ds} = C_o\mu\frac{W}{L} \left((V_{gs} - V_t)V_{ds} - \frac{V_{ds}^2}{2}\right)$$
This is the relation between drain current and drain-source voltage in non-saturated region.

The Saturated Region

Saturation begins when \( V_{ds} = V_{gs} - V_t \), since at this point the IR drop in the channel equals the effective gate to channel voltage at the drain and we may assume that the current remains fairly constant as \( V_{ds} \) increases further. Thus

$$I_{ds} = K\frac{W}{L} \frac{(V_{gs} - V_t)^2}{2}$$

Or we can also write that

$$I_{ds} = \frac{\beta}{2}(V_{gs} - V_t)^2$$

Or it can also be written as

$$I_{ds} = \frac{C_g\mu}{2L^2}(V_{gs} - V_t)^2$$

Or

$$I_{ds} = C_o\mu\frac{W}{2L}(V_{gs} - V_t)^2$$

The expressions derived above for \( I_{ds} \) hold for both enhancement and depletion mode devices. Here the threshold voltage for the NMOS depletion mode device (denoted as \( V_{td} \)) is negative.

16

BRO, the paragraph is mixing up the inequalities in the textbook. Let's rebuild the whole \(I_D\)-\(V_{DS}\) characteristic from absolute basics, then the graph becomes almost obvious.

1. First, what are we actually plotting?

For an NMOS, we control two voltages:

  • \(V_{GS}\): gate-to-source voltage → controls whether/how strongly the channel exists
  • \(V_{DS}\): drain-to-source voltage → pushes electrons from source toward drain

And we observe:

  • \(I_D\): drain current

So the graph is:

$$ \boxed{I_D\text{ vs }V_{DS}} $$

for several fixed values of \(V_{GS}\).

Think: Gate voltage decides how much channel is available. Drain voltage decides how hard we push current through it.

2. Start with the easiest case: NMOS OFF

Suppose:

$$ V_{GS} < V_{TH} $$

Here, the gate voltage isn't high enough to create an inversion channel. So:

$$ \boxed{I_D \approx 0} $$

even if you increase \(V_{DS}\).

Visually:

Why?

The gate hasn't created the conducting path between source and drain.
No channel → no significant drain current.

3. Now turn the transistor ON

Increase the gate voltage:

$$ V_{GS} > V_{TH} $$

Now an inversion channel forms between source and drain.

GATE
oxide
e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻
← channel
S
D
P-type substrate

Now current can flow. But here's the important part:

Increasing \(V_{DS}\) doesn't always behave the same way.

There are two major operating regions (with the transition called pinch-off):

  1. Linear / Ohmic / Non-saturation region
  2. Saturation region

4. Region 1: Small \(V_{DS}\) → Linear/Ohmic region

Suppose \(V_{GS}\) is fixed above threshold. Start increasing \(V_{DS}\) from zero.

At first, \(I_D\) increases approximately linearly with \(V_{DS}\).

Why?

Because the MOSFET is behaving somewhat like a voltage-controlled resistor. The channel exists from source to drain, and increasing \(V_{DS}\) increases the electric field along the channel. So:

$$ V_{DS}\uparrow \quad\Rightarrow\quad I_D\uparrow $$

5. But something interesting happens as \(V_{DS}\) keeps increasing

Remember: \(V_{GS} > V_{TH}\) creates the channel, but voltage inside the channel isn't the same everywhere.

  • At the source: \(V(x) \approx 0 \implies V_{GC} \approx V_{GS}\)
  • At the drain: \(V(x) \approx V_{DS} \implies V_{GC} \approx V_{GS} - V_{DS}\)

The effective gate-to-channel voltage gets smaller as we move toward the drain. This is the key to understanding pinch-off.

6. When does pinch-off happen?

The channel disappears at the drain end when the local gate-to-channel voltage reaches threshold:

$$ V_{GS} - V_{DS} = V_{TH} $$

Rearranging this gives us the boundary voltage:

$$ \boxed{V_{DSAT} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}} $$

🚨 Important correction to your textbook

Your paragraph says: "For \(V_{GS} < V_{DS} + V_{TH}\), the NMOS device is conducting and \(I_D\) is independent of \(V_{DS}\)."

That is not consistent with standard equations. The standard conditions are:

  • OFF: \(\boxed{V_{GS} < V_{TH}}\)
  • Linear / Ohmic: \(\boxed{V_{GS} > V_{TH}}\) AND \(\boxed{V_{DS} < V_{GS} - V_{TH}}\)
  • Saturation: \(\boxed{V_{GS} > V_{TH}}\) AND \(\boxed{V_{DS} \ge V_{GS} - V_{TH}}\)

7. What exactly is "pinch-off"?

Pinch-off does NOT mean current suddenly becomes zero.

It means: The inversion channel becomes zero-width at the drain end.

Before pinch-off:

GATE
oxide
e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻
← CHANNEL
Source
Drain
P-type substrate

At pinch-off:

GATE
oxide
← channel pinches here
Source
Drain
P-type substrate

8. Then why does current still flow after pinch-off?

Once pinch-off occurs, the electric field near the drain becomes very strong. Electrons traveling through the channel reach the pinch-off region and are swept toward the drain by this strong electric field.

$$ \boxed{\text{pinch-off} \neq \text{current stops}} \quad \rightarrow \quad \boxed{\text{pinch-off} \rightarrow \text{saturation}} $$

9. Why does \(I_D\) become almost independent of \(V_{DS}\)?

Once \(V_{DS} \ge V_{DSAT}\), the channel is pinched off near the drain. Increasing \(V_{DS}\) further doesn't significantly increase the amount of channel charge controlled by the gate.

Therefore: \(V_{DS}\uparrow\) but approximately \(I_D \approx \text{constant}\). That's the saturation region.

10. Why does the transistor act like a current source?

In saturation, \(I_D \approx \text{constant}\) while \(V_{DS}\) changes. Therefore the MOSFET behaves approximately like a controlled current source, controlled by \(V_{GS}\).

Conceptually: \(\boxed{V_{GS} \rightarrow I_D}\), while in saturation \(\boxed{V_{DS}\text{ has relatively little effect on }I_D}\).

11. The saturation current equation & \(V_{DSAT}\)

For an ideal long-channel NMOS:

$$ \boxed{I_D = \frac{1}{2}k_n(V_{GS}-V_{TH})^2} \quad \text{where} \quad k_n = \mu_n C_{ox} \frac{W}{L} $$

Notice there is no \(V_{DS}\) in this equation. \(V_{DSAT} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}\) is the minimum \(V_{DS}\) needed to put the NMOS into saturation for a particular \(V_{GS}\).

12. Here's the entire thing in one picture

13. Important correction about the textbook wording

Your text says: "This is because there is no carrier inversion at the drain region of the channel."

Don't interpret this as: "There are no electrons near the drain." More accurately: The inversion layer no longer extends continuously right up to the drain terminal. The channel terminates at a pinch-off region, and carriers are swept through this high-field region into the drain.

GATE
oxide
inversion channel
→ →
← PINCH-OFF
(high electric field)
SOURCE
DRAIN
P-type substrate

🧠 The exam-memory version

Memorize this, not the confusing paragraph:

Condition Region What happens
\(V_{GS} < V_{TH}\) Cutoff \(I_D \approx 0\)
\(V_{GS} > V_{TH}\), \(V_{DS} < V_{GS} - V_{TH}\) Linear/Ohmic \(I_D\) increases with \(V_{DS}\)
\(V_{GS} > V_{TH}\), \(V_{DS} \ge V_{GS} - V_{TH}\) Saturation \(I_D\) roughly independent of \(V_{DS}\)
\(V_{DSAT} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}\) Boundary Pinch-off begins

The one-line mental model:

$$ \boxed{\text{Gate creates channel} \rightarrow V_{DS}\text{ pushes carriers} \rightarrow \text{drain end pinches off} \rightarrow I_D\text{ saturates}} $$

And the golden equation:

$$ \boxed{V_{DSAT} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}} $$

This part is actually the physical foundation behind the \(I_D\)-\(V_{DS}\) equations we just discussed. The textbook throws several equations at you at once, so let's unpack the chain slowly.

1. First: what is physically happening inside an NMOS?

An NMOS has:

GATE
oxide
e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻ e⁻
← electrons
n+ Source
n+ Drain
P-type substrate

When we apply:

$$ \boxed{V_{GS} > V_{TH}} $$

the gate creates an inversion channel between source and drain. That channel contains mobile electrons.

Now apply:

$$ \boxed{V_{DS} > 0} $$

This creates an electric field along the channel. That electric field makes the electrons move from source → drain.

So the basic story is:

$$ \boxed{V_{GS} \rightarrow \text{creates electrons/channel}} $$
$$ \boxed{V_{DS} \rightarrow \text{makes those electrons move}} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{I_D = f(V_{GS}, V_{DS})} $$

2. Why does \(V_{GS}\) affect \(I_D\)?

The gate is insulated from the channel by oxide. So the gate doesn't directly inject current into the channel.

Instead, its electric field attracts electrons toward the surface and creates the inversion layer.

  • Higher \(V_{GS}\): \(\boxed{\text{more channel charge}}\)
  • Lower \(V_{GS}\): \(\boxed{\text{less channel charge}}\)

So:

$$ V_{GS}\uparrow \Rightarrow Q_C\uparrow \Rightarrow I_D\uparrow $$

where \(Q_C\) is the charge stored in the channel.

3. Why does \(V_{DS}\) affect \(I_D\)?

Imagine the channel as a road:

SOURCE
DRAIN
electron road
→ → →

\(V_{DS}\) creates an electric field along this road. Electric field:

$$ \boxed{E_{DS} = \frac{V_{DS}}{L}} $$

where:

  • \(E_{DS}\) = electric field along the channel
  • \(V_{DS}\) = drain-source voltage
  • \(L\) = channel length

So if \(V_{DS}\) increases:

$$ E_{DS}\uparrow $$

and the electrons are driven harder along the channel.

4. Now comes the textbook's main idea

The textbook says:

Drain current = charge in channel / time required for that charge to travel from source to drain

That is fundamentally just:

$$ \boxed{I = \frac{Q}{t}} $$

So:

$$ \boxed{I_{DS} = \frac{Q_C}{\tau_{DS}}} $$

where:

  • \(Q_C\) = channel charge
  • \(\tau_{DS}\) = electron transit time from source to drain

This is a very useful physical picture.

5. What is "transit time"?

Transit time simply means: How long does an electron take to travel from source to drain?

If the channel is length \(L\), and electron velocity is \(v\):

$$ \boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{v}} $$

That's just: \(\text{time} = \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{velocity}}\). Nothing mysterious.

For example, if \(L = 10\text{ units}\) and \(v = 2\text{ units/s}\), then \(\tau = 5s\).

Same idea inside the MOSFET, except the dimensions are tiny and velocities are enormous.

6. Now where does electron velocity come from?

The textbook gives:

$$ \boxed{v = \mu E_{DS}} $$

This is the drift velocity relation. Here:

  • \(v\) = average electron drift velocity
  • \(\mu\) = carrier mobility
  • \(E_{DS}\) = electric field

What is mobility?

Mobility tells us: How easily carriers move through the semiconductor when an electric field is applied.

Higher mobility: \(\mu\uparrow \Rightarrow v\uparrow\)

For electrons, we usually use \(\mu_n\). For holes, \(\mu_p\).

So for an NMOS:

$$ \boxed{v_n = \mu_n E_{DS}} $$

7. Substitute the electric field

We already have: \(E_{DS} = \frac{V_{DS}}{L}\)

Therefore: \(v = \mu \frac{V_{DS}}{L}\) or:

$$ \boxed{v = \frac{\mu V_{DS}}{L}} $$

This is exactly what your textbook says.

8. Now calculate transit time

We had: \(\tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{v}\) and \(v = \frac{\mu V_{DS}}{L}\)

Substitute:

$$ \tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{\mu V_{DS} / L} $$

Dividing by a fraction means multiplying by its reciprocal:

$$ \tau_{DS} = L \times \frac{L}{\mu V_{DS}} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L^2}{\mu V_{DS}}} $$

And that's the equation appearing in your notes.

9. Now notice something VERY important

Look at:

$$ \boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L^2}{\mu V_{DS}}} $$

What happens when \(V_{DS}\) increases? The denominator increases.

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{V_{DS}\uparrow \Rightarrow \tau_{DS}\downarrow} $$

Meaning: Higher \(V_{DS}\) makes the electrons cross the channel faster.

And since \(I = \frac{Q}{t}\), smaller transit time means larger current:

$$ \tau\downarrow \Rightarrow I_D\uparrow $$

That's why \(I_D\) initially increases as \(V_{DS}\) increases.

10. Put the entire chain together 🔥

This is the part I want you to actually understand:

$$ \boxed{V_{DS}\uparrow} $$
↓
Electric field increases
$$ \boxed{E_{DS} = \frac{V_{DS}}{L}} $$
↓
Carrier velocity increases
$$ \boxed{v = \mu E_{DS}} $$
↓
Transit time decreases
$$ \boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{v}} $$
↓
More charge passes drain per sec
$$ \boxed{I_D = \frac{Q_C}{\tau_{DS}}} $$
↓
Therefore
$$ \boxed{V_{DS}\uparrow \Rightarrow I_D\uparrow} $$

That is the physical reason for the rising part of the \(I_D\)-\(V_{DS}\) curve.

11. But wait... this seems to predict \(I_D \propto V_{DS}\)

Exactly! And this is where the linear/ohmic region comes from.

If channel charge \(Q_C\) were approximately constant:

$$ I_D = \frac{Q_C}{\tau} \quad \text{and} \quad \tau = \frac{L^2}{\mu V_{DS}} $$

then:

$$ I_D = Q_C \frac{\mu V_{DS}}{L^2} $$

Therefore:

$$ \boxed{I_D \propto V_{DS}} $$

That gives the approximately straight portion of the curve.

But there is one giant catch: \(Q_C\) is NOT actually constant along the channel when \(V_{DS}\) becomes significant.

And that's why the curve eventually bends.

12. Why does the curve bend?

This connects directly to what we discussed previously. The gate creates the channel, but the channel voltage isn't the same everywhere.

  • At the source: \(V(x) = 0\)
  • At the drain: \(V(x) = V_{DS}\)

Therefore, the effective gate-to-channel voltage near the drain becomes: \(V_{GS} - V_{DS}\)

As \(V_{DS}\) increases: \(V_{GS} - V_{DS}\downarrow\). So the channel becomes thinner toward the drain.

GATE
oxide
← channel gets narrower
SOURCE
DRAIN
P-type substrate

Therefore the available channel charge \(Q_C\) is no longer constant. That causes the \(I_D\)-\(V_{DS}\) curve to bend.

13. Eventually: pinch-off

When: \(V_{DS} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}\), we reach:

$$ \boxed{V_{DSAT} = V_{GS} - V_{TH}} $$

The channel pinches off at the drain end. After that, we're in saturation.

So our simple "higher \(V_{DS}\) → faster electrons → higher current" story stops being sufficient because the channel itself is changing.

14. One subtle point: \(I_{DS} = -I_{SD}\)

Your textbook writes:

$$ \boxed{I_{DS} = -I_{SD}} $$

This is just a sign convention.

For an NMOS, electron movement is: \(\boxed{\text{Source} \rightarrow \text{Drain}}\) because electrons are negatively charged.

But conventional current is defined opposite to electron movement: \(\boxed{\text{Drain} \rightarrow \text{Source}}\)

So don't let the negative sign scare you. It is mostly bookkeeping about current direction.

15. The entire derivation as a chain

Your textbook is basically doing this:

  • Start: \(\boxed{I_D = \frac{Q_C}{\tau_{DS}}}\)
  • Transit time: \(\boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{v}}\)
  • Carrier velocity: \(\boxed{v = \mu E_{DS}}\)
  • Electric field: \(\boxed{E_{DS} = \frac{V_{DS}}{L}}\)

Therefore: \(v = \mu \frac{V_{DS}}{L}\)

Then: \(\tau_{DS} = \frac{L}{\mu V_{DS}/L}\) giving: \(\boxed{\tau_{DS} = \frac{L^2}{\mu V_{DS}}}\)

And therefore, in the simplified constant-charge picture:

$$ I_D = \frac{Q_C \mu V_{DS}}{L^2} $$

which explains why current initially increases with \(V_{DS}\).

🧠 The BIG picture

Don't memorize these as disconnected equations. Think of the MOSFET as:

GATE
\( V_{GS} \)
↓ controls CHANNEL
\( Q_c \) = charge
SOURCE
→ electrons →
↓
DRAIN
DRAIN-SOURCE VOLTAGE
\( V_{DS} \)
creates electric field
↓
\( E_{DS} = V_{DS} / L \)
↓
\( v = \mu E_{DS} \)
↓
\( \tau = L / v \)
↓
\( I_D = Q_C / \tau \)

So there are two controls:

Gate:

$$ \boxed{V_{GS} \rightarrow \text{how much charge is in the channel}} $$

Drain-source voltage:

$$ \boxed{V_{DS} \rightarrow \text{how quickly that charge moves}} $$

And together:

$$ \boxed{V_{GS}\text{ controls the channel} \quad+\quad V_{DS}\text{ drives the carriers} \quad\rightarrow\quad I_D} $$

That's the physical intuition sitting underneath the MOSFET \(I_D\)-\(V_{DS}\) characteristics.

Threshold Voltage & Body Effect

MOS Transistor Threshold Voltage \(V_t\)

The gate structure of a MOS transistor consists, of charges stored in the dielectric layers and in the surface to surface interfaces as well as in the substrate itself. Switching an enhancement mode MOS transistor from the off to the on state consists in applying sufficient gate voltage to neutralize these charges and enable the underlying silicon to undergo an inversion due to the electric field from the gate. Switching a depletion mode NMOS transistor from the on to the off state consists in applying enough voltage to the gate to add to the stored charge and invert the 'n' implant region to 'p'.

The threshold voltage \(V_t\) may be expressed as:

$$V_t = -\Phi_{ms} \frac{Q_B - Q_{SS}}{C_o} + 2\Phi_{fN}$$
Where:
  • \(Q_B\) = the charge per unit area in the depletion layer below the oxide
  • \(Q_{SS}\) = charge density at Si: SiO₂ interface
  • \(C_o\) = Capacitance per unit area
  • \(\Phi_{ms}\) = work function difference between gate and Si
  • \(\Phi_{fN}\) = Fermi level potential between inverted surface and bulk Si

For polynomial [polysilicon] gate and silicon substrate, the value of \(\Phi_{ms}\) is negative but negligible and the magnitude and sign of \(V_t\) are thus determined by balancing the other terms in the equation.

To evaluate the \(V_t\) the other terms are determined as below.

$$Q_B = \sqrt{2\varepsilon_0\varepsilon_{Si}qN(2\Phi_{fN} + V_{SB})} \text{ coulomb/m}^2$$ $$\Phi_{fN} = \frac{kT}{q} \ln \frac{N}{n_i} \text{ volts}$$ $$Q_{SS} = (1.5 \text{ to } 8) \times 10^{-8} \text{ coulomb/m}^2$$

Body Effect (Substrate Bias Effect)

Generally while studying the MOS transistors it is treated as a three terminal device. But, the body of the transistor is also an implicit fourth terminal which fundamentally influences device characteristics. Considering the substrate/body of the MOS transistor as an active terminal is known as the body effect (or back-gate effect).

The potential difference between the source and the body (\(V_{SB}\)) alters the depletion charge beneath the gate oxide, directly shifting the threshold voltage of the transistor. In many single-transistor situations where source and body are tied together (\(V_{SB} = 0\)), this effect is absent. However, in series transistor stacks (such as the pull-down network of NAND gates or pass-transistor logic), the source terminals of internal transistors sit at non-zero voltages above ground, causing significant \(V_{SB} > 0\) that elevates \(V_T\) and slows down switching.

Figure: MOS Body Effect Physics — (Left) Transistor cross-section with reverse substrate-source bias \(V_{SB} > 0\); (Middle) Energy band diagram showing enhanced surface band-bending; (Right) Depletion layer widening requiring higher gate voltage to achieve strong inversion.

Body Effect - NMOS Device Derivation

Increasing \(V_{SB}\) widens the depletion layer and strips additional mobile carriers, increasing the uncompensated bulk acceptor charge \(Q_B\). Because the gate must support more fixed depletion charge before inversion can occur, the threshold voltage is raised.

EXAM DERIVATION · QUESTION Q6 (10 MARKS)

Expression for nMOS Threshold Voltage with Body Effect

When \(V_{SB} > 0\), the total potential drop across the depletion region increases from \(2|\phi_F|\) to \(2|\phi_F| + V_{SB}\). The depletion charge per unit area becomes: $$Q_B(V_{SB}) = -\sqrt{2q\varepsilon_{Si}N_A(2|\phi_F| + V_{SB})}$$ Substituting into the fundamental threshold expression \(V_T = V_{FB} + 2|\phi_F| - \frac{Q_B}{C_{ox}}\) yields the standard body effect formula: $$\mathbf{V_T = V_{T0} + \gamma \left( \sqrt{2|\phi_F| + V_{SB}} - \sqrt{2|\phi_F|} \right)}$$ where \(\gamma = \frac{\sqrt{2q\varepsilon_{Si}N_A}}{C_{ox}}\) is the body effect coefficient (typically \(0.4\text{--}1.0\,\text{V}^{1/2}\)).

Figure: Question Q6 Model Derivation — Step-by-step mathematical proof of the nMOS threshold voltage equation incorporating body bias \(V_{SB}\) and body-effect factor \(\gamma\).

The threshold voltage can be written as:

$$V_t = V_t(0) + \left(\frac{D}{\varepsilon_{ins}\varepsilon_0}\right) \sqrt{2\varepsilon_0\varepsilon_{Si}QN} \cdot (V_{SB})^{1/2}$$

Where \(V_t(0)\) is the threshold voltage for \(V_{sd} = 0\).

For n-MOS depletion mode transistors, the body voltage values at different \(V_{DD}\) voltages are given below:
  • \(V_{SB} = 0 \text{ V} ; V_{sd} = -0.7 V_{DD} \text{ (= -3.5 V for } V_{DD} = +5\text{V)}\)
  • \(V_{SB} = 5 \text{ V} ; V_{sd} = -0.6 V_{DD} \text{ (= -3.0 V for } V_{DD} = +5\text{V)}\) to 'p'.

CMOS Inverter DC Characteristics & Transfer Characteristics (VTC)

Complete graphical load-line derivation, the 5 operating regions (A through E), short-circuit supply current peak (\(I_{DD}\)), switching threshold (\(V_M\)) derivation, and skewed inverter design (\(\beta_p/\beta_n\)). Weste & Harris §2.5, pp. 79–86.

THE EXAM LINE

A static CMOS inverter exhibits a highly nonlinear Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC) segmented into five operating regions (A through E). At static logic rails (Regions A & E), one transistor is completely in cutoff, resulting in zero quiescent DC supply current. At switching threshold \(V_{in} = V_M \approx V_{DD}/2\) (Region C), both nMOS and pMOS simultaneously enter saturation, yielding maximum small-signal voltage gain \(|A_v| \gg 1\) and a crowbar supply current peak \(I_{DD}\).

PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · CHOLO BRO

"Cholo bro, static CMOS inverter-er DC transfer characteristic ekdom crystal-clear vabe bujhi. Ekta single stage e upore pMOS pull-up network, niche nMOS pull-down network. Jokhon input \(V_{in}\) 0 theke \(V_{DD}\) te slowly sweep kore, circuit ta 5-ti distinct physical region (A theke E) er modhye diye pass kore. Protek ta region e kon transistor Cutoff, Linear, naki Saturation e ache, seta bujhle VTC curve ar exam question konodino vul hobe na!"

1. CMOS Inverter Schematic & Current Conventions (Figure 2.25)

Consider the standard complementary static inverter shown in Figure 2.25. It comprises an nMOS pulldown transistor and a pMOS pullup transistor driven by a common input voltage \(V_{in}\) and sharing a common output node \(V_{out}\).

Figure 2.25: A CMOS Inverter with current conventions \(I_{dsp}\) and \(I_{dsn}\).

The terminal voltages and operating currents for both transistors are related to input and output voltages by:

nMOS Transistor Terminal Voltages

Source is tied to GND (0 V):

$$\begin{aligned} V_{gsn} &= V_{in} \\ V_{dsn} &= V_{out} \end{aligned}$$

nMOS conducts current \(I_{dsn}\) from output to GND.

pMOS Transistor Terminal Voltages

Source is tied to \(V_{DD}\):

$$\begin{aligned} V_{gsp} &= V_{in} - V_{DD} \quad (< 0) \\ V_{dsp} &= V_{out} - V_{DD} \quad (< 0) \end{aligned}$$

pMOS conducts current \(-I_{dsp}\) from \(V_{DD}\) to output.

Applying Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL) at the output node under DC steady-state conditions (assuming zero external DC load current, \(I_{out} = 0\)):

$$\mathbf{I_{dsn} = -I_{dsp} = I_{DD}}$$

At every point along the DC transfer curve, the drain current of the nMOS must precisely balance the source-drain current of the pMOS.

2. Graphical Derivation & The 5 Operating Regions (Figure 2.26)

To determine the output voltage for any given input voltage, we solve the condition \(I_{dsn}(V_{in}, V_{out}) = -I_{dsp}(V_{in}, V_{out})\). Figure 2.26(a) plots the nMOS \(I-V\) curves in Quadrant 1 and the pMOS \(I-V\) curves in Quadrant 3. Overlaying and equating them yields the classic CMOS Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC) in Figure 2.26(c), which partitions into five distinct operating regions (A, B, C, D, and E).

Figure 2.26: (a) Transistor I-V curves in Quadrant 1 (nMOS) and Quadrant 3 (pMOS); (c) Resulting Voltage Transfer Characteristic (VTC) showing Regions A, B, C, D, and E.
Figure 2.27: Simulated CMOS inverter DC characteristic showing realistic smooth transition due to finite channel-length modulation (\(\lambda > 0\)).

3. Master Analytical Breakdown Table: Regions A through E

\(V_{dsn} \approx 0 < V_{in}-V_{tn}\)		 CUTOFF
\(|V_{gsp}| < |V_{tp}|\)		 \(V_{out} = V_{OL} = 0\text{ V}\) \(I_{dsp} = 0 \implies I_{DD} = 0\)

PHYSICAL SIGNIFICANCE · THE ZERO-STATIC-POWER REVOLUTION

"Bujhte hobe keno CMOS technology puro semiconductor industry conquer korechilo! Old nMOS logic-e jokhon output 0 hoto, pull-up depletion transistor die VDD theke GND te continuously heavy DC current pass korto. Kintu Complementary CMOS e rails e static current ZERO! Current shudhu switching transition er majhkhane (Region C te) transient short-circuit crowbar hishabe flow kore."

1. Load-Line Intersection Analysis (Figure 2.26(b))

To visualize the simultaneous solution of both transistors, we construct the load-line plot in Figure 2.26(b).

Figure 2.26: (b) Load-line intersection between \(I_{dsn}\) (black) and \(|I_{dsp}|\) (blue) vs \(V_{out}\); (d) Supply current \(I_{DD}\) vs \(V_{in}\) showing the sharp triangular short-circuit peak.

How the load line operates graphically:

  • nMOS Curves (Black): Plotted as standard \(I_{dsn}\) vs \(V_{dsn} = V_{out}\), rooted at origin \((0,0)\), parameterized by gate voltage \(V_{gsn} = V_{in}\) (\(V_{in1}, V_{in2}, V_{in3}, V_{in4}, V_{in5}\)).
  • pMOS Load Lines (Blue): Plotted as \(|I_{dsp}|\) vs \(V_{out}\). Because \(V_{dsp} = V_{out} - V_{DD}\), the pMOS curves are flipped horizontally and anchored at \(V_{out} = V_{DD}\).
  • Operating Point: For any fixed \(V_{in}\), the circuit operating point \((V_{out}, I_{DD})\) is given by the unique intersection point of the corresponding nMOS curve and pMOS curve.

2. Supply Current Peak (\(I_{DD}\)) & Crowbar Derivation

As shown in Figure 2.26(d), the supply current \(I_{DD}\) drawn from \(V_{DD}\) exhibits a triangular characteristic across the input range:

At Logic Rails (\(V_{in} \le V_{tn}\) or \(V_{in} \ge V_{DD}-|V_{tp}|\))

$$\mathbf{I_{DD} \approx 0}$$

In Region A, nMOS is OFF (\(I_{dsn}=0\)). In Region E, pMOS is OFF (\(I_{dsp}=0\)). The series DC path between \(V_{DD}\) and GND is fully broken.

At Switching Midpoint (\(V_{in} = V_M \approx V_{DD}/2\))

$$\mathbf{I_{DD,peak} = \frac{\beta_n}{2}\left(\frac{V_{DD}}{2} - V_{tn}\right)^2}$$

In Region C, both nMOS and pMOS are simultaneously turned ON and operating in saturation. A direct conductive crowbar path exists from \(V_{DD}\) to GND.

EXAM DERIVATION · PEAK SHORT-CIRCUIT CURRENT

For a symmetric inverter with \(\beta_n = \beta_p = \beta\) and \(V_{tn} = |V_{tp}| = V_t\), the switching threshold is exactly \(V_M = V_{DD}/2\). Since both transistors are in saturation: $$I_{DD,peak} = \frac{\beta}{2}\left(\frac{V_{DD}}{2} - V_t\right)^2$$ This current exists only during input logic transitions (\(0 \to 1\) or \(1 \to 0\)). If an input signal has slow rise/fall times \(t_r, t_f\), the inverter lingers in Region C, causing massive transient crowbar energy loss: $$P_{short-circuit} \approx I_{DD,peak} \cdot \left(\frac{t_r + t_f}{2}\right) \cdot V_{DD} \cdot f_{clk}$$

Short-Circuit Peak Current (\(I_{DD,peak}\)) Calculator

Calculate the exact short-circuit peak current during logic switching based on power supply voltage \(V_{DD}\), transistor transconductance parameter \(\beta\), and threshold voltage \(V_t\).

0.00 μA
PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · SKEWING PHYSICS

"Inverter ki shob shomoy symmetric thaka dorkar? Not necessarily! Kono circuit e jodi falling edge beshi fast lagay, nMOS ke strong banabo (\(\beta_p/\beta_n < 1\)), takey bole Low-Skew Inverter. Abar jodi rising edge fast lagay, pMOS ke boro banabo (\(\beta_p/\beta_n > 1\)), takey bole High-Skew Inverter. Weste & Harris Figure 2.28 dekho, curve ta kivabe baame ba daane slide kore!"

1. Switching Threshold (\(V_M\)) Exact Derivation

The switching threshold \(V_M\) (often designated \(V_{inv}\)) is defined as the operating point where input voltage equals output voltage:

$$\mathbf{V_{in} = V_{out} = V_M}$$

At this point, both transistors are guaranteed to be in saturation because:

$$\begin{aligned} V_{dsn} = V_M &> V_M - V_{tn} \implies \text{nMOS is in Saturation} \\ |V_{dsp}| = V_{DD} - V_M &> (V_{DD} - V_M) - |V_{tp}| \implies \text{pMOS is in Saturation} \end{aligned}$$

Equating their saturation drain currents:

$$\frac{\beta_n}{2}(V_M - V_{tn})^2 = \frac{\beta_p}{2}(V_{DD} - V_M - |V_{tp}|)^2$$

Taking the square root of both sides:

$$\sqrt{\beta_n}(V_M - V_{tn}) = \sqrt{\beta_p}(V_{DD} - V_M - |V_{tp}|)$$

Solving algebraically for \(V_M\):

$$\mathbf{V_M = \frac{V_{DD} - |V_{tp}| + \sqrt{\frac{\beta_n}{\beta_p}} V_{tn}}{1 + \sqrt{\frac{\beta_n}{\beta_p}}} = \frac{V_{tn} + r(V_{DD} - |V_{tp}|)}{1 + r}, \quad \text{where } r = \sqrt{\frac{\beta_p}{\beta_n}}}$$

Symmetric Inverter (\(\beta_p = \beta_n\))

When \(V_{tn} = |V_{tp}|\) and \(\beta_p = \beta_n\), the equation simplifies directly to: $$V_M = \frac{V_{DD}}{2}$$ Since electron mobility is roughly 2 to 3 times hole mobility (\(\mu_n \approx 2\mu_p\)): $$\beta = \mu C_{ox}\frac{W}{L} \implies \left(\frac{W}{L}\right)_p \approx 2 \times \left(\frac{W}{L}\right)_n$$

Noise Margins (\(NM_L\) & \(NM_H\))

Noise margins quantify immunity against cross-talk and supply bounce: $$\begin{aligned} NM_L &= V_{IL} - V_{OL} = V_{IL} - 0 = V_{IL} \\ NM_H &= V_{OH} - V_{IH} = V_{DD} - V_{IH} \end{aligned}$$ Where \(V_{IL}\) and \(V_{IH}\) are the unity-gain points: \(\frac{dV_{out}}{dV_{in}} = -1\).

2. Skewed Inverters (Figure 2.28)

By altering the transistor size ratio \(W_p / W_n\), the designer changes the beta ratio \(\beta_p / \beta_n\). Figure 2.28 illustrates how the transfer characteristic shifts as \(\beta_p / \beta_n\) varies from 0.1 to 10.

Figure 2.28: Transfer characteristics of skewed inverters for \(\beta_p/\beta_n = 0.1, 0.5, 1, 2, 10\).
Inverter Type Beta Ratio (\(\beta_p/\beta_n\)) VTC Shift & \(V_M\) Fastest Transition Dominant Noise Margin Typical Applications
Low-Skew Inverter \(\beta_p/\beta_n < 1\)
(e.g. 0.1, 0.5)		 Shifts to the LEFT
\(V_M < V_{DD}/2\)		 Falling transition (\(1 \to 0\))
Stronger nMOS pulls down rapidly		 \(NM_H\) increases
\(NM_L\) decreases		 Dynamic logic evaluate paths, NAND output drivers, clock gating cells
Symmetric Inverter \(\beta_p/\beta_n = 1\)
(\(W_p \approx 2W_n\))		 Centered
\(V_M = V_{DD}/2\)		 Balanced
\(\tau_{PHL} \approx \tau_{PLH}\)		 Balanced
\(NM_L \approx NM_H\)		 General purpose static CMOS logic gates, standard cell libraries
High-Skew Inverter \(\beta_p/\beta_n > 1\)
(e.g. 2.0, 10)		 Shifts to the RIGHT
\(V_M > V_{DD}/2\)		 Rising transition (\(0 \to 1\))
Stronger pMOS pulls up rapidly		 \(NM_L\) increases
\(NM_H\) decreases		 Precharge circuits, active-low reset drivers, NOR pullup paths

MOS Inverter Delay, Sizing & Static Power

From switching delay definitions and exact time-domain differential solutions to parasitic self-loading speed limits and nanometer subthreshold leakage mechanics. Kang & Leblebici Ch. 6, Weste & Harris §5.3 & §7.4.

6.2 Delay-Time Definitions: Propagation Delay vs Rise/Fall Times

Cholo bro 😂 fabrication theke ebar jump dilam MOSFET switching speed and transistor sizing-e. The topic sounds equation-heavy, but the main physical intuition is straightforward:

Core Engineering Dilemma

"A transistor can produce the logically correct output and still fail specifications because it is too slow. VLSI design therefore asks two fundamental questions:
1. Exactly how long does an inverter take to transition its output voltage?
2. How can we make it faster by tuning transistor channel dimensions (\(W\) and \(L\))?"

1. Propagation Delay: How Late Does the Output Respond?

When the input to an inverter flips, the output does not switch instantaneously. The pull-up or pull-down network requires finite time to charge or discharge the load capacitance (\(C_{load}\)) tied to the output terminal. The time difference between the input transition and the resulting output transition is the propagation delay.

A. \(\tau_{PHL}\): Output High-to-Low Transition

• Trigger: Input rises from LOW to HIGH (\(V_{OL} \rightarrow V_{OH}\)).
• Circuit Action: nMOS turns ON, pMOS turns OFF. nMOS discharges \(C_{load}\) to ground.
• Measurement: Time delay between the 50% point of the rising input and the 50% point of the falling output (\(t_1 - t_0\)).

B. \(\tau_{PLH}\): Output Low-to-High Transition

• Trigger: Input falls from HIGH to LOW (\(V_{OH} \rightarrow V_{OL}\)).
• Circuit Action: nMOS turns OFF, pMOS turns ON. pMOS charges \(C_{load}\) from \(V_{DD}\).
• Measurement: Time delay between the 50% point of the falling input and the 50% point of the rising output (\(t_3 - t_2\)).

Crucial Memory Rule:
The subscripts in \(\tau_{PHL}\) and \(\tau_{PLH}\) always designate the direction of the OUTPUT transition, never the input!
• \(\tau_{PHL}\) = Propagation delay for High-to-Low output.
• \(\tau_{PLH}\) = Propagation delay for Low-to-High output.

What is the 50% Reference Voltage (\(V_{50\%}\))?

The 50% voltage level marks the midpoint of the output logic swing:

\[ V_{50\%} = V_{OL} + \frac{1}{2}(V_{OH} - V_{OL}) = \frac{V_{OL} + V_{OH}}{2} \tag{6.2} \]

For an ideal CMOS inverter swinging rail-to-rail (\(V_{OH} = V_{DD}\) and \(V_{OL} = 0\)):

\[ \boxed{V_{50\%} = \frac{V_{DD}}{2}} \]

Why the 50% point? It provides an unambiguous, repeatable benchmark. Real signals approach final voltages asymptotically; waiting for 100% settling would make delay calculations ill-defined.

Figure 6.3: Input and output voltage waveforms of a typical inverter, and definitions of propagation delay times \(\tau_{PHL}\) and \(\tau_{PLH}\) (idealized step input assumed).

2. Propagation Delay vs Rise/Fall Times

Students frequently confuse propagation delay with rise/fall times. They describe two completely different physical metrics:

Propagation Delay (\(\tau_p\))

Measures latency between two different nodes: the time elapsed from when the input reaches 50% until the output reaches 50%.

Rise & Fall Times (\(\tau_{rise}, \tau_{fall}\))

Measures edge steepness at a single node: the time required for the output itself to transition between 10% and 90% of its full swing.

\[ V_{10\%} = V_{OL} + 0.1 \cdot (V_{OH} - V_{OL}) \tag{6.5} \] \[ V_{90\%} = V_{OL} + 0.9 \cdot (V_{OH} - V_{OL}) \tag{6.6} \] \[ \tau_{fall} = t_B - t_A \quad (90\% \rightarrow 10\%) \tag{6.7a} \] \[ \tau_{rise} = t_D - t_C \quad (10\% \rightarrow 90\%) \tag{6.7b} \]
Figure 6.4: Output voltage rise and fall time definitions (\(10\% \leftrightarrow 90\%\)).

3. Average Propagation Delay (\(\tau_p\))

Because hole mobility in pMOS is roughly 2 to 3 times lower than electron mobility in nMOS (\(\mu_n \approx 2\text{--}3\mu_p\)), pull-up and pull-down delays rarely match exactly. The inverter's characteristic speed is therefore quoted as the arithmetic average:

\[ \boxed{\tau_p = \frac{\tau_{PHL} + \tau_{PLH}}{2}} \tag{6.4} \]
Quick Numerical Demonstration

If an inverter exhibits \(\tau_{PHL} = 80\text{ ps}\) and \(\tau_{PLH} = 120\text{ ps}\):
\[ \tau_p = \frac{80\text{ ps} + 120\text{ ps}}{2} = \mathbf{100\text{ ps}} \quad (1\text{ ps} = 10^{-12}\text{ s}) \]

4. The Fundamental Physics: Charge, Voltage, and Current

The output node possesses capacitance (\(C_{load}\)) originating from gate inputs of downstream gates, interconnect wiring, and parasitic drain junctions. To change the output voltage by \(\Delta V\), an electric charge \(\Delta Q = C_{load} \Delta V\) must be moved:

\[ I = C_{load} \frac{\Delta V}{\Delta t} \implies \boxed{\Delta t = \frac{C_{load} \cdot \Delta V}{I}} \]
The Universal VLSI Speed Rule:
• More capacitance (\(C_{load}\uparrow\)): More charge must be pumped → Delay increases.
• More transistor current (\(I\uparrow\)): Charge is pumped faster → Delay decreases.

6.3 Mathematical Calculation of Propagation Delay Times

How do we translate physical transistor parameters (\(W, L, \mu, C_{ox}, V_T\)) into exact picosecond delays? We examine two analytical approaches: the first-order Average-Current Method and the exact Time-Domain State Equation Integration.

Method 1: Average-Current Approximation

Approximates the continuously varying transistor current during discharge with an average current \(I_{avg}\) evaluated at the start and midpoint of the transition: \[ \tau_{PHL} \approx \frac{C_{load} \cdot (V_{OH} - V_{50\%})}{I_{avg,HL}} \tag{6.8} \] \[ I_{avg,HL} = \frac{1}{2} \left[ i_C(V_{out}=V_{OH}) + i_C(V_{out}=V_{50\%}) \right] \tag{6.10} \] Limitation: Simple for hand calculations, but underestimates nonlinear curvature.

Method 2: Exact Differential State Equation

Solves the output node Kirchhoff current law in the time domain: \[ C_{load} \frac{dV_{out}}{dt} = i_{D,p} - i_{D,n} \tag{6.12} \] When input steps to \(V_{OH}\), pMOS turns off (\(i_{D,p} \approx 0\)), reducing the circuit to an nMOS discharging \(C_{load}\): \[ C_{load} \frac{dV_{out}}{dt} = -i_{D,n} \tag{6.14} \]

Figure 6.5: Equivalent circuit of the CMOS inverter during high-to-low output transition.
Figure 6.6: Input and output voltage waveforms showing nMOS operating in saturation (\(t_0\) to \(t_1'\)) and linear region (\(t_1'\) to \(t_1\)).

The Two Operating Phases of Discharging nMOS

As the output voltage falls from \(V_{OH}\) to \(V_{50\%}\), the nMOS does not stay in one operating mode. It traverses two distinct physical regimes:

Phase 1: Saturation Region (\(t_0 \rightarrow t_1'\), Output from \(V_{OH}\) down to \(V_{OH} - V_{Tn}\))

Initially \(V_{out} = V_{OH}\), so \(V_{DS} = V_{out} > V_{GS} - V_{Tn} = V_{OH} - V_{Tn}\). The nMOS channel is pinched off at the drain end:

\[ i_{D,n} = \frac{k_n}{2}(V_{OH} - V_{Tn})^2 = I_{sat} \quad (\text{Constant Current!}) \tag{6.15} \]

Integrating the differential equation between \(t_0\) and \(t_1'\):

\[ \int_{t_0}^{t_1'} dt = -C_{load} \int_{V_{OH}}^{V_{OH}-V_{Tn}} \frac{dV_{out}}{i_{D,n}} = \frac{C_{load} \cdot V_{Tn}}{I_{sat}} \] \[ \boxed{t_1' - t_0 = \frac{2\,C_{load}\,V_{Tn}}{k_n (V_{OH} - V_{Tn})^2}} \tag{6.17} \]

Phase 2: Linear Region (\(t_1' \rightarrow t_1\), Output from \(V_{OH} - V_{Tn}\) down to \(V_{50\%}\))

Once \(V_{out} \le V_{OH} - V_{Tn}\), the pinch-off disappears and the nMOS enters the ohmic/linear regime:

\[ i_{D,n} = \frac{k_n}{2} \left[ 2(V_{OH} - V_{Tn})V_{out} - V_{out}^2 \right] \tag{6.18} \]

Integrating from \(V_{OH}-V_{Tn}\) down to \(V_{50\%}\):

\[ t_1 - t_1' = -2\,C_{load} \int_{V_{OH}-V_{Tn}}^{V_{50\%}} \frac{dV_{out}}{k_n \left[ 2(V_{OH} - V_{Tn})V_{out} - V_{out}^2 \right]} \] \[ \boxed{t_1 - t_1' = \frac{C_{load}}{k_n (V_{OH} - V_{Tn})} \ln \left( \frac{2(V_{OH} - V_{Tn}) - V_{50\%}}{V_{50\%}} \right)} \tag{6.21} \]

The Complete Closed-Form \(\tau_{PHL}\) Equation

Adding the saturation interval (\(t_1' - t_0\)) and linear interval (\(t_1 - t_1'\)) yields the exact propagation delay:

\[ \tau_{PHL} = \frac{C_{load}}{k_n (V_{OH} - V_{Tn})} \left[ \frac{2\,V_{Tn}}{V_{OH} - V_{Tn}} + \ln \left( \frac{4(V_{OH} - V_{Tn})}{V_{OH} + V_{OL}} - 1 \right) \right] \tag{6.22a} \]

Setting \(V_{OH} = V_{DD}\) and \(V_{OL} = 0\) for rail-to-rail CMOS:

\[ \boxed{\tau_{PHL} = \frac{C_{load}}{k_n (V_{DD} - V_{Tn})} \left[ \frac{2\,V_{Tn}}{V_{DD} - V_{Tn}} + \ln \left( \frac{4(V_{DD} - V_{Tn})}{V_{DD}} - 1 \right) \right]} \tag{6.22b} \]

Step-by-Step Textbook Numerical Example (Kang & Leblebici p. 203)

Standard University Exam Problem

Given Parameters:
Supply Voltage \(V_{DD} = 3.3\text{ V}\), Threshold \(V_{Tn} = 0.8\text{ V}\), Load Capacitance \(C_{load} = 300\text{ fF} = 0.3\text{ pF}\), Saturation Current \(I_{sat} = 2.0\text{ mA}\).

Step 1: Compute Saturation Duration (\(t_{sat}\))

\(t_{sat} = \frac{V_{Tn} \cdot C_{load}}{I_{sat}} = \frac{0.8\text{ V} \times 300\times 10^{-15}\text{ F}}{2.0\times 10^{-3}\text{ A}} = \mathbf{120\text{ ps}}\)


Step 2: Calculate Transconductance Parameter (\(k_n\))

\(k_n = \frac{2\,I_{sat}}{(V_{DD} - V_{Tn})^2} = \frac{2 \times 2.0\times 10^{-3}}{(3.3 - 0.8)^2} = \frac{4.0\times 10^{-3}}{6.25} = \mathbf{0.640\times 10^{-3}\text{ A/V}^2} = 0.640\text{ mA/V}^2\)


Step 3: Compute Linear Duration (\(t_{delay} - t_{sat}\))

\(t_{delay} - t_{sat} = \frac{0.3\times 10^{-12}}{0.640\times 10^{-3} \times (3.3 - 0.8)} \left[ \ln\left(\frac{5 - 1.65}{1.65}\right) - \ln\left(\frac{5 - 2.5}{2.5}\right) \right]\)
\(t_{linear} = \frac{0.3\times 10^{-12}}{1.60\times 10^{-3}} \times \ln(2.030) = 187.5\text{ ps} \times 0.708 = \mathbf{133\text{ ps}}\)


Step 4: Total High-to-Low Delay (\(\tau_{PHL}\))

\(\tau_{PHL} = t_{sat} + t_{linear} = 120\text{ ps} + 133\text{ ps} = \mathbf{253\text{ ps}}\)

⚡ Interactive Delay Solver (Kang & Leblebici Engine)

Adjust parameters to dynamically verify how saturation and linear operating regimes partition the total fall delay:

Saturation Time (\(t_{sat}\)):
120.0 ps
Transconductance (\(k_n\)):
0.640 mA/V²
Linear Time (\(t_{linear}\)):
133.0 ps
Total Delay (\(\tau_{PHL}\)):
253.0 ps

7.4 Transistor Sizing & The Inherent Speed Limit

A classic rookie mistake in VLSI design is assuming: "If my gate is too slow, I will simply make the transistors infinitely wide to supply infinite current." In real silicon, this intuition fails completely due to drain parasitic self-loading.

Narrow Transistor

• Small drive current (\(I_D \propto W\)).
• Small parasitic self-capacitance.
• Very slow when driving large off-chip or long bus line interconnects.

Wider Transistor

• Large drive current speeds up charging.
• The Catch: Wider devices have proportionally larger drain area and sidewall junction capacitances (\(C_{drain} \propto W\)), adding self-loading!

The Complete Load Capacitance Formulation

Kang & Leblebici decompose total inverter output capacitance into width-independent and width-dependent terms:

\[ C_{load} = \alpha_0 + \alpha_n W_n + \alpha_p W_p \tag{6.39} \]
  • \(\alpha_0\): Extrinsic load from wiring capacitance (\(C_{int}\)) and fanout gates (\(C_g\)). Independent of local transistor widths!
  • \(\alpha_n, \alpha_p\): Intrinsic junction capacitance parameters per unit channel width for nMOS and pMOS drain diffusions (\(C_{j0} D_{drain} + 2 C_{jsw}\)).

Aspect Ratio (\(R\)) and Width-Dependent Delay

Let the transistor aspect ratio be defined as:

\[ R \equiv \frac{W_p}{W_n} \tag{6.45} \]

Substituting \(W_p = R W_n\) into the propagation delay equations:

\[ \tau_{PHL} = \Gamma_n \left( \frac{\alpha_0 + (\alpha_n + R\alpha_p) W_n}{W_n} \right) = \Gamma_n \left( \frac{\alpha_0}{W_n} + (\alpha_n + R\alpha_p) \right) \tag{6.46a} \] \[ \tau_{PLH} = \Gamma_p \left( \frac{\alpha_0 + \left(\frac{\alpha_n}{R} + \alpha_p\right) W_p}{W_p} \right) = \Gamma_p \left( \frac{\alpha_0}{W_p} + \left(\frac{\alpha_n}{R} + \alpha_p\right) \right) \tag{6.46b} \]

where \(\Gamma_n\) and \(\Gamma_p\) are technology factors containing mobility, oxide capacitance, and threshold voltage:

\[ \Gamma_n = \left( \frac{L_n}{\mu_n C_{ox} (V_{DD} - V_{Tn})} \right) \left[ \frac{2\,V_{Tn}}{V_{DD} - V_{Tn}} + \ln \left( \frac{4(V_{DD} - V_{Tn})}{V_{DD}} - 1 \right) \right] \tag{6.47a} \]

The Inherent Asymptotic Speed Limit (\(\tau^{limit}\))

What happens if an engineer makes the transistors arbitrarily massive (\(W_n, W_p \rightarrow \infty\))?

\[ \lim_{W_n \rightarrow \infty} \left( \frac{\alpha_0}{W_n} \right) = 0 \] \[ \boxed{\tau_{PHL}^{limit} = \Gamma_n (\alpha_n + R\alpha_p)} \tag{6.48a} \] \[ \boxed{\tau_{PLH}^{limit} = \Gamma_p \left( \frac{\alpha_n}{R} + \alpha_p \right)} \tag{6.48b} \]
Exam Takeaway · The Physical Speed Limit

"The propagation delay times of a CMOS inverter cannot be reduced beyond these asymptotic limit values. The limit is dictated entirely by intrinsic technology parameters (doping densities, channel length, and minimum drain contact spacing \(D_{drain}\)) and is completely independent of external wiring (\(C_{int}\)) or fanout (\(C_g\)).

If extrinsic capacitance dominates, sizing up transistors yields huge delay reductions. If intrinsic self-capacitance dominates, sizing up yields diminishing returns while exploding active silicon area and dynamic power!"

5.3 Static Power & Nanometer Subthreshold Leakage Mechanics

Cholo bro, ebar Static Power. 😭 The good news is, this topic starts with one simple question:
A transistor is supposed to be OFF. So why is the chip still consuming massive power?

1. What is Static Power?

Imagine a CMOS circuit has finished switching. All clock signals and data inputs are completely static, holding steady logic values. Ideal textbooks claim CMOS consumes zero power in standby. But real transistors are leaky valves:

\[ \boxed{P_{static} = V_{DD} \cdot I_{static}} \]

2. Why CMOS Replaced nMOS Logic: The Contention Crisis

Old nMOS Logic (Contention Power)

In legacy nMOS gates, a depletion-mode pull-up resistor or transistor was permanently ON. When the nMOS pull-down turned ON to output a LOW, a continuous DC path formed directly from \(V_{DD}\) to ground. This contention current generated excessive heat and strictly limited integration density.

Complementary CMOS (Zero Contention)

Complementary CMOS pairs pMOS pull-up with nMOS pull-down. For any valid stable state, exactly one network conducts while the other is non-conducting. There is no steady-state contention path from \(V_{DD}\) to ground!

3. The Four Sources of Static Leakage

Leakage Mechanism Physical Cause Primary Scaling Factor
1. Subthreshold Leakage (\(I_{sub}\)) Diffusion current through the weak-inversion channel when \(V_{gs} < V_t\). Dominant source! Increases exponentially as \(V_t\) scales down.
2. Gate Dielectric Leakage (\(I_{gate}\)) Quantum mechanical electron tunneling directly through ultra-thin (\(< 2\text{ nm}\)) gate oxides. Driven by ultra-thin physical oxide thickness (\(t_{ox}\)); mitigated by high-\(\kappa\) metal gates (HKMG).
3. Junction Reverse Leakage (\(I_{junc}\)) Band-to-band tunneling (BTBT) and thermal carrier generation across reverse-biased Source/Drain-to-well pn junctions. Heavily doped shallow halo implants increase junction electric fields.
4. Contention Current (\(I_{cont}\)) Direct DC current flowing when pull-up and pull-down paths are accidentally ON together (e.g., bus fighting, ratioed logic). Zero in static CMOS; occurs only during dynamic switching or circuit faults.

4. Subthreshold Leakage: The "Almost Closed Tap"

Think of an ideal switch as a completely sealed water pipe: zero drops escape. A real nanoscale MOSFET is like a tap that is almost closed. Tiny microscopic drips escape continuously. When multiplied by 10 to 50 billion transistors on an advanced SoC, static leakage can consume over 30% of total active chip power!

The Master Subthreshold Leakage Equation

\[ \boxed{I_{sub} = I_{off} \cdot 10^{\frac{V_{gs} + \eta (V_{ds} - V_{DD}) - k_\gamma V_{sb}}{S}}} \tag{5.19} \]

Subthreshold Slope (\(S \approx 100\text{ mV/decade}\))

What does "one decade" mean? A factor of 10! (\(1\text{ nA} \rightarrow 10\text{ nA}\)).
With \(S \approx 100\text{ mV/decade}\), every single \(100\text{ mV}\) increase in \(V_{gs}\) multiplies OFF-state leakage current by tenfold!

Reference Leakage (\(I_{off}\))

Defined at \(V_{gs} = 0\) and \(V_{ds} = V_{DD}\):
• Low-\(V_t\) (LVT): \(\approx 100\text{ nA}/\mu\text{m}\) (Fast switching, high leakage).
• High-\(V_t\) (HVT): \(< 1\text{ nA}/\mu\text{m}\) (>100× lower standby leakage!).

DIBL Coefficient (\(\eta\))

Drain-Induced Barrier Lowering: In sub-100nm transistors, high drain voltage reaches across the short channel and lowers the potential barrier at the source, causing carriers to spill across even when the gate is turned OFF (\(\eta \approx 100\text{ mV/V}\)).

Body Effect Term (\(-k_\gamma V_{sb}\))

Applying a positive source-to-body voltage (\(V_{sb} > 0\), or negative substrate bias) raises the transistor threshold voltage via the body effect, exponentially suppressing subthreshold leakage!

4 Industry Techniques to Suppress Static Leakage

1. Power Gating (Sleep Transistors)

High-\(V_t\) header (pMOS) or footer (nMOS) transistors disconnect idle logic blocks from \(V_{DD}\) or GND during sleep mode, reducing leakage to near zero.

2. Multiple Threshold CMOS (MTCMOS)

Fast low-\(V_t\) transistors are used strictly on critical timing paths, while slow high-\(V_t\) transistors are used on 80% of non-critical paths to curb total chip leakage.

3. Adaptive Body Biasing (ABB)

Dynamically alters the substrate/well bias voltage: reverse body bias during standby to throttle leakage, forward body bias during turbo bursts to boost speed.

4. Stack Forcing

Replacing a single transistor with two series transistors. The intermediate node floats to a small positive voltage, creating a negative \(V_{gs}\) and positive \(V_{sb}\) on the top transistor, suppressing leakage exponentially.

📉 Interactive Subthreshold Leakage Decade Simulator

Test the exponential sensitivity of subthreshold leakage to gate bias, DIBL, and body effect (\(S = 100\text{ mV/decade}\)):

🎯 Quick Recall Check

Test your retention of inverter delay and static leakage mechanics:

1. What does the propagation delay \(\tau_{PHL}\) designate?
2. Why does inverter propagation delay approach an asymptotic lower limit (\(\tau_{limit}\)) as transistor width \(W \rightarrow \infty\)?
3. If a transistor has a subthreshold slope \(S = 100\text{ mV/decade}\), how much does subthreshold leakage current increase if \(V_{gs}\) increases by \(200\text{ mV}\)?

Dynamic power dissipation, comparative noise-margin widening, and the five foundational CMOS circuit families: Static CMOS, Ratioed/Pseudo-nMOS, Cascode Voltage Switch Logic (CVSL), Dynamic & Domino Logic, and Pass-Transistor / Transmission-Gate Logic. PEDAGOGICAL INTUITION · THE ENERGY PUMP ANALOGY (ACHA BRO)

"Acha bro! In static CMOS, DC current is virtually zero because one transistor is always OFF. So where does power go? Think of the load capacitance like a bucket of water. Every time the inverter output switches from 0 to 1, the pMOS takes energy from the VDD power supply and fills the bucket. When the output switches from 1 to 0, the nMOS dumps all that water straight into the ground! Dynamic power is the energy lost doing this bucket-fill and bucket-dump cycle millions or billions of times every second: \(P = \alpha C V_{DD}^2 f\)!"
